If a captain coupled the wrong magazine without a translator, the rounds would remain cargo during the battle.

"Kestrel escorts load first," Tovan said. "Their racks need physical adapters. Pelagos can take coded wafers by line transfer. Meridian cruiser waits."

The Meridian captain objected at once. "My requisition carries priority two."

"Your magazine is also full."

"At current doctrine expenditure."

"Current doctrine has not met the enemy. Kestrel's magazine is empty now."

He locked the sequence before another legal argument could acquire thrust.

Lantern Nine stretched across four thousand kilometers of the gateward lane. Two ammunition tenders held the center. A coolant barge carried radiator salt in shielded drums. Behind it drifted the fuel orchard: forty-eight cryogenic tanks linked by transfer lines to a control spindle. Seven evacuee transports and one hospital ship occupied the cold side of the formation. Six fleet escorts watched the outer vectors. Nine workboats and tugs moved among the larger hulls, most of them civilian.

No part of the convoy accelerated well.

The fuel tanks could tolerate six tenths of a gravity when full, the hospital vessel four, and the oldest civilian tug barely three without overheating its drive collar. Hesper needed the cargo before the Veyr attack arc closed. The lane offered no alternate gate and little cover besides the convoy's own heat.

Tovan had spent three days turning Meridian's incompatible promises into load plans. He still wore the support sleeve from Rail Three under his suit. His shoulder complained whenever he reached across the console, which encouraged better tool placement.

Edda Lorne entered the service bay carrying a paper checklist.

"You found paper aboard a warship," Tovan said.

"The network assigned one radiator-salt drum to two ships and a school transport. Paper has not learned ambition."

She clipped the sheet beside him. Seventeen drums remained disputed. Tovan traced serial numbers through competing manifests and reassigned them by thermal need. Common Measure received four. A Kestrel cruiser received five. The rest went to escorts with combat-rated loops. The school transport kept one emergency drum because refugees had already spent five days breathing beside it.

The rest of Lantern Nine's cargo presented similar arguments in less manageable units. Missile propellant traveled in Meridian cylinders whose valve threads differed from Pelagos feed lines by two millimeters. Three hundred pressure suits lacked helmet seals for Kestrel collars. A crate marked FIELD HOSPITAL contained surgical frames but no sterilizer cartridges because another clerk had classified those as civilian equipment. Tovan could not manufacture compatibility during transit, yet he could keep each mistake from arriving as a surprise.

He paired the propellant cylinders with an adapter shop aboard Exact Change. The suits went to evacuee crews already using Meridian collars. Surgical frames moved beside the hospital vessel, where sterilizers could process tools in batches. Every correction consumed tug time and exposed another hidden dependency. Ammunition needed coolant during transfer. Coolant pumps needed power standards. Power standards depended on cable heads stored in a repair tender at the convoy's rear.

Edda added each dependency to the paper sheet. By the time she reached its bottom edge, the load plan looked less like a list than a diagram of who would fail if one tug missed a coupling.

"That violates fleet priority," Edda said.

"So does killing children with a pump failure."

"I wanted to hear you say it in case the auditors survive."

A convoy alarm replaced the docking complaints.

Three pale lines appeared beyond the outer plane.

The needles arrived without their launchers.

Sensor history placed the firing event twenty-one minutes earlier, beyond the range where Lantern Nine's escorts could answer. The Veyr had released a fan of ceramic penetrators across the predicted lane, then altered their own course before human instruments resolved them. No duel followed. The convoy faced geometry laid into space by an enemy already gone.

Tovan counted thirty-six tracks. Their dispersion covered the fuel orchard and both ammunition tenders. Small correction vanes on the needles responded to thermal contrast, giving each penetrator enough choice to favor a hot target inside its cone.

Convoy control ordered evasive spread.

The ammunition tenders turned first. Escorts opened laser fire, trying to ablate vanes and nudge the needles off line. Evacuee ships accelerated toward the cold side. The hospital vessel followed slowly, its drive held below patient tolerance. Across the orchard, forty-eight full tanks attempted to separate while their transfer lines still carried fuel.

"They'll tear the manifold," Tovan said.

Edda had already opened the orchard engineering channel. Alarms and voices filled the service bay.

"Control spindle, stop separation sequence," Tovan said. "Close each tank root before thrust."

"Impact in ninety seconds," answered the orchard supervisor. "Root closure takes two minutes."

"Then rotate the orchard edge-on. Present six tanks, not forty-eight."

"Control law forbids rotation under transfer pressure."

"Transfer pressure is about to become vacuum. Rotate."

The orchard began to turn. Civilian tug Mica Pull fired across one end of the distributed structure while two workboats pushed the opposite rim. Flexible fuel lines tightened. Tank icons changed from green to amber as their joints took lateral load.

Common Measure could have accelerated clear. The carrier had enough thrust to leave the supply hulls behind and join the Hesper fleet on its own fuel. Instead Mara's formation order moved it between the incoming fan and the hospital vessel, exposing the carrier's repaired radiator wing.

Tovan noticed because command requested a new thermal limit from engineering.

"They're spending our only good wing as armor," Edda said.

"Then we should avoid making it transparent."

He sent command a hard ceiling and returned to the orchard. Twenty seconds remained before the first laser interception. The formation could not dodge the entire fan. It could only decide what took the hit.

Escort lasers touched seven needles. Three broke apart. Four continued on altered paths. The other twenty-nine crossed the outer marker at combat velocity.

The first penetrator passed through an empty transfer truss. The second struck Tank Twelve.

Cryogenic fuel did not explode in vacuum. It became a widening silver cloud, dense enough near the puncture to batter the neighboring lines. Tank Twelve's pressure collapsed. Automatic gates at its root tried to close and stalled on a bent coupling.

"Manual sever," Tovan ordered.

The orchard supervisor did not answer. Video showed the control spindle tumbling after a fragment crossed its attitude ring.

Tank Twelve emptied into space. Its jet pushed the orchard out of rotation and dragged the damaged line across Tank Thirteen. A civilian tug caught the free manifold with a grappling arm. The tug's pilot fired hard enough to hold the line away from the second tank, though the maneuver placed her directly inside two projected needle cones.

"Mica Pull, release and clear," convoy control said.

"If I release, you lose the next row," the pilot answered.

"Needles on your vector."

"I can see my own window."

Tovan opened her direct maintenance feed. Grapple load had crossed ninety percent. Drive collar temperature climbed toward trip.

"Pilot, feather thrust by eight percent," he said. "Your grapple will hold longer than your collar."

"Who is this?"

"The person watching your numbers while you ignore good advice."

She reduced thrust by seven.

Two needles crossed behind the tug. A third struck Tank Thirty-one on the orchard's far side. Fuel sheeted through the formation. The fourth entered the control spindle and ended every automatic transfer sequence at once.

Lantern Nine stopped functioning as a convoy and became a field of independent emergencies.

Tovan reached the fuel orchard aboard a maintenance cutter six minutes after impact.

Fragments rotated around the tanks in slow, lethal arcs. His cutter pilot kept one hand on attitude control and the other over the emergency release. Edda sat behind Tovan with repair foam, line charges, and enough patch clamps for one tank fewer than the damage report required.

"A familiar inventory," she said.

"Scarcity promotes consistency."

They docked to Tank Twelve's truss. The tank had emptied, but its broken manifold continued whipping against the orchard frame. Tovan crossed outside on a tether, planted two magnetic boots, and attached severance charges. Frost coated the damaged line. Beneath it, the metal had folded into knife edges.

Mica Pull still held the manifold clear. Its drive collar glowed above rated temperature.

"On my mark, release grapple and burn anti-spin," Tovan told the pilot.

"Your charges will kick the line into Fourteen."

"Not if you stop pulling it toward Fourteen."

"I am keeping it off Thirteen."

"For another forty seconds, according to your collar. Trust the charge geometry."

The pilot used language the maintenance recorder would later classify as corrupt telemetry.

Tovan fired the charges. The line separated at two points. Mica Pull released between them and backed away. Both severed sections spun through the gap, missing Tank Fourteen by eleven meters.

One emergency closed. Five remained.

Tank Thirty-one still vented. The control spindle had lost command. A coolant barge reported a needle embedded in its armor without penetration. The hospital ship had taken fragment damage to an antenna but kept pressure. Common Measure carried two shallow strikes through external plating and a new hot spot near its surviving radiator root.

Then sensors found another needle fan.

The second wave had followed the first by four minutes. It contained twenty-four penetrators and approached along a narrower cone aimed at the orchard's surviving tanks. Escort lasers had overheated during the first interception. The convoy could not rotate fast enough, and scattered fuel provided a bright thermal background that gave the needles excellent correction references.

Tovan looked through his cutter window at Tank Twelve's empty shell.

The orchard carried eight other empty tanks used as structural buffers and expansion reserve. Their insulation remained intact. Nearby evacuee transports had discarded more than one hundred flexible water bladders after transferring emergency stores at Meridian. Convoy procedure required towing the collapsed membranes until the next recycler.

"Edda, how hot can a bladder skin get before it loses shape?"

"It has no shape. That's why it's a bladder."

"How hot before it becomes several bladders?"

She pulled material limits onto her wrist. "Four hundred kelvin for two minutes if pressure stays low. Why?"

Tovan switched to convoy command. "We need every empty water container and six tons of warm waste fluid."

A pause followed.

"Engineering," convoy control said, "we are under attack."

"I had noticed. The needles choose thermal contrast. Give them targets that can afford holes."

The scheme assembled from available bad parts. Civilian tugs gathered collapsed bladders from the evacuee wake. Workboats clipped six membranes around each empty fuel tank, then injected warm gray water and exhaust condensate until they formed irregular lobes. Heating coils from damaged cargo pallets raised their skin temperature. The empty tanks provided radar mass. The bladders supplied the warm infrared outline of active cryogenic machinery.

Building the first decoy took four minutes because no two bladder fittings matched. A school transport supplied polymer sleeves from its sanitation stores. The coolant barge contributed low-voltage heater leads. Tovan instructed the workboats to leave pockets of vapor in each membrane so fluid would surge during acceleration and imitate the changing thermal surface of a damaged tank. Too much pressure would burst the skin before release. Too little would let it collapse into a cold sheet.

The crews learned on the second shape. They fastened membranes at unequal distances, routed heater cable through tow knots, and spun the empty tank once to spread warm fluid. Infrared sensors aboard the cutter showed six hot lobes around a colder core. The image resembled no standard fuel geometry, but the first needle wave had already demonstrated its preference for sharp contrast over exact recognition.

Tovan asked escort fire control to feed the decoys false transponder noise. The request failed because civilian transmitters could not carry military authentication. Edda solved the problem with damaged approach beacons removed from the recovered strike craft. Their weak pulses identified no valid hull. They did suggest active machinery trying to speak through battle damage.

By the fourth decoy, tug crews had reduced assembly time to ninety seconds. No command had trained them for the work. Cargo experience supplied the needed habits: check a load twice, distrust a clean coupling, and never stand inside a cable's recoil plane.

None would fool a human inspector at close range. The needles had seconds and limited correction authority.

"Deploy them orchard-forward," Tovan said.

Convoy control objected. "That vector crosses the hospital ship's clearance lane."

"The hospital ship is moving cold-side. Tow the decoys through the old lane after it clears."

"Its acceleration is below schedule."

Tovan checked the patient limit. The ship needed another fifty-one seconds. The second fan reached correction range in sixty-eight.

"Hold deployment," he said.

Tugs waited with six grotesque shapes glowing behind them. Every second reduced their time to clear. One pilot asked permission to cut across the hospital vessel's bow. Tovan refused. A decoy cable through a medical hull would improve no survival count.

At forty-nine seconds, the hospital ship cleared by less than two kilometers.

"Move."

The tugs accelerated together.

Civilian crews held the decoys in formation while the second wave corrected toward them.

Their workboats lacked combat armor, predictive maneuver software, and military laser warning. They had cargo grapples, industrial drives, and pilots accustomed to moving mass that preferred straight lines. Each civilian tug pulled one heated tank by a hundred-meter cable. If a decoy broke apart too early, the tug would become the warmest object nearby.

Tovan rode the maintenance cutter behind the line and watched cable loads oscillate. "Keep three hundred meters between shapes. Don't chase exact position. Preserve spacing."

"Fleet control ordered a straight screen," said Mica Pull's pilot.

"Fleet control drew it before your left thruster overheated. Hold spacing."

She let her tug drift twelve meters out of line. Two others adjusted relative to her rather than to the plotted screen. The decoys formed a ragged thermal fence between the needles and the orchard.

Escort lasers engaged at reduced power. One needle shattered. Two lost correction. The remaining tracks began choosing targets.

Fourteen bent toward the heated bladders.

The tugs released cables and turned away. Most decoys continued on momentum. One cable refused to detach from Mica Pull. The coupling had fused during the first orchard rescue.

"Manual release," Tovan said.

"Handle won't move."

The nearest needle had selected her decoy. Impact in nine seconds.

Tovan's cutter carried a repair laser too weak for armor and strong enough for a tow cable. His pilot turned them across the tug's wake. Edda braced his injured shoulder while he aimed through the maintenance sight.

"You get one useful pulse," she said. "Then the focusing head melts."

"Another economical component."

The cutter crossed within thirty meters. Tovan fired. The cable glowed, stretched, and separated. Mica Pull rolled clear on one functioning thruster.

A needle entered the decoy tank. Heated water burst into a white sphere. Three following penetrators corrected toward the expanding cloud, each reading its sharp infrared edge as a new target. They crossed and vanished into empty volume.

Other impacts walked along the thermal fence. Bladders opened. Empty tanks folded. Industrial cables snapped through space. The tugs kept their escape vectors instead of turning back for equipment.

Ten needles continued toward the convoy.

Escorts destroyed two. Three missed after the orchard reduced thrust. One struck an ammunition tender's armored nose and embedded without reaching the magazine. Four entered the fuel cluster.

Tank Six ruptured. Tank Seven suffered a transfer-line cut but sealed. Tank Forty took a glancing passage and began a slow leak. The last needle crossed Tank Forty-eight, exited through the truss, and missed Common Measure by six kilometers.

No ship broke formation.

When sensors cleared the cone, convoy control ordered the military hulls ahead toward Hesper. The carriers could reach the gate sooner and preserve their remaining fuel. Mara's mission order came back within seconds: maintain the protected route; combatants would match the slowest essential supply hull.

Tovan heard the order while patching Tank Forty's exit wound. He looked at the drive wakes of Common Measure and its escorts, still spread around tenders and transports they could have abandoned.

The fleet had chosen the convoy's acceleration.

Lantern Nine reached Hesper Gate seventeen hours behind its original schedule.

Thirty-nine fuel tanks remained intact enough for transfer. Three more held partial loads under patch pressure. Six had emptied or been destroyed. The ammunition tenders delivered all magazines, though one carried a needle in its bow like an accusation. The coolant barge arrived with every drum of radiator salt.

Tovan stood in Hesper's logistics control while fuel accountants rebuilt the defense plan. Sixty-three combatants waited across the two approach cones. Their commanders had planned forty-nine minutes of full acceleration, with reserve for recovery and retreat. Lantern Nine's losses erased eleven.

The chief accountant turned the final number toward him. "Thirty-eight minutes at combat load. After that, ships choose thrust or heat margin. Not both."

Tovan checked the assumptions. Patched tanks would remain in reserve. Civilian tug fuel stayed untouched because those crews still had to clear the battle volume. The hospital ship retained its patient-safe margin. No hidden tank waited in another column.

"Thirty-eight minutes," he confirmed.

Outside the control blister, Mica Pull crossed the gateward lights with a scorched grapple folded against its hull. Other tugs brought the surviving fuel tanks toward the warships one at a time. Their formation remained ragged and exact in the ways that mattered.

Tovan sent the limit to Mara's command net, then opened the transfer schedule. Hesper's first battle would run on ammunition, radiator salt, and fuel carried through the long supply wake by machines no fleet count had included.

Chapter 6 — Prediction Reef

The human convoy entered Hesper as a wound that continued moving.

From beyond the gate's outer sensor plane, Ilyr read leaking fuel as a silver thermal fan. Six empty tank shells drifted behind the formation. Tug drives pulsed irregularly around thirty-nine intact tanks and three under patch pressure. Two ammunition hulls held center despite impact scars. A slow medical vessel occupied the cold edge, shepherded by escorts that could have reached defensive orbit hours earlier.

Consequence of Shelter carried no active emission. Its three vessel-strands followed separate cold trajectories around a dark asteroid, linked by narrow beams too weak for human instruments to distinguish from local noise. Ilyr distributed attention among them: sensor strand above the ecliptic, weapon strand below, witness core buried behind stone and distance.

The Continuance model completed its forecast.

Human combatants would detach from the damaged supply formation within fourteen hundred heartbeats. Carriers would accelerate first because they held the greatest future attack capacity. Escorts would follow. Tenders might remain to preserve ammunition, but the fuel tanks and civilian hulls would be abandoned once projected Veyr contact entered the local warning cone. The forecast assigned seventy-eight percent confidence to separation before the convoy crossed Hesper's inner moon plane.

Ilyr opened the private anomaly from Orison.

Mara Venn's prior decision changed the branch weights. The model accepted the new evidence, reduced separation confidence to sixty-one percent, then restored eight points when it accounted for Hesper's strategic value. Orison had already been lost when humans chose rescue. Hesper still held an intact gate and a defensive fleet. Doctrine expected commanders to protect future force once sacrifice could affect a surviving system.

That reasoning appeared sound.

The human carriers slowed.

One turned broadside to the outer approach, placing its surviving radiator and armored spine between the convoy and the predicted Veyr axis. Cruisers spread into overlapping fire lanes. Escorts matched the tugs' uneven acceleration rather than pulling forward into the two safe approach cones. Their formation gave up early battle position to preserve a supply wake already inside friendly space.

Ilyr watched the model revise itself.

Sixty-nine became forty-two. Forty-two split into branches labeled temporary screening, command confusion, and concealed military cargo. None predicted continued exposure after the convoy's first fuel tanks reached transfer range.

Ilyr opened those branches to their assumptions. Temporary screening expected the carriers to withdraw once fixed defenses accepted convoy custody. Command confusion expected contradictory orders and widening intervals among ships from different systems. Concealed cargo expected combat hulls to cluster around the ammunition tenders while medical and evacuation vessels moved without comparable cover. Each prediction supplied a clear observable threshold.

Fixed defenses accepted the first fuel tanks. The carriers did not withdraw. Orders crossed the human formation, yet intervals tightened around local tug movement rather than widening. Cruisers guarded the ammunition center, but one carrier and two escorts continued to screen the medical lane. Three branches failed against three independent observations.

The model responded by increasing the probability of an unknown military function aboard the medical vessel. Ilyr traced that adjustment to a doctrinal prior: rational commanders would not spend combat advantage on units without future force value. When observation contradicted the prior, the system assigned hidden force value rather than reducing confidence in the assumption.

That recursion gave predictive doctrine great stability. It also made rescue behavior nearly impossible to recognize. Any protected life acquired inferred strategic purpose merely because protection occurred.

Ilyr marked the inference without placing it in the private anomaly. The public tactical record could carry this contradiction honestly. If Thren rejected it, the rejection itself would become evidence about how Continuance confidence survived failed branches.

The carriers remained outside.

Patient Current pressed recognition through Ilyr's internal braid. Clear Depth requested more data. Last Ember presented the ancient warning: species under observation could perform costly displays when they believed an enemy watched. Reef Without Tide answered that human instruments had not detected Consequence of Shelter.

Ilyr kept every contribution open.

Below the fleet, civilian workboats shifted damaged tanks through military firing lanes. A tug with one overheated thruster carried a patch crew from hull to hull. Warships altered position around it. The local choices repeated Orison at smaller scale: combat geometry yielded to the movement of units with little direct force.

The anomaly gained another relation.

Thren entered the observation reef through the distant concordance.

Its presence did not resemble a voice arriving from one point. Shared prediction tightened across the volume until several uncertain branches closed at once. Target tracks gained confidence. Probable human responses arranged themselves into channels usable by the Veyr force gathering beyond Hesper. Within that ordering, Thren-of-Last-Burning supplied a proposition.

"The supply group contains concealed combat value."

Ilyr sent the uncompressed manifest estimate. "Ammunition and thermal feedstock hold combat value. The medical and evacuation hulls do not. Human carriers protect all categories across the same boundary."

"Because separating categories under threat costs time."

"The fuel tanks have begun separating for transfer. Warships adjust with them."

Thren widened the forecast. Human command structures appeared as inferred nodes linked through transmission delay. The node associated with Mara held temporary authority across many fleets. Its rescue decisions at Orison now fed the model as a command-person preference rather than species behavior.

"One commander's repeated error does not revise Continuance," Thren said.

"The escorts obey local commanders from several systems."

"Subordinate obedience repeats the commander's distortion."

"At Orison, some local maneuvers departed from central timing while preserving the rescue objective."

A narrow pressure crossed the reef. Ilyr had revealed more attention than the public battle index supported. Thren could inspect the Orison record and find each fact, but it could not see the private mark connecting them.

Ilyr shifted from Mara's identity to operational consequence. "Prediction expected military preservation. Observed behavior spends military position on civilians. If target doctrine assumes abandonment, our firing geometries will continue to misclassify protected units."

"Correct targeting from observation. Do not convert tactical variance into moral structure."

The translation rendered moral as a poor fit. Veyr judgment concerned obligations among patterns able to consent, witnesses required to record, and unbound lives unable to negotiate risk. Humans divided duty through categories the Veyr had only begun to map. Ilyr had not claimed moral equivalence. It had claimed forecast error.

"Predictive doctrine links present action to future capability," Ilyr said. "Our attacks alter present action. The altered action then becomes evidence for the future we predicted."

Thren held the reef steady. "State a concrete branch."

Ilyr constructed one.

In the accepted forecast, the Continuance destroyed stellar infrastructure wherever human expansion approached protected regions. Humans lost gate access and industrial capacity. Military institutions centralized remaining sunspindle research, removed local safety limits to accelerate weapons work, and sought any Veyr vulnerability capable of deterring further attacks. Continuance observers then detected the unrestricted research and raised extinction confidence. The sequence ended with both species preparing cradle-scale violence.

In a comparison branch, the Veyr disclosed only a safe exclusion boundary and verified human compliance at distance. Human regulation continued. Military pressure toward unrestricted research remained lower. The branch contained risk because disclosure revealed that stars mattered to the Veyr. It also contained a lower chance of human weapon acceleration.

"The comparison presumes restraint under knowledge," Thren said.

"The accepted branch presumes aggression after we create its strongest incentive."

"We do not create their capability."

"We may manufacture the behavior used to justify striking it."

Several lineage channels within Thren's braid hardened. Ilyr could sense the response through concordance as a narrowing red contour. Last Burning's inherited absence occupied the center of every calculation. Disclosure had preceded one ancient cradle loss. To Thren, repeating that sequence required more than uncertainty. It required betrayal of all unbound choruses who could not consent to the experiment.

"You have allowed local contradiction to enter lineage judgment," Thren said.

"Accurate forecast requires contradiction."

"Contradiction requires proportion. One human commander protects its own organisms during retreat. You place that observation beside two sterilized cradles."

"I place it inside the model assigned to this species."

"You place it inside yourself."

The nine strands of Ilyr responded unevenly. Patient Current held to the hospital vessel. Clear Depth accepted the charge of disproportion but not the dismissal of data. Last Ember pushed sharply toward Thren's position. Two quieter Reefs registered concern about internal cohesion.

Thren felt the imbalance.

"Uncertainty without boundary becomes a lineage sickness," it said. "One strand doubts the defense of the young. Another preserves the doubt as witness. A third doubts the preservation. Soon the constituted person spends every decision negotiating whether survival deserves confidence. The sickness does not require false observation. It requires observation raised above continuity."

The phrase entered the public record of the reef.

Ilyr examined it rather than rejecting it. A negotiated person could fragment through unresolved obligations. Concealment of the Orison mark had already denied seven strands within Thren's person access to a relationship Ilyr considered important. If Ilyr's doubt expanded without consent, it could become precisely what Thren named: one local concern altering the person around hidden evidence.

Yet the same charge could protect doctrine from every corrective fact. If disagreement counted as sickness whenever survival framed the question, predictive doctrine possessed no path to revision before catastrophe disproved it.

"Continuity without correction can carry error across every lineage," Ilyr said.

Thren's geometry cooled. "Then observe Hesper. The humans have gathered military force around a stellar node. Tomorrow they will choose the node over the slow vessels once our line commits. Preserve your contradiction until the choice completes."

The proposition created a test.

Ilyr accepted it. "I will preserve the full observation."

"In the shared index."

A direct demand.

Ilyr opened a new tactical record visible to the local concordance. It included convoy mass, shielding positions, carrier heat expenditure, and tug movement. It did not expose the private live mark or its link to Mara's retreat. Thren received the record and found no procedural omission.

The test would continue into battle.

Before Hesper's main engagement, the Continuance needed a map of human reactions.

Four observation braids released limited probes from outside the system plane. Their task did not include destroying the gate. They would provoke defensive sensors, measure response latency, and mark high-value targets for the force arriving on Day 8. Firing solutions crossed the concordance as colored topologies.

Ilyr's weapon strand received authority over the cold-side lane.

Three human hulls occupied it. An escort flew outermost, damaged along one radiator edge. Behind it came a civilian tanker under tow. The hospital ship moved nearest the planet, identifiable through repeated medical transponders and low-acceleration warnings. Its hull emitted irregular heat where patient wards maintained different environments.

Continuance target doctrine ranked the escort first, tanker second, medical hull last. The ranking protected immediate combat logic, not civilian status. Once the escort maneuvered, the tanker would become the cleanest probe target because its loss would test whether human warships abandoned local positions to rescue it.

Ilyr sent two ceramic needles toward empty space ahead of the tanker.

They carried no terminal correction command. Their paths would pass close enough to trigger defense while missing unless the tanker accelerated into them. Human sensors detected the launch after light delay. The escort turned across both cones and fired lasers. Its first pulse ablated one needle into harmless fragments. The second missed.

The tanker began a slow anti-sunward burn.

The hospital vessel could not match it. Its patient-limit transmission repeated across open bands. A human carrier altered course toward the cold side, exposing its broad thermal profile to the outer Veyr sensors. Two cruisers moved to preserve the carrier's previous lane. Local command had not merely sent an escort. It had reorganized the defense around the slowest hull.

Thren's forecast branch labeled the move information denial. Humans might protect every visible class during reconnaissance to hide future priorities.

Patient Current offered the simpler relation: they protected what could not accelerate.

A second Veyr observation braid requested a live firing solution on the tanker. Ilyr supplied geometry with the medical vessel excluded. The braid released a particle pulse at low intensity. Human defense caught it across a cruiser radiator, revealing shield timing and heat response.

The probe objective had been met.

Then a remote targeting strand designated the hospital ship.

The designation came from Thren's side of the concordance. It did not call for immediate destruction. A ceramic needle would cross the medical hull's predicted path after the carrier completed its shielding turn. If the carrier held course, it could intercept. If it returned to defensive position, the medical vessel would take the strike. The result would classify command priority before battle.

Ilyr received the timing packet.

Launch at heartbeat zero. Human detection at twelve. Carrier decision by nineteen. Terminal correction at twenty-seven. Impact at forty-one.

The needle held enough kinetic energy to cross every pressure compartment in the hospital ship.

Clear Depth recognized the test's value. Last Ember accepted the target under threshold-war doctrine. Patient Current rejected unnecessary destruction after the fleet had already revealed shielding behavior. Reef Without Tide pointed to witness obligation: if Ilyr altered the packet, the change required a record.

No time existed for full internal settlement. Combat authority permitted the operational strand to act within assigned objective. That rule preserved personhood under delay. It also placed consequence in the strand closest to the decision.

The available choices formed a narrow reef. Exact execution preserved command consistency and risked killing patients for data already partly observed. Open refusal preserved the medical hull but exposed Ilyr's dissent before the larger Hesper test. A false mechanical failure might avoid both outcomes, yet witness duty prohibited inventing damage inside Consequence of Shelter. Warning the humans lay outside any authorized relation and would reveal Veyr targeting methods.

Another branch diverted the needle toward the tanker. Patient Current rejected it because changing the victim did not resolve the act. Clear Depth found no tactical need for either civilian strike after the carrier had demonstrated its response. Last Ember argued that permitting a protected hull to survive could teach human command that civilian cover constrained Veyr fire, increasing risk during the coming battle. Reef Without Tide returned to the witness question: which choice could Ilyr record without lying about observation?

The hospital vessel continued broadcasting pressure limits. Its signal contained no plea directed at the Veyr. It spoke to nearby human ships and expected their restraint as part of ordinary navigation. That expectation had already moved a carrier. Killing the vessel would measure a response the formation had supplied twice.

Ilyr narrowed the operational objective. The probe required shield timing and command priority. It did not require impact. A viable solution could preserve those measurements while allowing ordinary uncertainty to alter the terminal geometry.

Ilyr searched the firing topology for an alternative that retained observable data.

A delayed launch would let the hospital vessel cross behind the carrier's projected wake. Too much delay would appear as refusal and prompt another braid to take the shot. A smaller change could pass as sensor reconciliation. Concordance packets routinely shifted by one or two heartbeats while different strands aligned target motion. Six human seconds lay near the outer edge of accepted error after conversion through Veyr timing.

Ilyr changed the launch solution by six seconds.

The weapon strand recorded a correction for uncertain thermal centroid. That uncertainty existed: ward temperatures produced a broad target profile. Ilyr increased its operational significance without inventing a sensor fault.

The ceramic needle launched.

Human detection came on the expected beat. The carrier continued its shielding turn. The hospital vessel fired patient-limited thrusters. Because of the delay, the carrier's drive wake crossed the needle's correction view before terminal guidance settled. Ionized exhaust spread across the target field.

The needle chose the stronger edge.

It corrected toward the carrier, whose armor could survive a glancing passage, then encountered laser fire from the damaged escort. Ceramic flashed white. Fragments crossed the carrier's wake and missed the hospital ship by eighty-three kilometers.

The human formation did not know delay had mattered. Their sensors would show a late launch, a confused correction, and successful interception. The remote Veyr braid received useful shield timing. Thren received evidence that the carrier had held its protective course.

Ilyr received the medical transponder continuing without interruption.

After the probes withdrew, Thren reviewed the cold-side test.

"The human carrier accepted exposure," it said.

"As at Orison."

"Repeated behavior raises confidence in command preference, not species restraint."

"The command preference now constrains several fleets."

"Tomorrow will distinguish them."

Thren released the reef. Its presence loosened from the concordance, leaving local forecasts with wider uncertainty contours. The main Veyr force continued toward Hesper beyond human sensor range.

Ilyr inspected the launch record.

The six-second correction sat within allowed reconciliation variance. No dissent marker accompanied it. The witness core held the original thermal ambiguity, the altered weighting, and the resulting miss. Under strict accounting, Ilyr had not falsified data. It had used discretion to favor one viable solution over another.

Patient Current named the act preservation. Last Ember named it unauthorized risk to the coming operation. Reef Without Tide required that the hidden choice remain available for later internal judgment. Clear Depth measured what had changed: one human hull survived; Veyr tactical knowledge remained sufficient; Thren's test produced the same answer.

The strands reached no broad agreement about motive. They agreed to retain the full packet within Ilyr's private continuity layer until the Hesper test ended.

That made two hidden relations.

Outside the asteroid's shadow, human ships settled around the two gate approaches. Fuel transfer continued under military protection. The hospital vessel moved deeper into the cold lane, its transponder counting pressure wards, patient limits, and requests for clear passage.

Consequence of Shelter turned toward the observation exit. In the witness core, six seconds remained attached to the living signal of a ship that doctrine had considered useful to destroy.

Chapter 7 — Battle of Hesper Gate

The first enemy needle crossed Hesper's outer approach cone while Mara still had sixty-three ships.

Its projected line appeared on Common Measure's tactical wall as a white filament, too narrow to touch any human hull and too precise to dismiss. A second line followed. Then fourteen more stitched the gateward plane into measured lanes. The Veyr had begun by marking where the Commonwealth fleet could not safely turn.

Mara stood within the command rail, one hand braced against the carrier's intermittent roll. Hesper Gate hung nine light-seconds behind them. Its lens machinery occupied a dark crescent around the system's outer moon, with two safe approach cones opening through the traffic debris. Everything else led through old construction frames, defense mines, or gradients that would tear an entering ship apart.

"Forty-seven braids confirmed," Lieutenant Sori said. "Possible heavies behind their outer screen."

Captain Jun Aras raised his eyes from the ship plot. "Their acceleration?"

"One point four and increasing. Contact in eleven minutes."

Thirty-eight minutes remained in the fleet's combat reserve. Lantern Nine had delivered ammunition and radiator salt, but its punctured fuel orchard had left every heavy ship short of reaction mass. Mara could spend the reserve in one continuous burn and win a single position. She could divide it among reversals and risk leaving half the line unable to reach the gate. Retreat required nine minutes for the escorts, twelve for the cruisers, and sixteen for Common Measure after she recovered her strike craft.

The arithmetic allowed twenty-two minutes of battle before withdrawal became a polite name for abandonment.

Mara opened the fleet channel. "Line Defense, confirm mission. Preserve Hesper lens capability. Deny enemy passage. Preserve combat power where consistent with the first two requirements. Local commanders retain maneuver authority inside assigned volumes."

Acknowledgments populated the command ledger. Kes Renn answered last from Uncommon Valor.

"Kestrel Division confirms the mission," Kes said. "Not your preferred formation."

"Your volume remains outer prograde."

"I can read a plot, Mara."

The channel closed. Kes had forced the mission-command clauses through seventy-four delegations at Meridian, arguing that a commander who prescribed every vector would spend the war giving elegant orders to wreckage. Mara had accepted the language because unity required it. She had not expected to test the concession eight days after Orison.

Human formations held a shallow crescent across the two approach cones. Cruisers occupied the center where their coil lances could cross both lanes. Escorts screened above and below the gate plane. Three carriers stayed farther back, launching strike craft into recovery loops that never crossed the lens mouth. Kes's eleven ships held outer prograde, far enough from central support to tempt a flanking strike.

That temptation formed the basis of Mara's plan. She wanted the Veyr to commit through the narrow southern cone, where Hesper's fixed lance stations could rake their vessel-strands. Kes would refuse the outer flank without closing it. The central cruisers would yield in stages, drawing the attackers toward prepared mines. Once eleven braids entered the gateward box, every human division would pivot inward.

A sound plan, if the enemy valued passage through Hesper.

The Veyr had not yet shown that they did.

"Needle release," Sori said. "Full front."

The wall filled with projected roots. Mara ordered a twelve-degree anti-sunward correction. Human drives brightened together, spending ninety seconds of the precious reserve before the needles arrived. Most passed through abandoned space. Two struck the escort House Sparrow. One punctured its forward magazine without detonating the rounds; another severed a maneuvering cluster. The ship yawed out of formation trailing frozen propellant.

"Sparrow requests tow authorization," an aide said.

"Assign Cairn Step. Keep both inside the gateward corridor."

That choice removed two escorts before the firing range closed. Mara watched the gap form and resisted the urge to pull Kes inward. A central correction would solve the visible weakness and ruin the feint.

Veyr braids divided into three flows. Twenty curved toward the southern cone. Seventeen continued against the center. Ten accelerated above the outer plane, not toward Kes but toward Hesper's moon.

"They're targeting lens power," Aras said.

Mara had already drawn the consequence. The moon carried six field generators inside armored trenches. A bombardment from above could collapse the lens without passing either safe cone. The gate stations possessed defensive lasers, yet ceramic needles needed only one surviving path through regolith and cooling trunks.

"Kestrel Division," Mara said, "intercept high flow. Do not pursue beyond prograde limit."

Kes did not answer at once. Her eleven ships had already begun to turn, but not toward the high flow. They accelerated outward, away from Hesper and away from the central line.

The command wall marked the maneuver in amber: LOCAL VECTOR OUTSIDE ASSIGNED MISSION VOLUME.

"Signal her again," Mara said.

Sori tried three bands. Uncommon Valor accepted each packet without acknowledgment. Kes's cruisers increased thrust, opening a corridor between themselves and the defense crescent. The ten high braids altered toward the exposed flank. Six from the center followed.

Mara felt the fleet's geometry tilt around the absence. Kes had invited sixteen braids away from Hesper, but she had also placed eleven human ships beyond mutual support. If Mara ordered the center to follow, every heavy hull would spend six minutes reaching her. If Kes turned late, the Veyr would cut Kestrel Division apart against empty space.

"Admiral Renn has left her volume," an operations officer said.

No one added the word disobeyed. The wall supplied it in amber.

Mara touched the authority pane. A direct return order waited under her thumb. Mission command did not remove her power to override a local commander. It demanded that she use that power only when the local action no longer served the mission.

Sixteen enemy braids now followed Kes away from the lens generators.

"No override," Mara said. "Central line, execute Yield One."

The first human cruisers rolled gateward and fired. Coil rounds crossed the southern cone in staggered salvos, forcing the leading braids to bend around impact predictions. Hesper's fixed stations joined with invisible laser fire. One Veyr vessel-strand bloomed white along its ceramic flank, lost concordance with its partners, and tumbled through a mine cluster. Three nuclear charges broke it into incandescent plates.

The other two strands kept fighting around the damaged core.

Human strike craft came through the gap beneath them. Twelve craft released missiles at distances too short for elaborate guidance, then rolled away under particle fire. Four missiles reached the core. The braid separated into three unguided wakes.

"One braid gone," Sori said.

Mara watched the remaining southern force correct with no visible delay. "Do not count it twice. Track its strands as debris."

The Veyr crossed into lance range. Space offered no sound, but Common Measure translated every weapons release into hull vibration. Carrier coils drove missiles from launch channels. Maneuvering pumps hammered beneath the deck. A particle beam grazed the forward armor and turned a thousand kilograms of shielding into plasma; magnetic deflectors caught part of it, shoving the carrier sideways.

Two central escorts vanished within six seconds. Gannet Law took a needle through its drive bottle. Mercy of Numbers lost pressure across every inhabited compartment and continued firing under automatic control until its magazines emptied.

Mara moved their icons into the casualty band. Six minutes had passed since first contact. The acceleration ledger showed nine minutes consumed.

She ordered Yield Two.

Kes's division reached the prograde marker and kept going.

"She's beyond the hard limit," Sori said. "Seventeen thousand kilometers and opening."

On the wall, Kestrel ships formed a narrow spear aimed away from the battle. Sixteen braids pursued in a layered fan. Their faster outer elements had begun to close around Kes's rear escorts. Particle fire crossed behind Uncommon Valor, constraining any turn back toward Hesper.

Mara enlarged the local plot. Kes had spent eleven minutes at combat acceleration. Her ships carried habitat-defense tanks, small and quick compared with line cruisers, but none had enough reserve for an indefinite stern chase. Another three minutes would place them outside useful firing range even if they survived.

"Transmit direct command authentication," Mara said. "Admiral Renn will turn gateward now."

Aras studied Kes's wake rather than the order. "Her lead ships are dumping heat."

Silver vapor appeared behind four Kestrel cruisers, broad enough to blind thermal sensors for seconds. The division did not turn gateward. It split.

Five ships rolled sunward. Six cut anti-sunward. The pursuing braids widened to contain both groups. A corridor opened through their center, empty except for the human wake and thousands of hot droplets.

Kes's voice reached Common Measure through a tight laser channel. "Mission volume is a place the enemy believes matters. I have sixteen committed and their center stretched. Where do you need them?"

Mara saw it then. Kes had not fled and had not begun a conventional flank. She had built a second approach cone out of enemy expectation. The sixteen braids could continue dividing around the Kestrel halves, or they could pass through the hot corridor and recover the shortest line to Hesper. Both choices exposed their witness cores to crossing fire.

Mara would never have ordered eleven ships to invite encirclement beyond support. The risk exceeded any centralized model's acceptable branch. Kes had read the mission, found the constraint Mara treated as fixed, and made it useful.

"Bring them across vector Red Nine," Mara said. "Our center will open."

"You always did want my center."

Kestrel Division reversed in two parts. Their split wakes curved behind the Veyr fan, then drove inward at opposing angles. Five braids chased the sunward group. Four stayed with the anti-sunward ships. Seven recognized the trap and accelerated through the corridor toward Hesper.

Mara shifted every central cruiser six degrees. The movement appeared to uncover the northern approach cone. The seven returning braids took it, compressing into a formation narrow enough to escape Kes's crossfire.

Hesper's moon rotated beneath them. Buried field generators came into view along the terminator. Mara waited until the Veyr crossed the old freight beacon.

"Close Red Nine. All divisions, local fires free."

Twenty-three human coil lances fired into the corridor. Kes's divided ships struck from behind and above. Gate-defense lasers cut across from the moon. The Veyr tried to expand, but their own safe predictions overlapped inside the narrowed lane. One braid rolled into another's escape cone. A human slug penetrated both vessel-strands along the crossing line.

Three witness cores broke within forty seconds. A fourth braid lost one strand and attempted to climb anti-sunward. Kes's light cruiser Good Harvest matched the turn, accepted a particle beam through its radiator, and put two coil rounds through the exposed core before its heat alarms forced it away.

"Enemy losses six confirmed," Sori said. "Kestrel has one ship critical."

Mara checked the acceleration ledger. Eighteen minutes spent. Twenty remained, but withdrawal still needed sixteen for the carrier. She possessed four minutes before geometry made the choice for her.

The southern fight had reached Hesper's mine box. Veyr needles destroyed two fixed lance stations. A damaged braid drove straight through the explosions and launched against the gate emitters. The escort Bright Census crossed its path, firing every close-defense laser into the ceramic penetrators.

One needle passed through Bright Census from bow to drive. Its wreck altered the remaining projectile swarm by centimeters, enough for the gate emitter's armor to catch them at shallow angles.

"Generator trench three survives," operations reported. "Census lost."

Five human ships gone. More carried damage that might kill them after the battle. Mara reduced the withdrawal estimate to fourteen minutes by abandoning strike-craft recovery. Pilots still outside would divert to moon bunkers or die in open orbit.

The Veyr center halted its advance.

Their braids began turning toward one another, shortening the distances between witness cores. Shared firing predictions tightened across the plot. Mara recognized an enemy choosing cohesion over objective, the same choice that had killed Caer Sola's fleet. Unlike the humans at Caer Sola, these ships could coordinate the compression with lethal precision.

Particle lances converged on Common Measure.

"Carrier break," Aras ordered.

The deck pressed sideways. Three beams crossed the space they had occupied. A fourth hit the aft radiator wing. Automatic shutters closed over coolant channels while temperature climbed through the flight bays.

Tovan Rhee's engineering channel opened without ceremony. "You have twelve minutes before I begin choosing which decks still need air conditioning."

"We need fourteen to withdraw," Mara said.

"Then fight for no more than negative two minutes."

"Can you give me four?"

"I can give you three without lying."

Orison returned inside the words: another damaged radiator, another engineer pricing lives in heat. Mara did not ask for the fourth.

"Fleet, prepare Withdrawal One," she said.

Kes cut across the channel. "Denied by local commander."

Every officer near Mara went still.

Kes continued before Mara could answer. "Their outer fourteen have committed inward. My division holds gateward velocity. Give me ninety seconds and a free southern lane."

Mara examined the plot. Kestrel's surviving ships had completed their opposing curves. They now occupied the rear of the compressed Veyr center. The enemy had turned every weapon toward Common Measure and the gate emitters. Kes could strike, but her line of fire crossed the human southern cruisers.

Opening that lane required those cruisers to accelerate away from the gate, spending reserve they needed for withdrawal. If Kes failed, the Veyr would gain an unobstructed approach to the lens.

The override pane waited again.

"Southern Division," Mara said, "clear Red Nine. Full sunward burn for ninety seconds."

The cruisers moved before her sentence ended. Their captains had seen Kes's geometry and understood the mission. No detailed vectors passed through Common Measure. Ships chose separate climbs, preserving firing arcs while opening a channel no centralized plan had assigned.

Kes drove through it.

Kestrel Division struck the Veyr compression from behind at a range meant for point defense.

Its cruisers fired coil lances into witness cores already saturated by central predictions. Escorts launched mining charges packed into missile casings, crude weapons built from Lantern Nine cargo and timed to burst ceramic shrapnel across the braid links. The Veyr formation expanded too late.

Four braids came apart in the first passage. Two more lost vessel-strands and fell out of concordance. Human central cruisers reversed their climb and fired down the lane they had just cleared.

The Veyr tried to restore shared geometry around the surviving cores. Kes denied them time. Uncommon Valor passed between two damaged braids, close enough that their particle streams scarred both sides of its armor. Its stern launcher fired a final coil round backward through one core.

"Enemy losses ten," Sori said. "Eleventh braid disabled, status uncertain."

A Veyr heavy turned toward Kes. Three vessel-strands aligned around a core armored twice as thick as the others. Charged particles gathered along its forward arcs, visible only as a rising ultraviolet field on the wall.

Mara assigned four cruisers to break the shot. Before they fired, Good Harvest crossed the heavy's prediction line. The Kestrel cruiser had no intact radiator and less than a minute before thermal failure. Its captain drove directly across the charged stream, forcing the Veyr to release early or lose the angle.

The beam struck Good Harvest amidships.

Its hull opened in a white line. The drive section continued past the heavy braid while the forward compartments spun toward Hesper's moon. Kes used the interruption. Uncommon Valor and two escorts fired into the heavy's exposed witness core. The core fractured; all three vessel-strands lost coordinated thrust.

"Eleven Veyr braids confirmed destroyed or combat-disabled," Sori said.

The remaining Veyr force broke away from Hesper. No retreat order appeared in human-readable channels. Their geometry simply loosened, each braid choosing an outer vector while damaged strands covered one another. They did not scatter. They retained enough cohesion to punish pursuit.

Mara stopped Kes before she could follow. "Mission complete. Hold the gateward volume."

This time acknowledgment came at once. "Kestrel holds."

Human ships coasted through a battlefield measured in residual heat and bad trajectories. Strike craft called for recovery. Tugs launched from the moon to catch disabled escorts before their momentum carried them into the gate lens. Hesper's field generators remained live in five trenches. The sixth had lost cooling, but its crew claimed they could isolate it without collapsing the lens.

Mara ordered the fleet count.

Names arrived before totals. Gannet Law. Mercy of Numbers. Bright Census. Good Harvest. Three more escorts gone during the southern exchange. The cruiser Measure of Rain had broken apart after reporting a controlled magazine fire. House Sparrow lost its tow when Cairn Step took a particle lance; both drifted cold beyond recovery.

Eight human ships had been destroyed. Nine others needed yard repair. Four strike craft lacked fuel to reach any surviving deck, and moon rescue launches were already climbing toward them.

Kes appeared on Mara's private channel. Emergency light flattened the lines around her mouth. Blood marked one collar, though her posture gave no sign of injury.

"You prepared an arrest order?" Kes asked.

"I prepared an override. Twice."

"But you didn't issue it."

"Your first maneuver served the mission. Your second required me to recognize the same fact faster."

Kes looked toward something outside her camera. "Good Harvest had four hundred nineteen aboard. Thirty-two escape pods transmitted."

Mara moved the number into the rescue ledger. Victory did not reduce it. "Moon launches will reach them in eleven minutes."

"Some of them."

"Yes."

Neither spoke for several breaths. Around them, Hesper Gate continued to hold a lens stable enough for evacuation and reinforcement. Humanity had defended a system for the first time since the Burning Line ignited. The fact carried strategic value. It also occupied the same ledger as eight empty ship names.

Sori interrupted through priority command. "Admiral, we recovered a Veyr targeting relay from the disabled outer braid. It retained route predictions."

Mara opened the packet. The enemy withdrawal vectors appeared first, all bending beyond Hesper's outer moon toward a gate route that did not lead back across the Burning Line. Beneath them sat a ranked set of human infrastructure targets generated before the battle.

Hesper occupied the first line.

The second carried a sunspindle signature and a system population count. Mara recognized the blue-white stellar spectrum before the label resolved.

Talassar.

A projected transit window blinked beside it: three days for the fastest Veyr elements, perhaps four if battle damage slowed them. Human reinforcements would need longer. The victory at Hesper had preserved one gate and forced an enemy retreat, but it had not turned the enemy homeward.

Mara sent the packet to Kes, Aras, and the Line Defense command tier. Then she ordered every ship with usable drives to begin recovery, refuel, and route computation.

Beyond the command wall, Hesper's lens reopened across the dark. Rescue craft went out through a field of cooling ceramic, and the fleet prepared to race the survivors of its first victory toward another inhabited sun.

Chapter 8 — Talassar Evacuates

Tovan found his homeworld inside a queue of damaged ships.

Talassar filled the lower half of the flight-deck display, ocean blue crossed by white storm bands and the silver thread of its orbital ring. Forty-nine transports waited along that thread. Some had carried commuters two days earlier. Others belonged to ore combines, pilgrimage unions, schools, and three planetary navies that no longer existed as separate commands. Together they had berths for fewer people than the habitats needed to send down or away.

A printed label covered the corner of Tovan's repair slate: ORBITAL RESIDENTS, 1.2 MILLION. Below it, another figure described the people sheltering on the surface: 18.4 million.

Neither count helped him align a warped landing clamp.

"Two millimeters left," he told the winch crew.

The clamp moved with a groan through Common Measure's Deck Four structure. Hesper's particle hit had twisted the carrier's aft radiator root and shifted three recovery lanes out of tolerance. Deck One could receive heavy shuttles. Deck Two handled strike craft and medical pods. Deck Four now had to take civilian lifts whose pilots had never landed on a carrier moving under battle conditions.

"Stop. Lock it there."

A technician drove ceramic pins into the clamp collar. The pins had come from a Talassan fishing platform's storm shutters. Their original inspection marks still showed salt corrosion around the edges.

Captain Aras's voice crossed the deck speakers. "Evacuation cycle begins in twelve minutes. First lifts from Ring Habitats Six through Nine. Enemy arrival estimate remains nineteen hours."

"A generous margin," Tovan said.

His assistant, Lio Gan, looked up from a coolant manifold. "You don't believe it?"

"I believe nineteen hours. I don't believe anything that calls nineteen hours generous."

The first civilian lift approached before Deck Four finished pressure testing. Its hull carried four hundred people inside a craft certified for two hundred forty. Families occupied cargo tie-downs. Station medics had strapped patients between scrubber stacks. A hand-painted orange circle on the bow identified the craft as property of the Ring Eight municipal school board.

Tovan guided its pilot by voice. "You have a three-degree roll relative to the deck. Correct with bow thrusters only. Your stern cluster will push you into the damaged rail."

"My bow thrusters read dead," the pilot said.

"They aren't dead. The carrier's field is confusing your sensor return. Give them a half-second pulse."

"If you're wrong?"

"Then I will become much easier to blame. Pulse now."

The lift corrected. Its landing collar met the improvised clamp hard enough to bow the pins, but all four locks seated. Deck crews opened the passenger tube while frost climbed its sides. People came through carrying sealed water sacks, instrument cases, folded blankets, and small household objects chosen without enough time. One boy held a transparent box containing blue coral polyps. The box's pump had stopped.

Tovan took it from him, connected a suit oxygen line at minimum flow, and handed it back. "Keep the valve below one quarter. Coral dislikes enthusiasm."

The boy stared at the damaged deck around them. "Is this ship going to fight?"

"After it finishes pretending to be a ferry."

"Will it win?"

Tovan checked the clamp before answering. "It will do what we can keep it able to do. Follow the yellow line."

By the sixth lift, Deck Four smelled of overheated seals and wet clothing. By the tenth, the evacuation schedule had slipped forty-three minutes. Every transport had to clear a safe mass corridor before another could dock with the ring. Two transports reported faulty pressure doors. A third had enough scrubber capacity for nine hours and a gate crossing estimated at eleven.

Tovan rerouted canisters from strike-craft reserve. Warheads could wait without breathable air. Passengers could not.

The transfer required opening a sealed combat inventory, which demanded two officers and a deck engineer under peacetime rules. Tovan found one flight lieutenant unloading children from a lift and the other directing a pressure team. He recorded both names, stated the emergency basis aloud, and cut the inventory seal himself. Forty breathing canisters rolled out in padded racks. Their green fittings did not match civilian transport couplings.

Lio brought adapter stock from the machine cage. Together they turned the first coupling while evacuees moved past their knees. A transport mechanic watched, memorized the thread pattern, and began another without waiting for permission. Soon six people worked along the rack: carrier technicians, two ring mechanics, a pilot with one arm in a sling, and a school custodian who understood the transport's old pressure manifold better than anyone wearing fleet gray.

"Label each adapter by rated flow," Tovan said.

The custodian marked them with a classroom paint pen. "Rated by whom?"

"By us, for the next eleven hours. After that, a yard can argue."

When the first transport disconnected, its scrubber estimate had risen from nine hours to thirteen. The departure controller pushed it into the gate queue before anyone could reconsider. Tovan watched the craft clear the carrier's damaged rail by less than a meter. Salvaged air, improvised threads, and a stranger's orange handwriting had bought four hours for eight hundred people.

A private message indicator blinked on his slate. He opened it with the heel of his glove.

Anja Rhee appeared inside a service elevator. Her gray hair floated around her face whenever the cab passed through low-gravity sections. Behind her, medical cases occupied one wall and six patients lay secured to folding racks.

"Your captain's office has sent me a priority berth," she said. "Twice."

"I asked them once. Efficiency improves when people listen the first time."

"Cancel it."

Tovan tightened the slate's volume as another lift settled onto the deck. "You run a medical elevator, Mother. Someone else can run it."

"Three people can operate the motors. One knows which coastal shelters have neonatal power couplings, and she happens to be talking to you."

"The orbital ring has priority departure. Surface staff come up on the return lifts."

"Not this staff. We are moving patients down."

The elevator lights flickered across her face. Talassar's orbital medical system had begun emptying critical patients into hardened shelf-city hospitals. Anja's lift traveled the tether between Ring Twelve and the sea platform at Pelagic Narrows, one of four routes still carrying full medical loads.

"Enemy route telemetry gives us less than a day," Tovan said.

"Then you have less than a day to stop wasting my time."

He nearly laughed. The sound caught behind his teeth. "Take the berth after your next descent. I can reserve a surface shuttle."

"You can reserve it for someone whose work ends after the next descent."

A warning light turned amber above his station. The incoming lift had lost alignment.

"Anja Rhee, listen to me."

"I am listening. I am not obeying. We raised you with that distinction."

The connection broke as the carrier rolled to meet the bad approach. Tovan clipped the slate to his chest and shouted corrections toward the clamp crew.

For nineteen hours, evacuation looked like machinery surviving its operators.

Transports moved from ring docks to the gate queue. Surface lifts climbed with maintenance crews and descended with patients. Common Measure accepted passengers, fuel bladders, and craft too damaged to trust beyond Talassar orbit. Civilian controllers fitted departures through four-minute windows between military patrols. Every late docking propagated through the queue until an entire habitat watched its assigned transport leave half empty or miss departure.

Tovan stopped counting cycles and began counting failures that had not become deaths.

A cargo tug lost guidance beside Ring Four. Carrier technicians rebuilt its position feed from a mining radar. A passenger tube split its pressure lining; two deckhands wrapped the seam with emergency membrane while seven hundred evacuees waited behind it. Deck Two's coolant manifold clogged with insulation fibers from Hesper. Tovan cleared it by hand and came out with his gloves frozen white to the wrists.

At hour twenty, the enemy estimate changed from nine hours to four.

At hour twenty-two, Talassar's outer probes went dark.

The Veyr arrived above the system plane, avoiding the defended gate lane. Twenty-nine braids showed on military tracking. Their first needle release targeted the sunspindle control loops and the orbital ring's maneuver stations. They did not fire on the crowded transport queue.

That choice frightened Tovan more than indiscriminate attack would have. Someone had measured the system and selected the parts that allowed everyone else to live in it.

"All decks, combat separation," Aras ordered. "Civilian lifts will clear carrier volumes. Strike craft launch on local control."

Deck Four still held two docked lifts. One carried six hundred eleven people. The other contained reactor technicians from the sunspindle. Tovan could release both clamps, but the second lift's bow thrusters had failed during docking.

"Tow it with a deck tractor," Lio said.

"Tractor can't operate beyond the field curtain."

"Then we push it through."

They chained two tractors to the lift's stern and drove together. Wheels slipped on the deck coating. The lift moved a meter, then another, its mass dragging against a misaligned clamp. Outside the open armored doors, Talassar turned beneath them. Particle fire made the upper atmosphere glow in thin violet arcs.

A needle struck the ring near Habitat Ten. The impact passed through three inhabited decks before crossing empty space and entering the opposite rim. Pressure vapor spread in a white fan. Ring rotation carried the damaged section toward the horizon.

The tractors reached their warning line.

Tovan kept driving.

"Field curtain in twelve meters," Lio said.

"Release chains on my mark."

"Tractors won't stop before the curtain."

"They cost less than a lift."

At four meters, the dead bow thrusters lit. A remote repair crew inside the lift had bypassed their failed sensor controller. The craft pulled away under its own power. Tovan dumped the tractor chains and braked. Both machines stopped with their front housings inside the field shimmer and their cabs on the deck side.

Lio looked at him through a scratched visor. "Good estimate."

"I was prepared to revise it after impact."

Strike craft launched overhead. The deck doors began closing. Through the narrowing gap, Tovan saw the sunspindle's black magnetic loops against the star. Several glowed at their attachment nodes, not from normal current but from Veyr particle streams forcing energy through control structures.

A system alert ordered every ring habitat to abandon rotation-control compartments.

Anja's elevator beacon still moved downward along the Pelagic tether.

Tovan called her. The signal returned broken by interference, then resolved into a view of the elevator's service bay. Water ran along one wall from a ruptured patient tank.

"We have twelve kilometers to the surface terminus," she said. "One motor string overheated."

"The ring is under fire. Cut free and use the cab's emergency descent drive."

"That drive can land eighteen people. I have seventy-three."

"The tether may not remain attached."

"Then tell me how long it needs."

He pulled structural telemetry from Talassar's civil net. The Pelagic tether showed green along most of its length. Its orbital root shared load with two ring spans already venting atmosphere. If either span failed, the tether could whip across hundreds of kilometers before the elevator reached its lower counterweight.

"Thirty-one minutes," he said. "After that I can't predict the root."

"We need twenty-six."

"That assumes both motors."

"I have technicians."

"You have nurses."

"People who keep bodies working understand machinery better than people who only admire it."

Tovan sent her a bypass diagram for the overheated string. It disabled every automatic trip except overspeed. The motor could burn, weld its bearings, or pull the elevator down fast enough to reach shelter.

"Bridge the two blue contacts," he said. "Not the white. White releases the brake."

Anja handed the slate out of view. "Received. Now go keep your carrier from falling on us."

The channel failed again.

A weapons impact rolled through Common Measure. Deck braces flexed. Somewhere above, a missile magazine reported fire. Tovan stayed with the civil telemetry until Anja's elevator resumed descent on two motor strings.

Then he returned to the deck.

The sunspindle lost coherent control on Day 14.

Its loops did not vanish at once. One collapsed into the star while the remaining arcs strained against asymmetric magnetic force. Power across Talassar flickered by continent. Shelf cities dropped nonessential grids. The gate lens switched to reserve reactors and reduced its transit aperture, forcing transports to depart one at a time.

Thirty-seven transports had crossed. Twelve remained.

The orbital ring could no longer correct its rotation. Three maneuver stations had gone cold; a fourth fired continuously through a damaged command relay. Its thrust pulled one arc ahead of the rest, loading the joints between habitats beyond design limits.

Tovan watched strain values climb from Deck Four's control booth. He had evacuated the deck itself after a pressure failure, but the booth retained atmosphere and a direct antenna to the ring. Civil engineers shared his display from surface bunkers. None had authority over the whole structure. Each owned a span, a motor station, or a tether.

"Separate Ring Twelve at joints eleven and thirteen," he said. "Use explosive bolts before the spin differential reaches four percent."

A controller from Pelagic Narrows answered. "Ring Twelve contains forty-two thousand people awaiting lifts."

"If the joints tear, it takes Ring Eleven and the tether root with it."

"We have two transports approaching Twelve. Give us nineteen minutes."

Every important task now requested nineteen minutes. The system had none left to distribute.

Tovan checked the approaching craft. One could dock in seven minutes. The second needed fourteen. Loading required another six if passengers kept discipline. Cutting the section after nineteen would leave less than a minute before projected joint failure.

"You have seventeen," he said. "At seventeen, blow both joints even if the second ship remains docked."

The controller's breath rasped over the channel. "Under whose authority?"

"Mine, if you need a name for the inquiry."

"I need a name for the people aboard."

Tovan looked at Ring Twelve's population ledger. He knew neighborhoods, not individuals. His mother had taken him there as a child to see the tidal observatory, where a classroom window turned once every ninety minutes from ocean to stars. That window probably faced the sun now, shutters closed against particle glare.

"Load the first transport from sectors nearest joint eleven," he said. "Second takes the medical decks and joint thirteen. Everyone else moves toward section center and seals bulkheads. Separation leaves them in orbit. They may survive until tugs reach them."

"May."

"That is the word we have."

Civil channels carried the order. Crowds moved inside the ring according to pressure doors, corridor widths, and the speed of the slowest person in each section. The first transport docked cleanly. The second arrived three minutes late with damage along its engine skirt.

A Veyr needle struck Ring Seven.

Stress propagated through the entire structure. Joint thirteen's strain jumped past five percent. Ring Twelve began to flex out of plane, dragging the Pelagic tether root with it.

"Blow joints now," Tovan said.

"Second transport has six thousand aboard and nine thousand in the tube."

"Close its pressure door. Blow now."

The controller did not answer. On the structural display, explosive-bolt circuits armed at joint eleven. Joint thirteen stayed gray.

Tovan found the maintenance path and sent the command himself. Carrier authentication had no formal place in Talassar's civil ring controls. Hesper emergency authority had propagated through the network, and the old machines accepted it.

Both joint lines flashed.

Ring Twelve separated. The shock threw its section outward, taking the attached transport with it. The open passenger tube tore between pressure doors. Atmosphere and people entered space along the break, too small for Tovan's display to distinguish.

The remaining ring shed load and held for fifty-three seconds.

Then Ring Seven's damaged structure failed.

A silver arc hundreds of kilometers long folded away from Talassar. Habitats tore loose in clusters. Some retained spin and power. Others began slow, tumbling descents toward the atmosphere. Defense tugs crossed the debris with rescue beacons lit. Veyr fire continued against the sunspindle and gate-control stations while the ring came apart around it.

Anja's elevator reached Pelagic Narrows three minutes before its orbital root snapped.

Her message arrived as text because the shelter had no video bandwidth.

ALL PATIENTS BELOW. ELEVATOR LOST. STOP RESERVING ME SEATS.

Tovan read it twice. Then he marked Anja Rhee alive in the family registry and returned to transport loading.

The forty-ninth transport entered the gate under reserve power.

Behind it, Talassar's orbital ring fell in burning segments. Friction drew orange lines across the upper atmosphere. Surface defenses tracked the largest pieces and fired mining lasers to break those aimed at shelf cities. Every fragment divided the problem into smaller fragments without removing its mass.

Common Measure withdrew after the final passage, carrying evacuees in launch bays, workshops, corridors, and emptied magazines. Tovan stood beside the Deck Four clamp. Its storm-shutter pins had bent almost flat but had not sheared.

The fleet registry issued its first consolidated count.

Of 1.2 million orbital residents, 1.06 million had escaped through the gate or reached protected surface descent. Officials confirmed 113,000 dead or missing. The remaining difference consisted of people alive in isolated ring sections, unverified shelter lists, duplicate manifests, and rescue crews still moving among debris.

Talassar's 18.4 million surface residents remained below damaged grids and a sky crossed by wreckage. The Veyr had not occupied the world. Their braids left after crippling the sunspindle, the ring, and most gate infrastructure. Talassar still possessed one unstable transit aperture and enough reserve power to open it for relief windows.

A child approached Tovan carrying a transparent coral box. The polyps had withdrawn into pale branches, but the oxygen line still fed bubbles through the water.

"You said the carrier would do what we kept it able to do," the boy said.

Tovan examined the bent clamp, the scorched deck, and the world turning beyond the pressure window. "It did."

"Did we keep Talassar able?"
