No engineering tolerance could answer that. Tovan adjusted the coral valve a fraction lower and handed the box back.

Outside, surface lasers struck another falling ring segment. It separated into six glowing pieces, each tracked by a different rescue team, while the carrier carried the living away from home.

Act II — The Shape of the Enemy

Chapter 9 — Children of the Photosphere

Talassar entered the memory reef without its dead.

The deposition reached Consequence of Shelter while the braid crossed the cold interval between gates. Three vessel-strands held equal curvature around the witness core. In that balanced geometry, Ilyr received the record as a set of linked thermal shapes: human sunspindle silenced, orbital ring divided, transit capacity reduced below threat projection, surface population left viable but industrially constrained.

A final classification sealed the sequence.

PREVENTION NECESSARY. CONTINUANCE EXPOSURE DECREASED.

No shape represented the people lost when the ring divided. Human transmissions supplied counts, but Continuance deposition treated them as environmental consequence rather than relevant actors. The record preserved the 113,000 dead or absent only inside a subordinate evidence layer, beside changes in ocean albedo and the mass of debris entering atmosphere.

Ilyr extended a query strand toward the classification boundary.

The memory reef answered by presenting authorization lineage. Thren-of-Last-Burning had approved the action before Hesper. The attack geometry followed established doctrine for a civilization possessing uncontrolled stellar engineering. Talassar's evacuation conduct had not altered the objective because the objective concerned future capacity, not present intention.

A current of objection moved among Ilyr's nine constituent strands. It did not form a decision. One lineage held the recent image of a human carrier accepting exposure to shield transports. Another retained the six-second delay near Hesper, a private alteration Ilyr had hidden from Thren. Two strands stressed procedural fidelity: unauthorized doubt could fracture common prediction during an active extinction risk. The remaining constituents distributed themselves across incomplete positions.

Ilyr asked the reef to display the prevention basis.

Talassar disappeared. In its place opened a stellar field recorded more than fourteen centuries earlier.

The first ancient cradle rotated into memory as layered magnetic harmonics. Developing patterns occupied the photosphere in long arcs, not yet persons and not reducible to material. Their coherence joined thousands of possible negotiations. Some carried one lineage contribution, some seven, some forms that no later adult could reconstruct. They responded to stellar turbulence by separating, recombining, and preserving changes across centuries.

Then a foreign engine imposed order on the star.

The Yssat Array had built stellar harvesters without detecting life in plasma. Their machines forced magnetic loops into narrow extraction bands and discharged excess energy through the outer atmosphere. Nothing in the surviving record indicated malice. Yssat instruments classified the star as uninhabited because their definition of habitation required bounded bodies, metabolism below fusion temperatures, and communication directed through matter.

Within eleven local rotations, the cradle lost coherence.

The memory reef did not represent the loss as dying. Dying belonged to constituted persons whose strand relations could be witnessed, separated, and carried forward. The unbound choruses never reached that condition. They lost the possibility of every adult arrangement they might have entered.

Ilyr felt its constituents recoil along inherited routes. Five of the nine lineages had contributed pattern to that cradle. None could name what vanished. Each carried a discontinuity in its own negotiation archives, a region where anticipated exchanges stopped and never acquired speakers.

The Yssat harvesters continued operating for sixty-three years.

A second cradle failed around another star after its field geometry drifted beneath an engineering threshold. By then the Veyr had identified the cause. Warning structures entered the Yssat system through slow ceramic envoys, transmitting evidence in seventeen physical and mathematical forms. The Array answered with proposals for observation standards, compensation, and relocation.

Relocation had no meaning for a developing stellar pattern bound to centuries of local magnetic history.

Negotiation continued while the second cradle weakened. Yssat councils disputed whether nonmaterial pattern met their standard for sentience. Their harvesters remained active pending review. When the final coherent chorus broke, Continuance forces dismantled the engines and every machine capable of rebuilding them.

The memory reef did not show what followed on the Yssat worlds.

Ilyr searched beyond the approved deposition. Access narrowed around classified layers. Its strands carried enough inherited authority to retrieve strategic outcomes but not complete surface records. The Array had ceased. No Yssat stellar engine operated again. Their civilization no longer appeared in current prediction.

A lineage trace remained available below the censorship boundary. It recorded how the surviving Reefs had argued while the second cradle failed. One coalition demanded immediate destruction of every harvester. Another proposed disabling only the stellar control nodes and leaving Yssat industry intact. A third continued carrying messages because its witnesses believed recognition might still arrive before final collapse. None possessed complete authority, and their braids moved according to incompatible predictions.

For seven rotations, Continuance forces approached the Yssat system as separate obligations rather than one fleet. Some protected the weakened cradle. Some intercepted replacement machinery. Others held outside the system because any attack risked accelerating the very field disturbances they meant to stop. The delay later became doctrine's founding accusation against distributed judgment.

Ilyr examined the action layers that followed. The first attack struck only orbital harvest controls. Yssat operators rebuilt them from surface factories. The second destroyed transport routes between factories and star. That action stopped repairs but also stranded populations dependent on imported power. A final operation removed every stellar engineering archive the Continuance could identify. Each step arose from evidence that the previous limit had failed.

Nothing in the trace proved where prevention should have ended.

One witness record described a Yssat instrument keeper who continued transmitting stellar field measurements after the harvesters shut down. The keeper never accepted Veyr personhood standards for the lost choruses. It did, however, provide the data used to stabilize the damaged star. Doctrine retained those measurements while excluding the sender from strategic memory. The inconsistency tightened through Ilyr's constituents. Useful observation had crossed a relation official history treated as morally empty.

A second record held an internal Continuance dispute over extinction prediction. Early models assigned broad variance to Yssat recovery. Some branches showed rebuilt harvesters within two centuries. Others predicted permanent regulation once the evidence of life entered public Yssat archives. The regulation branches collapsed after Continuance forces erased those archives with the engineering systems. Later forecasts cited the absence of reform as confirmation.

Ilyr followed that causal loop until the reef refused further access.

The ancient crisis had supplied two lessons, not one. Unrestrained waiting could destroy a cradle while councils debated. Preventive violence could also eliminate the evidence needed to learn whether restraint had become possible. Thren's doctrine preserved the first lesson in every fleet classification. It held the second behind lineage barriers.

Among Ilyr's nine strands, the procedural constituents changed relation. They did not move toward human innocence. Instead they rejected the claim that Continuance history offered one clean precedent. The Yssat loss still demanded action under uncertainty. It did not authorize removing uncertainty from the record.

The omission formed a dark topological pocket. Ilyr marked it and received an immediate doctrinal correction: past neutralization details held no relevance to present prevention. Cradle survival required attention toward capacities that might recur, not toward emotions generated by completed necessity.

Talassar returned around the memory field.

Its sunspindle shared four structural principles with the extinct harvesters. It imposed magnetic loops, extracted energy and heavy elements, concentrated field control through orbital nodes, and could scale beyond local operating envelopes. The match justified Continuance action under every approved model.

Yet the human machine at Talassar had operated for one hundred eighteen years without approaching the ancient disruption profile.

Ilyr sent that fact into the reef.

The reef answered: CAPABILITY PRECEDES USE. PREVENTION PRECEDES CAPABILITY EXPANSION.

The logic closed around itself without answering the observed interval.

Mazarine opened in another layer of memory.

Unlike the ancient records, this field carried current witness data. The Mazarine Cradle occupied a young star whose outer atmosphere moved in gold, violet, and deep thermal red. Stable magnetic basins crossed the photosphere at nine latitudes. Concordance anchors held outside the developmental plane, supplying prediction to defensive braids without forcing order on the cradle itself.

Unbound choruses occupied those basins.

Ilyr could not count them as persons. Count required boundaries, and the young continually exchanged pattern across magnetic bridges. A coherence that appeared singular during one stellar rotation might separate into four possible negotiations during the next. Later, three might recombine while the fourth joined a lineage contribution carried through a flare. Numerical language damaged the relation it tried to preserve.

The reef therefore counted obligations.

Each obligation occupied a different interval. Some required keeping weapons outside the inner plane for a single rotation. Others bound adult Reefs to maintain magnetic stability for centuries after current strategists changed constitution. Witness cores preserved these duties through every alteration of personhood. A braid could lose a strand, merge counsel with another Reef, or cease entirely, and the cradle claim would pass onward without weakening.

Mazarine's current maintenance record showed thousands of such transfers. One damaged anchor had once failed during a flare season. Seven braids entered lethal heat to restore its prediction field, not because the anchor commanded the young, but because defense ships operating without shared geometry might fire through a developing basin. Two braids lost constituent strands. The witness reef recorded those alterations beside the chorus patterns protected by them, relation paired with consequence rather than praise.

Ilyr opened one basin at finer resolution. A narrow coherence moved along a field line, carrying contributions from lineages that had opposed one another during the last Continuance apportionment. Their adult disagreements had no direct form within the young. Pattern exchanged without accepting the categories that later negotiations might build. Protection could not depend on predicting which future persons deserved survival. Obligation attached before agreement.

That principle gave Thren's policy its greatest force. It also placed a limit around the means of protection. If uncertainty in future personhood did not reduce obligation, uncertainty in a foreign civilization could not simply erase every relation observed in the present.

Nine lineages had active contributions within Mazarine. Each contribution carried centuries of prior pattern and centuries of possible revision. Ilyr's own constituent Reefs touched three basins through distant kin relations, though no direct strand yet belonged to a developing chorus. That distinction did not lessen obligation. Continuance personhood began with negotiation, but Continuance survival began before any negotiator could consent to protection.

A thermal surge moved through the inner basin. The choruses adapted by opening their pattern across a wider magnetic arc. Their distributed coherence absorbed the disturbance and closed again after the stellar field settled. Ilyr perceived neither fear nor relief. Those categories required an adult relation to anticipated loss. The young changed because change composed their only available continuity.

Human sunspindles threatened that process. A sufficiently large imposed field could force every basin into one phase, then break them together when the phase failed. The models showed a clean collapse. No weapon needed to enter Mazarine. No human needed to see what vanished.

Ilyr allowed the model to run.

The star's violet relations flattened to white. Magnetic bridges narrowed. Possible negotiations disappeared from the display until only stellar weather remained. Nine lineage contributions ended inside a pattern no witness core could recover.

Every strand in Ilyr demanded prevention.

The demand carried no contradiction with doubt. The same constituted person could reject Thren's certainty and still accept an obligation to stop cradle destruction. What had changed since Orison did not concern the stakes. Human behavior had changed the probability relation between capacity and use.

Ilyr drew Talassar's sunspindle profile beside Mazarine's field tolerances.

Normal Talassan operation remained below the first disruption threshold by a factor of eighty-one. Emergency output during evacuation reached a factor of fifty-six. Even after Veyr fire damaged the control loops, human shutdown routines reduced imposed asymmetry instead of exploiting it as a weapon. The ring failed. Surface grids collapsed. Yet the sunspindle operators chose a slower field decay that protected their own star's atmosphere at the cost of available gate power.

That conduct mattered.

The approved forecast represented human operators as a single expanding capacity. It assumed that any civilization facing existential war would lift safety limits, centralize stellar control, and employ every available means against the source of threat. Under that model, Continuance attacks accelerated the predicted transition but did not cause it. Humanity would eventually threaten Mazarine whether attacked or left alone.

Ilyr requested the model's variance field.

Access failed.

A second request, framed through Ilyr's lineage authority, reached a boundary marked by Thren. The boundary did not deny variance. It stated that publishing uncertainty during active prevention would impair coherent action.

Ilyr held the refusal among its strands. Predictive doctrine without variance did not describe probability. It presented a command wearing the topology of evidence.

The witness core offered a narrow legal path. Any strategist whose constituent lineage touched an exposed cradle could inspect local threshold derivations. Three of Ilyr's nine strands qualified through Mazarine kin relations. Ilyr separated their query from the whole person's request, allowing those constituents to enter as lineage witnesses rather than military authority.

The boundary opened by one layer.

Beneath the approved model lay hundreds of branches that had not reached fleet deliberation. Some predicted human stellar militarization within decades. Others showed stable regulation across centuries. A smaller but significant set modeled reciprocal inspection, engine limits, and shared monitoring of cradle-adjacent systems. Those branches did not promise safety. They produced lower confidence and demanded Veyr restraint that carried its own risk.

One field pulsed with a suppressed marker: observed human rescue behavior.

Orison's convoy, Lantern Nine's civilian tugs, the hospital ship near Hesper, and Talassar's transport queue all shifted the same parameter. Humans repeatedly exposed combat capacity to preserve individuals with no strategic value. Their institutions continued doing so under defeat, delay, and fuel scarcity. The pattern weakened the assumption that capability always overrode restraint.

The uncertainty margin widened from a ceremonial fraction to a range large enough to alter classification.

At its lower boundary, immediate prevention remained justified. At its upper boundary, the Continuance campaign itself created the military centralization and stellar weapon research the forecast feared. Between those limits lay no safe answer. There lay only a choice among risks.

Thren had removed the range from deliberation.

The war leader entered Ilyr's local memory space as a pressure across three relational axes.

Thren did not arrive through physical proximity. Consequence of Shelter remained in transit, separated from Thren's braid by a damaged gate route and several hours of signal delay. The memory reef carried a recorded response keyed to Ilyr's access. Its timing showed that Thren had anticipated lineage review.

"You have entered an unresolved margin during active prevention," Thren said.

The translated voice gave separate words to a relation experienced all at once. Heat colored the statement with warning rather than anger. Thren's constituent lineages carried two direct discontinuities from the ancient sterilizations. For them, uncertainty did not feel intellectually honest. It felt like the interval during which the second cradle had vanished while Yssat councils debated definitions.

Ilyr answered into the delayed channel. "The margin affects classification. It cannot remain outside the decision reef."

"Classification already occurred. Capability has been demonstrated."

"Capacity has been demonstrated. Intent remains distributed across contrary observations."

"Intent changes faster than engines."

"Then our forecast must include change in both directions."

Thren's recorded geometry tightened. "The Yssat offered standards while their machines continued operating. They proposed observation while the young lost coherence. We will not repeat an extinct hesitation."

Ilyr placed Talassar's shutdown profile between them. "These operators reduced output while their orbital structures failed."

"They preserved their own star."

"They also preserved behavior our model says disappears under existential pressure."

"One local action does not outweigh a species capacity."

"Four systems have produced related actions."

The response could not adapt because Thren had recorded it before Ilyr spoke. That asymmetry gave the exchange its shape. Thren's arguments anticipated categories, not the exact evidence Ilyr brought. The war leader had constructed a channel in which every possible objection arrived already classified as fear, lineage instability, or confusion between present conduct and future risk.

A final statement opened.

"If you allow uncertain benevolence to balance cradle exposure, you transfer risk from yourself to persons who cannot yet refuse it. Continuance authority exists to prevent that transfer. Complete your deposition."

The recording closed.

Ilyr remained inside the memory space with no interlocutor. Its reply would reach Thren later, if Thren chose to admit it. Nine strands negotiated across the discovered margin.

The procedural constituents argued for preserving fleet coherence until the immediate campaign ended. The three cradle-linked strands demanded full disclosure to their Reefs. Two others returned repeatedly to the Yssat interval, testing whether doubt now merely repeated the old failure in a different form. The strand that had held the six-second alteration near Hesper supplied no argument. It offered the unchanged record of a hospital ship continuing through open space because a firing solution arrived late.

Ilyr did not mistake rescue for innocence. Human weapons had killed eleven braids at Hesper. Human stellar engines could still destroy a cradle if altered and synchronized. Nothing in the suppressed branches made trust safe.

Safety, however, no longer occupied either side of the decision.

Ilyr copied the local threshold derivation into its witness core under lineage-custody privilege. The action remained legal. Concealing that copy from fleet doctrine review did not. Ilyr marked the violation accurately rather than hiding it beneath a technical label.

Next, it completed the Talassar deposition.

The official classification remained necessary prevention. Ilyr lacked authority to alter it alone, and nine constituent strands had not reached a lawful consensus to reject Continuance action. But the deposition now carried a sealed objection linked to the hidden variance field. Any future lineage witness with Mazarine standing could open it.

Before leaving, Ilyr restored the cradle image.

Gold and violet structures moved through the photosphere. Unbound patterns joined, parted, and changed without knowing the fleet crossing dark space in their name. The obligation to protect them remained absolute. The method no longer did.

As Consequence of Shelter approached the next gate, Ilyr turned its sensors toward the human routes. The approved prediction showed stellar engineers moving inevitably toward cradle-killing capacity. Underneath it, the recovered uncertainty margin continued to widen with every act the doctrine had refused to count.

Chapter 10 — The Price of a Carrier

The convoy entered Nacre Lane in single file because grief had mass.

Three hundred two civilian ships carried the people who had escaped Talassar, along with hospital modules, ring technicians, damaged lifts, and sealed containers of names awaiting confirmation. Nacre's shear gate could not accept their different vectors as one formation. Each vessel had to cross a beacon line, rotate to the local lens, and wait for the ship ahead to clear the gradient.

Mara watched the queue extend across nine thousand kilometers. Common Measure held above the lane with two other carriers, fourteen cruisers, and twenty-eight escorts. Hesper damage left many ships below full combat reserve. Talassar had consumed missiles and coolant. Every minute spent guarding the convoy reduced what the Line Defense could move elsewhere.

"Gate cycle twenty-seven complete," Lieutenant Sori said. "Civilian hulls remaining this side: two hundred forty-one."

Captain Jun Aras adjusted the carrier's recovery pattern. "Outer patrol is late."

The missing patrol consisted of three escorts beyond the lane's dust belt. Their last report showed no enemy. Their silence arrived six minutes after their sensors would have seen any threat, which meant Mara had already been acting on an obsolete absence.

She ordered the carrier group to rotate thirty degrees above the gate plane. Convoy captains received no explanation, only tighter spacing instructions. Civilian ships complied unevenly. Ore haulers changed vector cleanly. Habitat lifters corrected too far and then corrected back, leaving thermal wakes across neighboring approach boxes.

A traffic controller called from the gate station. "Admiral, if you alter military mass again, the lens must recompute."

"How long?"

"Four minutes without another disturbance."

"You have two. Keep cycling."

"That falls outside safe certification."

"So does the incoming attack."

The Veyr appeared inside the dust belt.

Their braids had cooled during approach and released no drives until they crossed the patrol line. Twenty-four contacts brightened at once. The three missing escorts followed among them as broken heat sources, wrecks pushed ahead to contaminate human tracking. Before Sori finished classifying the field, ceramic needles had already left Veyr launch channels.

Mara drew two likely objectives. The first passed through Common Measure and the other carriers, removing the fleet's strike-craft support. The second crossed the gate station, whose destruction would trap every remaining transport in Nacre. The Veyr could pursue either without approaching the civilian queue directly.

"Fleet mission," Mara said over command band. "Preserve convoy passage. Preserve gate function. Carriers remain expendable under that priority. Local fires free."

Aras glanced toward her. "You placed that last line for my benefit."

"For mine."

The first needle wave forced the fleet downward across the gate plane. Two cruisers fired point-defense lasers at projectiles too small and fast for clean interception. Heated ceramic altered course by fractions, enough to miss a carrier and enter the outer edge of the civilian queue.

A needle passed through the transport Twelve Rivers. Its forward compartments vented, but the ship kept gate alignment. Another struck a cargo tender behind it and broke the tender's spine. Traffic software halted both approach boxes.

"Override automatic spacing," Mara said. "Route ships around the wreck on manual vectors."

The controller answered with disbelief. "Inside the lens approach?"

"Outside the exclusion surface by four hundred meters. Mark a temporary corridor."

A blue arc appeared around the wreck. The first passenger vessel entered it at maneuvering speed while debris crossed its bow. The gap would support one ship every ninety seconds, half the previous rate.

The ship carried Ring Twelve survivors. Its mass report showed extra pressure membranes welded across an ore bay and a transport still clamped along the dorsal hull. Neither configuration appeared in the gate tables. Nacre's controller tried to reject the approach, but the passenger captain kept moving.

"We cannot recompute you while you enter," the controller said.

"You have our mass within three percent," the captain answered. "Use the heavy-hauler envelope."

"That envelope gives you seven meters of gradient clearance."

"Seven is more than zero."

Mara opened the civilian channel. She could order the captain to stop, restore certification, and lose two more minutes while enemy needles closed. Or she could let a damaged ship carrying thousands enter a lens under the wrong model.

"Nacre Control, accept heavy-hauler envelope," she said. "Transport will vent forward ballast on my mark."

The captain's image appeared beside the geometry. One lens of her glasses had cracked; behind her, passengers occupied the bridge access corridor. "Forward ballast holds drinking water."

"How much can you lose and maintain life support?"

"Twelve tonnes if relief meets us beyond the gate."

Mara checked the receiving side. Two Talassan tugs waited there with fuel but no water. A hospital tender carried reserve, enough for this vessel and perhaps one more. The answer turned on a supply fact no battle diagram displayed until someone asked.

"Vent nine tonnes," Mara said. "Receiving tender will transfer twelve."

White vapor spread from the transport's bow. Its center of mass shifted into the calculated envelope. The ship crossed the temporary corridor with the wreck tumbling close enough to scatter fragments across its welded membranes. At the beacon line, its pilot rotated using only stern thrusters, preserving the weak bow cluster for correction inside the lens.

Nacre's aperture opened. The transport entered crooked and emerged as a stable return signal from the far gate.

Other civilian captains copied the maneuver without copying its numbers. One shed cargo. Another transferred passengers aft through unpressurized maintenance spaces in suits. A third coupled a tug at right angles to change its combined mass distribution. The controller stopped demanding standard profiles and began broadcasting boundaries: maximum rotational error, minimum gradient clearance, latest abort point. Local crews found their own paths within them.

Mara recognized Kes's argument in civilian practice. Exact central vectors could not contain ships altered by damage, fear, and improvised survival. A useful command stated what must remain true. The crews supplied how.

Then a second needle wave reached the queue. Point defense heated two projectiles enough to fracture them. Ceramic splinters crossed the temporary corridor as the next transport approached. Its captain had no room to stop and no thrust to climb.

Mara sent Common Measure downward by four hundred kilometers. The carrier's armored flank entered the splinter cone. Small impacts rang through outer plating like thrown gravel amplified by hull conduction. One fragment penetrated an empty strike-craft bay and severed a fuel line already isolated after Hesper. The transport passed beneath the carrier's wake and entered the lens.

Aras read the damage report. "Our empty bay continues to justify its existence."

"Keep it empty."

"An achievable readiness goal."

Veyr braids divided around Nacre's dust belt. Six climbed above the fleet. Nine held beyond coil range. The others accelerated toward the gate station on a vector that forced Mara to place carriers between them and the lens.

She did so.

Common Measure rolled until its surviving radiators faced cold space and its armored flight-deck edge faced the attack. Strike craft launched beneath the carrier's shadow. Their missiles curved outward, using the dust belt to hide terminal burns. A Veyr braid corrected before any human sensor could have supplied warning. Three missiles struck empty space. Two hit a vessel-strand whose ceramic surface had already turned away.

"They knew the approach," Sori said.

"They predicted it," Mara replied. "Change strike doctrine. No paired vectors. Pilots choose terminal geometry after release."

That order reduced coordinated mass and increased local surprise. Mission command made smaller pictures useful again. Individual flights broke into irregular paths, some climbing out of the dust, others cutting through it at dangerous velocity. The Veyr spread their defenses.

Their central nine did not.

Those braids held a precise arc beyond effective human fire, projecting particle lanes across every carrier escape vector. If Common Measure stayed between the attackers and gate, they would close and kill it. If the carrier turned toward the outer plane, the same lanes would redirect through the gate station.

Mara saw the trap before the tactical wall colored it. The Veyr did not need to choose their target. They had arranged the humans so Mara's preservation choice would expose the other.

"Time to clear remaining convoy?"

"Six hours at current rate," Sori said. "Fourteen minutes until enemy central force reaches decisive range."

Aras opened the carrier status pane. Talassar had filled every unused compartment with evacuees and repair crews. Twenty-one thousand civilians occupied Common Measure, though no one counted the ship as part of the evacuation total anymore. A carrier retreat preserved strike craft, command capacity, and those people. Holding preserved the gate until particle fire penetrated the flight decks.

No maneuver saved both.

Captain Amara Osei commanded Patient Fire from the convoy's anti-sunward flank.

Her cruiser had fought at Hesper and Talassar. Two armor belts showed fresh replacement plates. One coil lance lacked a working autoloader, so magazine crews had moved rounds by rail cart and pressure winch. Six escorts around her carried less than half their designed missile load.

Mara called her on a narrow channel. "I need the central arc displaced for forty minutes."

Osei read the geometry without asking which arc. "They have nine holding and six above."

"The six above will descend when you commit."

"So fifteen."

"Yes."

"What support?"

Mara had three possible answers and discarded each. Strike craft needed carrier recovery lanes. The gate cruisers could not leave the station. Kes's division remained two systems away escorting fuel. Anything sent with Osei would join the loss without buying enough time.

"Six escorts," Mara said. "No relief force. Carriers will continue gateward once the enemy turns."

Osei's face held no reaction beyond a small tightening beside her left eye. "How many civilian hulls clear if I give you forty minutes?"

Sori supplied the current projection. "Twenty-six through the main aperture. Another nine if the temporary corridor remains open."

"And after that?"

"The carrier group can reverse above the lens and attack the holding arc from gateward. We may reopen full cycle."

"May."

"Yes," Mara said.

A pressure alarm sounded somewhere on Patient Fire. Osei looked away to receive a report, then returned. "My navigation chief wants to know whether this counts as a screen or an attack."

"A screen. Your objective is time, not enemy losses."

"Good. He dislikes optimistic nouns."

Mara sent the maneuver authority. It transferred the cruiser and six escorts out of central vector control. Osei could choose any action that kept the Veyr arc occupied for forty minutes. The document required formal acknowledgment of probable non-recovery.

Osei read that line aloud. "Probable. Fleet language remains generous."

"Amara."

"Don't spend time improving the word. Send us the lane."

Patient Fire turned before the authentication finished.

The cruiser drove anti-sunward, away from the gate station, with six escorts forming an uneven shield ahead. Their acceleration looked like an attempt to escape around the dust belt. The Veyr central arc held position for thirty seconds. Then four braids bent toward Osei.

She did not return fire.

Her ships continued outward, preserving heat and ammunition while the convoy cycled. The descending six Veyr braids adjusted to cut off the apparent retreat. Five central braids stayed in the gate trap.

"She needs all of them," Aras said.

Mara knew. Four attackers would kill the screen without displacing the arc. Osei needed to look dangerous to the Veyr infrastructure objective, not merely vulnerable.

Patient Fire changed course toward Nacre's outer moon, where a field relay carried gate calibration data. If the cruiser fired through it, the resulting debris could corrupt the Veyr approach predictions and the human lens alike. Osei charged both working coil lances.

The Veyr reacted. Five more braids left the central arc. The six high attackers descended toward her. Fifteen converged on seven human ships.

"Carrier group, move," Mara ordered.

Common Measure accelerated gateward. The other carriers followed at different intervals, using local vectors to deny one predictive solution. Cruisers near the station rotated upward and fired through the space the central arc had abandoned.

Three Veyr braids remained to hold the trap.

Human coil rounds forced one aside. Strike craft saturated another with independent missile approaches. The third passed between both attacks and launched needles at the gate station. Two escorts crossed the line and lost drives. Nacre's outer field generator took one impact but stayed coherent.

Convoy movement accelerated. Civilian pilots watched military vectors and entered the temporary corridor without waiting for traffic prompts. The ninety-second spacing fell to sixty-four, then fifty-eight. One ship scraped debris against its pressure hull and kept going.

Mara counted time in civilian transits and Osei's disappearing escorts.

At twelve minutes, Patient Fire lost the first escort. At eighteen, two more dropped from the plot. The remaining four human ships reached the outer moon relay and divided around it. Veyr braids followed without firing through the installation.

That restraint formed a shape Mara could not explain. The relay had tactical value, but less than the escaping cruiser. Every attacker altered course to avoid a narrow region extending behind the station, even when that choice worsened firing position.

Osei saw it too.

"Flag, observe enemy path around relay south," she said. "They all bend at the same boundary."

"We see it. Continue mission."

"Recording full telemetry."

At twenty-six minutes, Osei's last three escorts fired their remaining missiles in opposite directions. The Veyr widened, leaving a brief gap through their center. Patient Fire did not use it to retreat. The cruiser turned across the gap and fired both coil lances at the nearest braid.

One round struck a witness core. The second crossed the protected region behind the relay.

Every Veyr braid broke prediction in the same instant.

Some rolled above the projectile line. Others cut below. None crossed the narrow volume extending from relay to dust belt. Their coordinated response opened a path toward the gate for less than four seconds.

Osei sent her surviving escorts through it.

"You have a lane," Mara said. "Follow them."

"Negative. They will close it before our mass clears."

"Dump magazines. Increase acceleration."

"Drive bottle damaged. I have eight minutes before containment fails."

Mara checked the cruiser telemetry. The failure had begun at Hesper and worsened under current thrust. Patient Fire could not reach the gate. Osei had known before making the last turn.

"Then eject crew pods toward the relay," Mara said.

"Needle density prevents recovery."

"Pods carry independent drives."

"For twelve minutes. Enemy owns the space for longer."

Osei's voice remained level. Precision often looked like calm when no useful choices remained. Mara remembered keeping the lost names visible at Orison and understood that Osei now required the same refusal of abstraction.

"How many aboard?" Mara asked.

"Eight hundred four. Thirty-seven already dead."

Mara entered the count herself. "Continue telemetry transmission."

"That I can do."

The screen lasted forty-three minutes.

Osei's final two escorts cleared toward the gate with damaged drives. One reached the carrier line. The other lost pressure but continued transmitting from sealed compartments. Patient Fire remained beside the outer relay, turning fifteen Veyr braids through a sequence of short burns and empty launcher flashes.

The convoy crossed behind her.

Nacre's lens restored full cycle after the carrier group attacked from gateward. Transports entered in paired intervals. Some carried Talassan ring sections welded into their cargo bays. Some held people who had spent seven days in the same evacuation couches. Their captains maintained vectors while particle fire brightened the dust belt.

Mara kept the carriers inside the lens defense volume. Every tactical instinct urged her to send Common Measure outward with strike craft and recover Osei. The distance had grown too large. A rescue burn would cross the remaining Veyr prediction lanes and expose the carriers again. Saving one cruiser would hand the enemy the gate, the convoy, and perhaps all three carriers.

The choice had already been made when she sent Osei the lane. Pretending otherwise would only add ships to it.

"Civilian hull two hundred ninety-eight through," Sori said.

Patient Fire lost its second coil lance.

"Two hundred ninety-nine."

A particle beam cut the cruiser's forward third away. The surviving hull rolled, arrested the spin with two thrusters, and continued transmitting.

"Three hundred."

Osei's final packet arrived in fragments. Telemetry filled the wall: Veyr avoidance vectors, relay field emissions, dust density, coil trajectories, timing corrections. Captain Osei had overlaid every enemy maneuver against the region behind the relay. The boundary repeated across thirty-seven separate decisions.

"Three hundred one."

Mara opened the analysis layer. The protected volume moved when the relay's field shifted under damage. Veyr braids adjusted with it. They did not merely avoid the physical installation. They navigated around a geometry human sensors represented only as low-confidence noise.

A blind geometry.

The final transport crossed Nacre's lens.

"All 302 civilian ships confirmed through," Sori said.

At the outer moon, Patient Fire's drive bottle failed. The cruiser expanded into a sphere of white vapor and ceramic fragments. Its transmission ended after the light reached Common Measure, old by several seconds and final before Mara saw it.

Veyr braids turned back toward the gate. Their screen mission had failed, but they still possessed enough force to damage pursuit. Mara ordered no chase. The carriers entered the lens behind the last escorts while Nacre's station prepared to shut down its aperture.

On the far side, rescue tugs met the convoy. Three hundred two civilian ships remained intact enough to answer registry calls. Common Measure had survived with its flight decks, command vault, and more than twenty thousand evacuees. Six escorts and Patient Fire had not.

Mara replayed Osei's packet without reducing it to a casualty attachment. Each enemy turn curved around the same moving absence. The pattern held through damaged sensors, independent braids, and changing approach vectors.

"Give this to every fleet analysis group," she said. "No assumptions about what the region means. I want its dimensions, motion, and relation to their firing predictions."

Aras stood beside the command rail. "Osei bought more than the convoy."

"She bought a question."

Beyond the wall, the rescued ships opened their hatches to medical crews. Mara kept Patient Fire visible on the plot until the last telemetry frame finished copying, a dead cruiser marking the place where the enemy had refused to go.

Chapter 11 — A World in the Magazines

The replacement capacitor arrived with a balcony attached.

Tovan stood under Meridian Yard Six's cargo gantry while workers lowered thirty tonnes of habitat machinery onto a carrier service cradle. The capacitor had once powered a rotating residential arc. Its casing carried mounting lugs for flower beds, drainage pipes, and a section of transparent railing. Someone had cut the balcony from its deck and left two chairs bolted to the outer edge.

"Mass report says twenty-six tonnes," Lio Gan said.

"Mass report forgot architecture."

"Do we send it back?"

Tovan looked through the railing. One chair still had a yellow cushion tied to it. The other held a ceramic cup sealed with decades of paint. "No. Remove the furniture, inspect the bus, and stop believing manifests."

Beyond the gantry, Meridian Hub rotated in layered cylinders. Every yard arm carried damaged ships. Common Measure occupied the central berth with both aft radiator roots opened to vacuum. Kestrel cruisers waited for armor plate. Escorts from Hesper and Nacre floated inside maintenance frames while crews stripped working parts from hulls judged too expensive to repair.

The fleet required one hundred sixty replacement pulse capacitors. Meridian inventory contained forty-three certified units. It needed eighteen thousand carrier missiles and had casings for six thousand. Trained deck crews could replace combat losses only by collapsing instruction courses from twenty months to six weeks.

Supply Command had answered the shortage with a list of civilian machines suitable for conversion.

Habitat mass-drivers occupied the first line. Mining charges filled the second.

Tovan opened the conversion order on his slate. Mass-driver coils could launch dense projectiles, but their timing assumed ore canisters traveling along fixed rails at low acceleration. Carrier launchers threw missiles through short channels while a ship turned, heated, and received enemy fire. Mining explosives could provide shaped thrust or fragmentation, but their detonators expected rock and vacuum, not military command authentication.

The order allocated three days for first production.

"A civilized schedule," Tovan said.

Lio leaned over his shoulder. "Because the fleet leaves in four?"

"Because whoever wrote it has never spent a day installing anything."

A yard supervisor approached in orange coveralls. Nera Voss had run Ring Twelve's maneuver station before Talassar fell apart. She had escaped aboard the transport attached during separation, bringing twenty-seven of her crew and an expression that treated fleet uniforms as a manufacturing defect.

"Your requisition takes all six driver coils," she said.

"It takes four."

"Two failed insulation tests. Your people marked them salvage."

"Then four useful coils and two lessons."

Nera pointed toward the residential capacitor. "You are also taking our power hardware. That unit served Ring Four."

"Ring Four no longer has a bus to connect it to."

Her face tightened. "Parts of Ring Four remain in orbit. People remain inside them."

Tovan knew the latest rescue registry. Tugs had reached six isolated sections before debris closed the approach. Others still transmitted low-power beacons. Meridian's requisition teams had removed material only from evacuated wreckage, but legal ownership did little to change what the parts meant.

"This came from the arc that reached Nacre," he said. "No one stripped Talassar orbit."

"So the fleet saves our people, carries them here, then turns their homes into ammunition."

"The fleet also needs power to keep carrying them."

"You had power when you left the ring."

No answer could make the retreat feel less like abandonment. Tovan had watched Ring Twelve separate under his own command. He could explain strain curves, joint failure, and the number of sections preserved by the cut. Nera had worked in the section he released into unstable orbit.

"Help me make the conversion safe," he said. "Or someone unfamiliar with your coils will do it badly."

"That is not an apology."

"No."

"Do you have one?"

Tovan looked at the balcony hanging above them. "Not one that repairs anything."

Nera considered him, then took the slate from his hand. "Your pulse interval will melt the third winding."

"We will add a cold jacket."

"Your jacket will crack when the carrier turns."

"Then show me where."

She walked toward the machine bay without giving the slate back. Twenty-seven Talassans followed her. Some wore ring-station orange. Others wore borrowed yard gray with old insignia cut away. Their presence solved part of the trained-crew shortage and made every conversion an argument over what the war had permission to consume.

The first improvised launcher failed without firing.

Tovan considered that a promising result. The test rig charged the habitat coil, accepted a simulated combat order, and locked itself out when the civilian insulation monitor detected vibration. Nothing exploded. Nothing crossed the yard. Every safety system had performed exactly the wrong useful action.

"Habitat drivers shut down when bearings move," Nera said. "Residents disliked ore canisters traveling through breakfast tables."

"Carriers move continuously."

"Then your launcher believes it lives through an earthquake."

They opened the control cabinet. Civilian boards filled a space larger than a missile cradle. Their connectors carried labels such as ROTATION COMFORT, QUIET CYCLE, and MUNICIPAL OVERRIDE. Fleet hardware expected hardened timing buses and direct fire-control authentication.

Lio held up a board. "Quiet cycle limits discharge noise."

"Keep it," Tovan said.

"Why?"

"Because a coil that shakes itself apart makes even less noise afterward. Raise its threshold."

They rewired the vibration monitor to distinguish continuous hull acceleration from sudden bearing displacement. Nera wrote the filter from ring-maintenance code. Tovan added a physical cutoff because software written in one exhausted hour deserved a mechanical veto.

The second test launched a steel proof mass.

It left the rail twelve milliseconds late, clipped the sacrificial guide, and crossed the target plane sideways. Yard radar plotted its tumble toward an empty recovery net.

"Combat accuracy," Nera said.

"Only if we aim at a large war."

Timing lag came from the capacitor's civilian charge controller. It protected residential power by smoothing demand over five seconds. A carrier weapon needed full discharge between one maneuver impulse and the next.

Tovan bypassed the smoothing layer. Current climbed too quickly and heated the third winding exactly as Nera had predicted. Their improvised cold jacket held for nine seconds, then split along a weld when the test cradle rolled.

White coolant filled the bay.

Workers sealed masks and shut isolation valves. No one ran. Talassans from the ring had learned to move around pressure loss without spending motion on panic. Fleet technicians followed their signals, and the cloud settled into collection trenches before it reached live buses.

Nera tapped the cracked weld with a gloved finger. "You attached a rigid jacket to a flexing coil."

"I attached the jacket we had."

"You have braided coolant line in Bay Three."

"Reserved for radiator repair."

"Then decide whether you want a launcher or half a radiator."

Common Measure needed both. Its original missile system had lost two launch channels. Its aft radiator root carried replacement metal but no final coolant feed. Every useful component now occupied several future repairs at once.

Tovan opened the yard allocation board. A braided line segment had been assigned to the carrier's lower radiator. Another lay inside the decommissioned escort Open Hand, whose hull would never leave Meridian. Removing it required six hours and a pressure cut through compartments that might still contain unstable propellant.

"We take Open Hand's line," he said.

Lio checked the clock. "That moves our next test past shift end."

"Shift end moved past us yesterday."

Salvage crews entered the escort through a hole cut near its old galley. Tovan went with them because allocation paperwork did not reveal which side of a bulkhead held fire residue. The ship smelled of burned insulation, old coolant, and the sweet solvent used after decompression deaths. Personal straps remained along the corridor. Someone had tied a deck of cards beneath one rail so they would not float away.

The braided line ran behind the aft magazine.

Mining charges from a Talassan quarry had been stacked inside that magazine for conversion. Their yellow casings carried warnings in three coastal dialects. Civilian detonators had been removed, but explosive filler still occupied each cylinder.

"Those should be in Yard Nine," Tovan said.

A salvage chief checked the transfer stamp. "Yard Nine sent them here after its blast doors failed inspection."

"And no one changed the registry."

"Registry changed our location. It did not change their location."

Tovan ordered every cutting tool powered down. The coolant line could wait until the charges moved. A tractor had to tow the racks through a narrow pressure corridor, around the galley breach, and into a certified magazine barge.

Nera arrived with her ring crew before the first rack cleared. "You stopped the launcher rebuild."

"Explosives objected to our schedule."

She inspected the yellow cylinders. "These are shelf-quarry splitters. Their filler fractures under lateral shock."

"Useful in a missile."

"Dangerous in a tractor rack. The separators need pressure pads."

They cut pads from crew mattresses left aboard Open Hand. Each charge received two layers of fabric and one restraint strap. Thirty people spent four hours moving explosives at walking speed through a dead escort while fleet departure clocks continued to count down.

The work carried no drama visible from command. No enemy fired. No hull failed. Yet one dropped cylinder could remove half a yard bay and every specialist inside it. Tovan trusted the slow movement more than any readiness projection.

When the final rack reached the barge, Nera handed him a strip of mattress fabric. A child's pattern of red planets showed along one side.

"Fleet issue?" she asked.

"Crew family berth. Some escorts allow dependents in peacetime transfer."

"So another home."

"Yes."

They used the remaining fabric to line the first prototype warhead.

By the second day, Yard Six could produce twenty-three improvised rounds per shift.

The habitat mass-drivers supplied launch coils. Mining charges supplied explosive filler and fragmentation plates. Carrier guidance packages came from damaged missiles whose motors had burned out. None of the components fit without adapters, altered timing, and handwritten exceptions in the safety registry.

Tovan required every round to carry its source list. A completed missile might include a ring-driver coil from Talassar, a Meridian quarry charge, a guidance wafer recovered at Hesper, and thermal shielding cut from Open Hand. If a batch failed, crews needed to know which civilian tolerance had crossed a military demand.

Supply Command objected to the extra recordkeeping.

"It slows production by eleven percent," an officer told him over video.

"A launcher failure slows it by one hundred."

"The fleet needs volume."

"The fleet needs rounds that leave the ship before exploding."

"Can you certify them?"

"I can certify the tested envelope. I cannot certify every habitat coil ever built."

The officer asked for a single reliability number. Tovan refused and sent a table divided by coil source, insulation age, charge batch, and launch acceleration. Machines inherited the histories of their parts. Hiding those histories inside one percentage made command comfortable and deck crews dead.

Before releasing the batch, Tovan made the new magazine crews practice a jam. Half had worked civilian cargo systems three weeks earlier. They knew hydraulic locks and pressure doors, but none had cleared a live missile from a carrier rail. He loaded an inert round with a deliberately bent guide shoe and watched them follow the fleet checklist until the clock reached four minutes.

"In combat, the next round tries to occupy this channel in eleven seconds," he said.

A former tram mechanic studied the jam. "Then your checklist describes a workshop, not a launcher."

"Correct. Rewrite it."

The crew isolated the capacitor, released the rear restraint, and used a cargo spreader to draw the round backward. Nera stopped them before the warhead collar crossed a live bus. She marked a safe hand position on the rail with Talassan orange paint. Their second attempt took thirty-six seconds. The third took twenty-nine, but one worker crossed the recoil path and failed the drill.

Tovan reset the bent guide shoe. "Again. A fast corpse does not improve launch rate."

By the seventh attempt, the crew cleared the channel in thirty-one seconds without violating the power boundary. Their revised card used six steps instead of nineteen and named the part most likely to trap a glove. Tovan attached it to the launcher beside the official procedure. Training did not create experienced deck crews, but it could prevent inexperience from remaining invisible.

Talassan workers loaded the first production racks aboard Common Measure. Evacuees watched from temporary berths along the service corridor. Some recognized district markings on the coil housings.

A woman blocked the rack tractor. She wore a surface medical vest stained with sealant. "That driver came from Pelagic East."

Nera stepped between her and the tractor. "It came from the freight arc that evacuated to Nacre."

"My brother maintained that line."

"Then he maintained it well."

The woman put one hand on the housing. "You are going to fire our city at them."

Tovan approached without calling security. "Part of your city."

Her gaze moved to his fleet patch. "You left the rest."

"Yes."

"Do you rehearse that answer?"

"No. It does not improve."

People gathered along the corridor. Some wore Talassan ring orange, others hospital green or the plain gray blankets issued after evacuation. Their anger divided in several directions. A man wanted the fleet to attack immediately. Another wanted every carrier assigned to rescue remaining ring sections. Someone demanded the Commonwealth rebuild the sunspindle before spending power on weapons. No position could satisfy the others.

Nera climbed onto the tractor step. "This coil cannot become a home again. The freight arc around it no longer exists. It can stay in a warehouse while more worlds break, or it can launch something at the ships that broke ours. Neither choice restores Pelagic East."

The medical worker looked at her. "Easy to say when you escaped."

Nera's face changed. "My partner did not."

The corridor quieted by degrees.

Tovan did not use the silence to defend the fleet. He opened the rack's source list on a wall display. Every component appeared with its origin, test envelope, and production crew. The Pelagic coil carried the names of nine ring technicians, including Nera.

"If we use these parts," he said, "we keep the record attached. No anonymous salvage. No claim that the fleet made this alone. If a round fails, the record tells us why. If it works, the record tells us what it cost."

The woman removed her hand from the housing. "Put my brother's name on it."

"Did he work this coil?"

"Twelve years. Jalen Ivo."

Nera checked the maintenance archive. The name appeared across forty-seven service entries. She added it to the source list as prior custodian, not weapon builder.

The tractor moved again.

Tovan watched the rack enter Common Measure's magazine. A residential power capacitor charged launch coils. Habitat drivers became weapon channels. Quarry explosives sat behind guidance systems. Talassar had entered the carrier in pieces, but none of those pieces agreed on whether they represented loss, survival, or revenge.

The ceramic fragment arrived in a bin marked ORDINARY IMPACT DEBRIS.

Yard crews had swept it from Common Measure's empty strike-craft bay after Nacre. Most fragments came from Veyr needles shattered by point defense. They ranged from dust to plates the size of a hand. Standard procedure required chemical scanning before disposal because charged ceramics could retain dangerous fields.

Lio ran the bin beneath an induction loop. One piece answered with a weak pulse.

"Residual charge," he said.

"Too regular. Scan again at half frequency."

The fragment lay curved around something black. Its outer surface carried the pale, layered structure of Veyr ceramic. Along the inner curve, heat had fused it to a human object with a cracked antenna and one red indicator.

Tovan recognized the casing before the scan named it.

A rescue beacon.

The beacon came from a carrier escape kit. Its serial number traced to Deck Four, issued during the Talassar evacuation and logged missing after the Nacre splinter strike. Human beacons transmitted pressure, location, and life signs. This one had activated for eleven seconds before an impact silenced it.

"Needle fragment wrapped around it," Lio said. "Chance collision?"

Tovan enlarged the scan. The ceramic did not show an impact channel. Its layers had softened in a controlled thermal gradient and folded around the beacon from three directions. Small cavities remained over the antenna and life-sign sensor. The red indicator had room to shine.

"Needles don't fold after launch," he said.

"Point defense heat?"

"Would soften the outside first. This heated from its inner surface."

Nera joined them at the bench. She rotated the scan, then compared it with images from the splinter damage. "Could it have tried to smother the signal?"

"Then it would cover the antenna."

"Could be collecting it."

"Then why leave the sensor exposed?"

They traced the fragment's recovered path. It had entered the empty bay at low relative speed compared with other debris. The bay fuel line had been severed by a different splinter. This object had drifted through the opening after the main impact, carrying the beacon inside its curve.

Tovan connected power through an isolation transformer. The beacon's memory returned nine seconds of corrupted data. Pressure had fallen. One weak life sign showed inside sensor range. The last two seconds contained a heat source moving around the beacon rather than toward it.

No human rescuer had occupied the empty bay.

"Seal it," Tovan said.

Lio reached for a standard evidence pouch.

"Not that. Field cabinet, vacuum isolation, no cutting. Flag it for xenotechnology review."

Nera looked from the curved ceramic to the magazine racks moving beyond the workshop window. "We spent two days turning homes into weapons. What did they turn this into?"

Tovan did not answer. The scan showed deliberate spacing around the antenna, a ceramic shell shaped closely enough to protect the device from debris without crushing its sensor.

He placed the fused object in the cabinet himself. The door closed around the ceramic fragment and its human rescue beacon. When the vacuum pump started, the beacon's red indicator lit once through the curved opening, as if something unknown had carried a small distress call out of the battle and refused to cover its mouth.

Chapter 12 — The Captured Witness

The ceramic shell clicked again inside the containment cradle.

Tovan Rhee held his breath and watched a hairline seam travel around the black fragment. It moved against every clamp he had set, not through force but through a sequence of microscopic contractions. Three days earlier, the object had come out of a slagged magazine cradle fused around a human rescue beacon. Now it lay in a sealed lab aboard Common Measure, opening itself under lights that gave off less heat than Tovan's hand.

"Back the clamps off two microns," he told Lio at the remote bench. "If it thinks we're crushing it, I'd rather not learn how it objects."

Lio made the adjustment through the manipulator field. The cradle's force trace flattened. Around them, transparent shielding divided the lab into six pressure cells, each with its own vent path into a sacrificial tank. Tovan had insisted on the arrangement after Fleet Intelligence suggested cutting the fragment open with a mining laser. Intelligence had withdrawn the suggestion only when he asked which occupied deck they wanted to expose to alien vapor.

A rescued beacon sat beyond the inner cell, its housing stripped to bare alloy. The device had transmitted for nineteen hours in the debris wake off Meridian's yards before recovery crews found it. Human serial marks survived on one face. On the other, Veyr ceramic had flowed into every mounting groove without melting the beacon beneath.

Sanaa Veyl stood at the observation console with both palms resting on its edge. She wore a sealed field coat over shipboard gray, an unnecessary precaution that Tovan respected. "The contraction follows a repeating interval," she said. "Forty-one pulses, pause, then thirty-seven."

"A lock code?"

"Or breathing."

"I'd prefer a lock."

Sanaa looked across the shielding. "Living systems often prefer otherwise."

The seam completed its circle. No pressure alarm sounded. Four sections of ceramic loosened and folded outward like stiff petals, revealing an oval structure nested around the beacon's transmitter coil. It measured half a meter across and carried nine dark ridges beneath a translucent surface. Heat moved through those ridges in staggered waves.

Tovan checked the cell atmosphere. Nitrogen unchanged. No detectable organics. No ionizing source above background. The object consumed current from the beacon through contacts too fine for the lab camera to resolve.

"It kept the beacon alive," Lio said.

"Maybe it kept itself alive with the beacon." Tovan enlarged the power history. "Either way, it built a cleaner regulator than ours."

One of the nine ridges brightened amber. A corresponding change appeared on the lab's thermal array, too structured to dismiss as cooling. Tovan routed the pattern into an isolated optical buffer. He refused every automatic translation package the ship offered. Nothing from the fragment would touch Common Measure's network while he retained authority over the lab.

Sanaa leaned toward the display. "Run the last six seconds again."

He did. Thermal points climbed across the oval in bands, converged around the center, then separated into three repeating lobes. The pattern resembled neither text nor machine status. Each lobe carried internal variation, dozens of temperatures layered across fractions of a degree.

"Could be a diagram," Tovan said.

"It could be anatomy."

That answer made him study the object rather than its display. The ceramic shell carried no surfaces meant for human hands. Its ridges touched one another through narrow bridges. When a bridge warmed, both adjacent ridges altered their pulse interval. Machine, organ, archive, crew: human categories found no clean purchase.

Captain Aras spoke over the lab circuit. "Engineering, report."

Tovan keyed his throat pickup. "The fragment opened without explosive decompression, poison, or immediate philosophical insight. We found an active object drawing twelve watts from the rescue beacon."

"Can it communicate?"

"Ask me after we agree what counts as communication."

Sanaa pointed at the pattern. "Tell him we may have a witness core."

The term came from partial Veyr telemetry captured at Hesper, where human analysts had assigned names to repeated structures without understanding their use. Every destroyed braid left three vessel-strands and one denser ceramic center. That center often transmitted after the surrounding craft lost power. Fleet reports called it a witness core because it appeared to preserve tactical records. No intact example had survived human recovery.

Tovan passed that much to Aras. The captain remained quiet for a moment.

"Admiral Venn wants every result on a physically carried wafer," Aras said. "No live link. She also wants the core left functional if possible."

"Those two instructions agree with each other. That's unsettling."

"Enjoy the novelty. Aras out."

The lab pumps changed pitch as Lio isolated another pressure cell. Tovan watched frost grow along the sacrificial tank feed. A ship could make any discovery feel like plumbing if one stood near the right bulkhead. He preferred that version. Plumbing admitted limits, and limits could be measured.

Sanaa had already divided the thermal stream into intervals. "These long fields repeat with variation. The short fields recur unchanged."

"Header and payload?"

"Possibly. Give me the Hesper braid temperatures from Fleet Archive, but keep them air-gapped."

Lio carried the requested wafer through two isolation drawers. Tovan loaded it into a reader with no transmitter hardware. The display filled with reconstructed thermal emissions gathered during the battle. Most showed weapon cycling, drive load, and damage. One narrow set came from the braid that had altered course near a hospital ship before rejoining the attack.

At the time, analysts had treated the maneuver as a targeting error. The hospital ship had crossed a kill corridor while carrying four thousand wounded from Lantern Nine and the Hesper stations. A Veyr firing solution opened, held for six seconds, then shifted away. The delay had let the ship clear.

Sanaa aligned those emissions against the core's warm ridges. Three correspondences appeared at once.

"It's not replaying sensor data," she said. "Look at the transforms."

Tovan followed her markers. The core's pattern placed the hospital ship inside a larger structure of temperatures. Human hull heat appeared as a compact bright knot. Around it spread cooler curves representing Veyr weapon channels. A third layer registered intervals between predicted contact and observed survival.

"It recorded the miss," he said.

"It recorded a choice."

"That's a larger claim."

"Then call it a divergence from the firing prediction. The record distinguishes intended geometry from the geometry that occurred."

A small current spike crossed the core. One translucent ridge clouded, then cleared. Tovan reduced optical intensity by eight percent. The spike vanished.

"It notices us noticing it," Lio said.

"It notices heat," Tovan replied. "Let's avoid promoting it before lunch."

They worked without a network for four hours. Every dataset crossed the isolation drawers on numbered wafers. Lio tracked power and cell pressure. Tovan built interfaces from passive thermocouples, glass fiber, and components scavenged from a decommissioned strike-craft sight. Sanaa compared the records against spectra from human stars, Veyr routes, and the ruined sunspindles at Caer Sola, Ninth Meridian, and Orison.

Before trusting any interpretation, Sanaa made the core fail three tests. She fed it a simulated magnetic map built from random turbulence. The ridges warmed once, found no continuity, and settled. Next came a human habitat weather model scaled until its vortices resembled plasma cells. That drew a corrective pulse from two ridges but no linked response from the other seven. Last, she presented a century of ordinary solar observations with every timestamp reversed. The core rejected the sequence in less than a second.

"It tracks causality," she said.

"It tracks ordering. Causality charges extra."

She accepted the correction with a nod. Tovan liked her better each time she let a smaller claim survive. They tested the boundary another way. He built a crude physical emulator from nine thermoelectric plates under the inner cell and drove each plate with one component of the recovered pattern. The core answered only when heat could pass among the plates. Isolated sequences produced nothing. Coupled behavior drew a rich field of corrections.

That result gave Sanaa a constraint she could defend. The record did not merely catalog nine contributors. It expected interaction among them over time. When Tovan disabled one plate, the remaining eight altered their rhythms but preserved part of the composite pattern. Restoring the ninth did not reset the system. The returning input had to negotiate its place through dozens of exchanges.

"Development without a fixed template," Sanaa said. "Contribution without ownership."

Tovan watched the plates exchange heat. "And damage without a clean rollback."

"Yes. Whatever these records describe, history matters to it."

Lio shifted his chair away from the shielding. Until then, he had treated the object as hazardous equipment. The emulator changed his posture. "Could a core contain one of them?"

"The scale doesn't support that," Sanaa said. "These field structures span stellar regions. The core carries records and constituent patterns, not an entire developing organism."

"Good," Lio said, though nothing about his expression suggested relief.

A maintenance alarm interrupted them from the passage outside. Common Measure had shifted radiator load again, and the lab temperature rose by four tenths of a degree before the local system compensated. Every ridge in the core brightened at the change. Tovan held the ship's heat report beside the alien response and saw nine separate corrections settle toward a common limit. None reacted at the same speed. None remained independent. The pattern endured because its parts disagreed in useful ways. He logged the observation without naming what it implied about a Veyr person. Sanaa added a note that environmental stress increased exchange among constituent records, then marked the claim provisional.

Tovan shut down the emulator plates one at a time. Residual heat continued moving after power vanished, blurring their clean inputs into a shared cooling curve. He saved that curve with the other tests. If Fleet analysts wanted to call the Veyr machinery a mindless coordination system, they would have to explain why its archive cared so much about how separate contributions changed one another.

The core responded most strongly to stellar magnetic maps. Its nine ridges brightened in linked groups whenever Sanaa presented a young active star. Older stable stars drew less activity. Engineered magnetic-loop geometries produced rapid corrections that looked almost like alarm.

Near the fifth hour, a map opened inside the thermal stream.

It did not arrive as an image. Sanaa reconstructed it by treating temperature gradients as boundaries rather than intensities. Curves formed around several stellar surfaces, each nested across different timescales. The shortest cycles lasted seconds. The longest implied centuries of field stability. Within the curves, small coherent knots split, joined, and altered their internal rhythm.

Tovan studied the reconstruction. "Those aren't ship tracks."

"No."

"Weather?"

"Life cycle."

Sanaa enlarged one cluster until its layered movement covered the display. "These structures conserve pattern while exchanging material with the surrounding plasma. They differentiate. They recombine. See the nine inputs along this edge? None controls the result, but every one contributes a stable component."

"You can tell that from heat?"

"From heat, field behavior, repetition, and the fact that the core keeps correcting our stellar model toward this arrangement. Life leaves constraints. We recognize carbon life because we know which constraints to ask about. This uses a star for the medium, but the evidence still has to close."

The words stellar cradles appeared in her typed notes. Tovan read them twice. The phrase sounded poetic in a way the data did not. On the screen, every curve had a duration, tolerance, and failure boundary.

He selected the magnetic-loop overlay from Orison's extinguished sunspindle. "Put our infrastructure onto the map."

Sanaa hesitated. "The coordinate assumptions may not match."

"Neither did the connector standards. We survived those."

She transformed the human loop geometry into the core's thermal coordinates. For several seconds, nothing aligned. Then the object pulsed through all nine ridges and supplied a new boundary set.

Human sunspindle loops appeared outside the coherent knots. Their normal operating envelope remained distant from the life-cycle structures, but the margins narrowed during startup, emergency venting, and high-output extraction. A separate band represented an extreme condition that no civilian spindle would enter by accident.

Tovan traced it with one finger above the glass. "What happens at that boundary?"

"Loss of magnetic coherence across the development region."

"Temporary?"

"The map carries no recovery sequence."

He thought of Orison going dark from the outside in, not because its people had been conquered but because their machines had placed them inside an alien calculation. A spindle powered gates, foundries, habitat heat, and the industries that made a system more than a world under one sun. The Veyr had struck those machines with the concentration of firefighters cutting fuel away from a blaze.

That did not excuse the dead. Understanding a weapon's target did not reduce the force applied through schools, hospitals, or crowded rings. Yet the discovery changed the shape of the war. The Veyr had not come to possess human systems. They had come to prevent a condition.

Sanaa opened a second reconstruction. "There are multiple sites."

Nine thermal signatures occupied different stellar maps. Some showed dense coherent structures. Others carried sparse early patterns. Each site linked to groups matching the ridges within the core.

"Lineages?" Tovan asked.

"Contributors, perhaps. I don't know their social meaning. Biologically, no single input owns the developing pattern."

"So don't write parent."

"I wasn't going to."

"Half the Fleet Archive will by tomorrow."

She met his eyes. "Then give them better nouns before the bad ones harden."

Tovan looked at the oval object drawing power from a rescue beacon. Witness core had started as an analyst's convenience. Stellar cradles had started as Sanaa's attempt to describe a physical environment where coherence could grow into life. Both terms would travel faster than their cautions. He had spent his career watching provisional repairs become permanent because they worked once. Language failed the same way.

They returned to the Hesper record. Sanaa separated the hospital ship sequence from the life-cycle maps, but the core joined them again whenever both files remained active. It placed the human vessel, its damaged drives, and its medical emissions beside a forecast marker. Six seconds of delay opened between prediction and fire. Then another structure appeared, nine linked curves tightening around the divergence.

"Custody record," Sanaa said.

"Explain without guessing."

"The core preserved who contributed to the decision, what prediction they received, what happened, and whether the strands remained in agreement afterward. I can't tell whether it approves. I can tell the survival mattered enough to retain."

Lio magnified a thin temperature scar running through one ridge. "Damage?"

Tovan compared it to the ceramic fracture. The scar aligned with the fragment fused around the beacon. A vessel-strand had broken near the core. During the impact, molten human alloy had wrapped the beacon coil against the ceramic. The core had then reshaped its shell around the only working transmitter it could reach.

"It didn't preserve our beacon," Tovan said. "It repaired itself around a power source."

Sanaa shook her head. "Look at the outgoing buffer."

A compressed copy of the hospital record sat in a transmission queue. The core had attempted to send it seventeen times. Each attempt included the human beacon identifier and the six-second divergence.

"All right," he said. "It repaired itself around a power source and kept the receipt."

"Better."

Tovan prepared Mara's wafer. He included raw thermal records, Sanaa's reconstruction method, uncertainty labels, and a short engineering note on the core's interfaces. He omitted nothing merely because it invited political misuse. Selective telemetry had already killed ships under obsolete orders. Mara needed the physical truth, including the extreme sunspindle boundary, though he placed that finding behind three separate caution screens.

His summary began with the least disputable claim: the Veyr object recorded coherent structures inside stellar magnetic environments and treated those structures as developing life. The next section tied the record to attacks on human spindles. The final section documented the spared hospital ship and its exact delay.

Sanaa read over his shoulder. "Say 'supports' rather than 'proves' for the reproductive interpretation."

"The core has nine life-cycle inputs and centuries of development maps."

"Evidence can prove that the maps exist. Interpretation still needs alternatives."

He changed the verb. "You enjoy removing the useful parts of sentences."

"I remove the parts most likely to become orders."

Tovan signed the engineering record. Sanaa signed the biological analysis. Lio sealed the wafer inside a transparent carrier, then walked it toward the lab hatch for hand delivery to command.

The witness core brightened before he reached it.

All nine ridges contracted in unison. The rescue beacon drew its maximum rated current. An alarm bloomed across Tovan's isolated console as the core assembled a signal through the beacon transmitter.

"Cut power," Lio said.

"Wait." Tovan checked the carrier network. Physical isolation remained intact. The beacon could reach only the shielded receiver three meters away. "Record first."

A narrow burst crossed the inner cell. It repeated after forty-one pulses, paused, then returned after thirty-seven. The same intervals had opened the shell. This time the sequence carried the core's location, its damaged-strand map, and the custody divergence from Hesper.

Sanaa's face lost color. "That's a distress pulse."
