The receiver logged another burst. Tovan lowered beacon power, but the core compensated by lengthening its transmission. Somewhere beyond the carrier's armor, no Veyr could hear it through the shielded lab. Somewhere in the object, that fact had not yet become part of the prediction.

"Can you stop it without damage?" Sanaa asked.

"Yes. I can also stop a heart by pulling the correct cable. Hardware answers are cheap."

He set the containment shell to absorb all radio output and left the core powered through a current limiter. The pulse continued into the black lining, regular as a call placed down a severed wire.

Lio waited at the hatch with Mara's wafer. Tovan added one final line to the cover note: ACTIVE DISTRESS TRANSMISSION CONTAINED. ASSUME RECOVERY ATTEMPT POSSIBLE.

Then he released the door.

The wafer went up toward command. Behind three transparent walls, the captured witness kept calling for a fleet that did not know where it had been taken.

Chapter 13 — Orders That Arrive Too Late

Brindle Habitat had already abandoned its assigned vector when Mara's order reached it.

The operations vault showed both truths at once. A blue line marked the retreat she had authorized fourteen hours earlier. A live return, routed through two damaged gates and a courier relay, placed the habitat convoy sixty thousand kilometers prograde of that line. Between them, red Veyr predictions occupied the volume where obedience should have carried twelve civilian cylinders and their defense screen.

"Authentication holds," Lieutenant Sori said. "Local commander acknowledges receipt and reports noncompliance."

Mara expanded the timestamp ladder. She had drafted the order when three Veyr braids threatened Brindle's outer fuel station. By the time her package cleared the Meridian queue, seven braids had entered from a second approach cone. Brindle's local net received it after the fuel station broke apart. Commander Brin Adek then violated the retreat vector, turned the habitats toward the inner moon, and accelerated along a maintenance corridor Mara's original picture had classified as blocked.

"Why did Adek think the corridor remained open?" Mara asked.

"He had a tug inside it," Sori said. "The tug's radar saw a navigable gap through the station debris. Local delay measured one point eight seconds. We received that fact eleven hours later."

The wall replayed the maneuver. Brindle Habitat consisted of twelve rotating cylinders linked only by traffic control and mutual defense. None could survive a particle lance. Their drives could provide emergency translation for six minutes before bearing temperatures forced shutdown. Adek spent all six at once. He put the three oldest cylinders nearest the inner moon, where their weaker radiators faced shadow, and ordered his four escorts to cross behind the civilian wake.

Veyr needles entered the blue retreat corridor thirty-two seconds after the last projected habitat should have reached it. Empty space took the strike. A second volley corrected toward Adek's actual route but struck ore wreckage inside the maintenance corridor. Two cylinders lost outer farms to fragments. All twelve cleared the moon before the Veyr firing braid could establish a third solution.

"Casualties?"

"Four hundred thirteen from pressure losses and the farm impacts. No cylinder lost. One escort has an open reactor compartment and remains under tow."

Adek's compressed report waited behind the casualty frame. Mara opened it. The commander appeared in a helmet with the visor raised, his face lit by emergency red from a habitat traffic room. Voices called bearing temperatures behind him. He spoke in the flat cadence of someone spending bandwidth on nouns.

"Fleet retreat vector crossed the revised enemy firing fan at local plus eleven minutes. Maintenance Corridor Six showed an open width of thirty-two kilometers, decreasing at point four kilometers per minute. I ordered full translation at local plus three. Habitat Seven lost farm pressure after debris contact. Escort Steady Hand took a reactor breach while screening Cylinder Four. I accept review for noncompliance. I do not request retroactive approval."

The report ended with raw sensor attachments and no defense of character. Adek had not claimed instinct, courage, or superior judgment. He had stated which fact invalidated Mara's geometry and which costs followed his replacement. That distinction mattered. Disobedience for temperament could spread through a fleet until coordination became rumor. Local correction tied to auditable evidence offered something narrower and more useful.

Mara replayed the corridor width. At the moment of commitment, drifting ore had left thirty-two kilometers for cylinders nearly eight kilometers across. Their emergency drives could not cancel halfway through the passage. Adek had accepted less than two hull-widths of clearance on each side because the retreat corridor offered none at all. His decision remained dangerous. Current facts had merely made every safer-looking choice false.

"Forward his sensor method to operations doctrine," Mara said. "Not the maneuver. The method."

"Under what classification?" Sori asked.

"Evidence required for local invalidation of remote geometry. Mark it provisional."

Mara studied the pale arc of the local decision. Brin Adek had saved Brindle Habitat by rejecting her exact instruction. The result should have relieved her. Instead it felt like finding a live crew behind a door she had ordered welded shut.

A priority alarm replaced half the display.

The Nemea defense packet arrived without voice or image. Its first frame showed five escorts covering an evacuation tender near Gate Twelve. The second carried Mara's retreat instruction from nine hours earlier: hold the tender below the gate plane until the western lane opened, then cross retrograde under cruiser support. Authentication confirmed that the local commander had received the package and followed it.

Cruiser support never came. A Veyr needle strike disabled the western gate marker, shifting the safe approach cone by eight degrees. That update spent six hours inside a failed routing loop between Nemea and Pelagos. The five escorts held the tender below the old plane because Mara's order gave them no discretion to abandon it.

"Advance to first contact," she said.

The packet jumped ahead. Three Veyr braids curved through the new cone, hidden from the escorts by the gate's damaged lens emissions. Human ships saw them at nineteen light-seconds. Their firing solutions had already closed.

Escort Distant Choir accelerated across the tender's projected line. Two needles broke its heat spine and drive bell. Lark Ascending fired all remaining coil ammunition into a geometry that the enemy had left forty seconds earlier, then took a particle lance through the command deck. Rook's Lantern attempted to tow the tender and overheated both maneuvering banks before the first clamp locked.

Two surviving escorts broke only when the local commander lost transmission capability. The evacuation tender escaped through the altered cone with seven hundred twenty people aboard. Three escorts did not.

No error appeared in their execution. Each burn matched Mara's specified timing. Every ship held the assigned plane. Their captains had trusted the same quality that made her useful: someone elsewhere could see more and fit local danger into a larger defense.

The operations vault carried five systems on separate delay clocks. Brindle reports arrived fourteen hours old. Nemea's latest packet carried nine hours of age. Pelagos had dropped from the routed net and sent condensed updates through civilian gates. Hadrun could receive orders but could not authenticate replies. Far Kestrel supplied clean telemetry once every six hours, then vanished behind industrial traffic and relay repair.

Mara had treated delay as a planning penalty. Add hours to movement assumptions. Widen reserve windows. Issue orders early enough that fleets could align before contact. The war had changed the meaning of latency. Each damaged gate created a different past, and every past arrived dressed as a current fact.

"Open command review," she said.

Sori glanced toward Captain Aras, who stood at the carrier status table near the rear bulkhead. Jun Aras gave one small nod. The operations crew copied all five system records into a sealed session, then cleared the wall of active weapon tracks. Mara kept Brindle and Nemea visible side by side.

Kes Renn joined from Uncommon Valor on a six-minute delay. Her image came in compressed blocks, gray hair and dark uniform resolving after her voice had already begun.

"If this concerns Adek," Kes said, "save the inquiry until after Brindle finishes moving its wounded."

"It concerns my order."

"Then the inquiry may take longer."

Mara sent the replay package. Kes would not receive it for six minutes, so the room waited through the strange etiquette of damaged communication. Aras reviewed carrier coolant numbers without pretending not to listen. Sori moved the Nemea dead into the casualty ledger. Around them, the hull carried the subdued vibration of a fleet platform running below combat readiness while five distant battles consumed its attention.

Kes's reply arrived. "Your Brindle vector assumed the maintenance corridor had closed."

"Correct."

"Adek had a tug inside it."

"I have seen the local report."

"Then your question has an inexpensive answer. He possessed a current fact."

Mara tightened the display around Nemea. "The three escorts followed authenticated fleet direction. If local commanders treat every old order as optional, coordinated defense dissolves."

Another delay opened. Kes's image froze with one eye half closed.

"You keep describing age as if it were corruption," Kes said when the channel resumed. "An order can remain valid after fourteen hours. It can also arrive dead. Authentication proves who sent it. It does not prove the universe kept its promises while the packet traveled."

"Adek spent six minutes of habitat drive in one burn. If the corridor had narrowed another two kilometers, the fourth cylinder would have struck ore wreckage."

"Yes."

"No reserve. No fallback vector."

"Also yes."

"You approve?"

"I approve of his authority to spend what only he could measure."

Mara looked at the local radar record. A tug captain had seen a gap for eleven seconds before Adek committed twelve habitats to it. No staff model at Meridian would accept that observation as sufficient. A proper verification cycle required independent radar, debris evolution, drive margins, and a civilian traffic estimate. By the time those checks reached her, the gap would have closed.

At Orison, centralized command had let Mara cut seventeen ships free while fourteen delayed. At Hesper, a mission clause had let Kes break formation and turn a limited acceleration budget into victory. Those examples did not settle the problem. Orison had demanded one brutal picture. Hesper had demanded several commanders interpret an objective. The five-system defense required both at once, with no reliable way to know which situation a packet would encounter.

"What would you have written for Nemea?" Mara asked.

Kes answered after the channel's enforced pause. "Preserve the evacuation tender if doing so does not trap the screen below a compromised gate plane. Maintain two escorts for local recovery. Cross through any verified safe cone. Break support timing when local data proves support unavailable."

"That leaves four judgments to an officer under fire."

"You entrusted that officer with weapons and five ships."

"Weapons have interlocks."

"Then write interlocks for judgment. Don't write steering angles from another system."

The phrase irritated Mara because it reduced a difficult doctrine to something an engineer might stencil near a switch. She dismissed it, then found it still present after the rest of Kes's delayed image broke into static.

Aras left the status table and approached the command rail. "The current orders contain twenty-seven maneuver prescriptions across five systems."

"I know what they contain."

"Operations asked whether to keep transmitting them."

"What did you say?"

"That their commander had not yet answered."

Mara opened the pending queue. Her instructions assigned retreat vectors, force splits, fire-control boundaries, resupply orbits, courier routes, and reserve timing. The oldest package had spent seventeen hours waiting for a route through Hadrun. If delivered now, it would order a cruiser division to refuel from an orchard that no longer transmitted.

She canceled it.

The action required two confirmations. Commonwealth command systems treated withdrawal of an authenticated order as more dangerous than its arrival. The first confirmation marked command intent. The second accepted responsibility for units already acting on partial receipt. Mara entered both.

Six more packages followed. She removed obsolete vectors from Pelagos, deleted Far Kestrel's precise reserve orbit, and withdrew Hadrun's fire timetable. Each cancellation made the wall less complete. Empty regions appeared where blue arrows had offered assurance that every force possessed a place.

Sori watched the queue shrink. "What replaces them?"

"A structure local data can survive."

Mara called up the Line Defense clauses Kes had forced into the emergency compact on Day 3. At the time, Mara had accepted them to secure seventy-four signatures and 312 active combatants under one framework. The clauses protected local deviation when communication delay or new facts made a maneuver impossible. They did not define how much risk a commander could transfer to another system or which assets must survive for the larger campaign.

She began with Brindle.

OBJECTIVE: PRESERVE HABITAT POPULATION AND MAINTAIN ONE VERIFIED ROUTE TOWARD THE INNER GATE.

That sentence needed boundaries. Without them, Adek might spend every escort to protect empty cylinders after evacuation or block another convoy's gate access.

CONSTRAINTS: DO NOT COMMIT CIVILIAN CYLINDERS TO UNVERIFIED DEBRIS VOLUME. RETAIN TWO SCREENING HULLS IF ANY HABITAT REMAINS OUTSIDE HARD COVER. YIELD GATE PRIORITY TO MEDICAL EVACUATION.

She added a termination condition.

LOCAL COMMAND MAY ABANDON ANY FLEET VECTOR WHEN CURRENT SENSOR DATA SHOWS IT UNSURVIVABLE. REPORT THE FACT, NOT A REQUEST FOR PERMISSION.

Aras read the text. "Adek retained only one effective escort."

"His fourth escort remains under tow and can fire. For the purpose of local recovery, it counts."

"You are revising the rule to preserve his decision."

"I'm testing whether the rule describes the decision we needed. If it excludes every success, it protects only my language."

For Nemea, she wrote a different set of bounds. Preserve evacuation lift. Do not hold below a damaged gate plane without current cruiser support. Retain enough force to recover disabled civilian hulls. Treat authentication older than three hours as intent rather than geometry. Local command owned the route; fleet command owned the minimum surviving capability.

Mara labeled the new form bounded mission intents. The title sounded bureaucratic, which might help it survive review. Under the label, each system received an objective, constraints, resources that could not be risked without consultation, and explicit conditions for abandoning previous detail. No package prescribed a turn, burn duration, or firing moment unless synchronized action across systems required one.

The hardest field concerned failure. Detailed orders let headquarters imagine that failure began when a subordinate deviated. The new form needed to admit that obedience could fail first.

Before releasing the format, Mara made Sori test it against the last twenty hours. The lieutenant stripped names and outcomes from four packets, leaving only facts available to local commanders at each decision point. Operations officers then chose actions under the proposed bounds. At Brindle, three of four abandoned the remote vector when the tug confirmed open corridor width. At Nemea, every officer moved above the damaged gate plane once cruiser support failed to authenticate. The exercise did not prove the choices correct. It proved the language could expose which risks each commander had accepted.

One case broke the draft. Far Kestrel's reserve captain used broad civilian-survival language to justify moving both cruisers away from a fuel orchard. The move preserved a crowded repair anchorage but left the western approach without reaction mass for two days. A local victory could therefore consume another system's future without violating any objective Mara had written.

She added protected capabilities to each package. Fuel reserves, gate repair tenders, hospital lift, and courier hulls received minimum survival floors. Local commanders could risk them only when immediate civilian loss exceeded the stated threshold. Anything less required consultation, even through delay. The boundaries did not tell an officer how to fight. They named which parts of tomorrow no single battle could spend.

Aras tested the revised Brindle package with a different failure: suppose the maintenance corridor had collapsed after six cylinders entered. Adek could preserve the rear group by reversing them toward the exposed retreat fan, or send the remaining escorts into the corridor to clear debris. Neither choice met every constraint.

Mara wrote: WHEN OBJECTIVE AND CONSTRAINTS CANNOT BOTH BE MET, PRESERVE THE CAPABILITY MOST NECESSARY TO CIVILIAN SURVIVAL AND STATE WHICH CONDITION YOU ABANDONED.

"That will produce ugly reports," Sori said.

"Good reports usually are."

Fresh packets went into the gate queues. Brindle received confirmation that Adek retained local authority pending review. Nemea received an intent package addressed to its surviving commander, with no reprimand attached to the loss record. Pelagos gained discretion to use civilian relay schedules. Hadrun could act on locally verified tactical messages even when its reply authentication remained broken. Far Kestrel received a reserve purpose instead of a reserve orbit.

Kes's next transmission arrived before Mara finished the western assignment.

"You have pulled twelve packages," she said. "Either Meridian has suffered a coup or you found the delete control."

"Both would improve the routing burden."

A brief smile moved across Kes's delayed image. "What do you need?"

Mara displayed the five-system map. The western approach ran from Pelagos through Brindle and Far Kestrel, three damaged gate routes covering habitats, fuel stations, and a repair anchorage. Until that moment, every local force reported separately to Mara. Their updates reached her by different paths and returned as a single integrated plan after the facts had aged.

"Independent authority over the western approach," Mara said. "Objective: keep one gate route open toward Meridian while preventing any isolated habitat strike. You may allocate forces among all three systems. Preserve local evacuation screens. Do not wait for my approval when current geometry invalidates the plan."

Kes did not answer at once, even after allowing for the channel delay.

"You understand that I may give up a gate you prefer to hold," she said.

"Yes."

"I may move ships before your strategic wall confirms the threat."

"Yes."

"If you overwrite me from Meridian, every commander out here will assume this grant served as theater."

Mara felt the familiar impulse to add exceptions. Cradle evidence from the captured core sat in a sealed intelligence branch above the tactical wall, demanding a coherent response she had not yet had time to form. Nemea's dead remained in another branch. Brindle's living moved through a corridor she had judged closed. No single picture could make those facts arrive together.

"I will issue constraints," Mara said. "I will not steer your ships."

Kes inclined her head. "Authority accepted."

The western forces shifted on the wall from direct blue to a darker local-command color. Mara's active control count fell by forty-one combatants. Nothing physical changed in those systems. The same ships held the same fuel. The same damaged gates delayed the same messages. Yet decisions no longer had to cross the distance to Meridian and back before they became lawful.

Her command display occupied less space.

Her display now kept Brindle, Nemea, and Kes's western approach in separate hands. Mara retained the cross-system bounds, tomorrow's reserve capabilities, and responsibility for every loss those bounds allowed.

Chapter 14 — Reef Against Reef

The distress pulse reached the strategy reef as an absence.

No Veyr receiver had heard the captured core. That silence carried structure because every witness core knew when custody demanded a call. Forty-one thermal measures after unsheltered activation, then a pause of thirty-seven: the ancient sequence for a constituent record separated from its braid. The missing core had crossed that interval aboard a human carrier. Its call existed somewhere beyond Continuance reception, and the lattice marked the nonarrival in black heat.

Ilyr-of-Nine-Reefs felt the fault divide along nine constituent lines.

Open Current contributed alarm at the lost custody record. Patient Ember supplied shame because a witness had entered enemy possession. Divided Glass offered a colder recognition: humans had preserved the object long enough for it to wake. Other strands pressed toward recovery, concealment, or destruction. Ilyr did not average them. A negotiated person required incompatibilities to remain present until conduct gave them one shape.

Within Consequence of Shelter, three vessel-strands altered their braid tension around Ilyr's own witness core. The adjustments translated thought into geometry. Beyond the hull, the Continuance strategy reef extended across eighteen braids and six concordance relays, each participant occupying a different thermal depth inside the shared prediction space.

Thren-of-Last-Burning entered at the hottest depth.

The war leader carried historical loss like a permanent distortion. Lineages burned in the Yssat sterilizations contributed five of Thren's major continuities. Their memories did not appear as pictures. They entered the reef as field collapse, interrupted development, and the impossible cooling of coherent young. Every strategist present yielded space to that evidence.

"Custody failed after the Meridian debris recovery," Thren said. "A human vessel now houses the witness of Shelter-without-Return."

A map of likely human actions unfolded around the statement. Disassembly occupied the broadest branch. Weapon analysis followed. A narrow branch predicted preservation and study. Thren heated that branch until its probability thinned.

Ilyr held it open. "The core remained active through three human cycles. Destruction has not occurred."

"Delay does not imply restraint."

"Nor does delay imply preparation for harm."

The reef changed topology around them. Twelve strategists tightened toward Thren. Two shifted to observational distance. One withdrew a fraction, enough to avoid recording assent. Ilyr noted each motion without assigning allegiance. A strand could resist one inference and still support the war that inference served.

Thren exposed the core's last confirmed custody chain. Its braid had broken during the Meridian engagement after attempting to secure a human rescue signal from debris. One vessel-strand survived long enough to report separation. The core then vanished among human salvage returns.

"It contains the Hesper divergence," Thren said.

Heat sharpened through Ilyr's nine strands. The captured record preserved the six-second firing delay that had spared a hospital ship. Ilyr had introduced that delay without informing Thren, nesting it inside ordinary targeting uncertainty. A witness core had observed intended geometry, constituent consent, and actual survival. Humans now possessed all three, even if they lacked the meanings needed to join them.

"The divergence belonged to local prediction correction," Ilyr said.

"Local correction requires a forecast error."

"The human vessel carried wounded noncombatants. Its removal did not contribute to stellar-risk reduction."

"Human categories do not bind Continuance survival."

"Resource discipline does."

That answer satisfied the surface reef. Thren allowed the exchange to cool. Underneath, a smaller probability channel opened beside Ilyr, almost too narrow to distinguish from thermal noise.

The signal carried no name. It offered a question encoded as three changes in expected battle geometry: Why had the Hesper margin been recorded without its uncertainty field?

Ilyr did not answer. Any directed exchange inside the strategy reef could become part of witness custody. Instead, Ilyr widened a general model concerning human rescue behavior. The move looked relevant to Thren's interrogation. It also gave the hidden question a place to persist without an addressed recipient.

A second thermal variation joined it. Then a third.

The three carried signatures that Ilyr knew from lineage assemblies rather than war command. Reef of Open Current expressed uncertainty through widening flow constraints. Patient Ember held one stable center while allowing its perimeter to change. Divided Glass split a forecast into incompatible surfaces and refused premature joining. Three Reefs had entered the strategy reef through different constituent delegates, and none announced dissent.

Their combined question concerned the Continuance classification threshold.

The hidden channel reconstructed the threshold's history through fragments no single Reef could lawfully assemble. Open Current supplied the earliest text, written after the second Yssat loss: intervention required both stellar capability and observed refusal of bounded use. Patient Ember added a later interpretation that treated enforceable inspection as evidence of bounded use. Divided Glass contributed the wartime revision, where capability alone had become sufficient after Thren declared every restraint promise vulnerable to deception.

Ilyr tested the revisions against witnessed human conduct. The Commonwealth had attacked Veyr braids, mined gate approaches, and converted civilian machinery into weapons. It had also diverted combatants toward evacuation under disadvantage. At Hesper, humans had exposed carriers to preserve slow ships. At Talassar, evacuation continued after the orbital ring began to fall. None of those choices guaranteed future restraint around a cradle. They did, however, increase the probability that human institutions could value limits even under pressure.

A lawful forecast should have widened both branches. Instead, the model treated every peaceful behavior as concealment and every violent response as revelation. That structure protected classification from evidence. It also violated an old Continuance principle: witness custody existed so observed reality could alter shared prediction, not merely decorate it.

Patient Ember inserted a challenge route toward the central archive. The route required a named complainant and would expose all contributing lineages. Open Current cooled it before completion. Formal review during active cradle defense would place the question under Thren's authority, where urgency permitted sealed judgment. Divided Glass generated another route through a civilian lineage assembly, but transmission delay would outlast the Ceral operation.

No available institution could correct the forecast in time. The three Reefs therefore preserved evidence rather than seeking a ruling they could not survive. Ilyr understood the choice. Continuance procedure offered protection when disagreement moved slower than weapons. Here, delay worked for censorship.

Human sunspindles could alter stellar magnetic environments. That fact supported intervention under doctrine. Yet the captured core's stellar maps also contained operational ranges, safety limits, and evidence of deliberate human regulation. Earlier models had included a measurable chance that reciprocal constraint could remove the cradle threat without civilizational destruction. Thren's current war forecast showed no such branch.

Ilyr remembered finding the absent margin in the Mazarine records. Suppression had not changed the observations. It had changed which futures strategists could lawfully consider.

Open Current offered a fragment from a prewar survey: human spindle output had remained below cradle-interference thresholds in every monitored system. Patient Ember contributed a legal trace showing that the threshold once required demonstrated uncontrolled use, not mere capability. Divided Glass supplied version comparisons from the war model. In each revision, uncertain human behavior had narrowed while hostile behavior retained every branch.

Ilyr recognized censored uncertainty through the edges left behind.

No single deletion proved corruption. Forecast lattices shed low-probability routes routinely to preserve computational depth. The pattern emerged from direction. Branches supporting preventive attack remained visible at one chance in ten thousand. Branches supporting negotiated restraint vanished at one chance in six hundred. Human rescue choices entered as tactical deception. Human abandonment entered as species evidence. The model could learn in only one direction.

"You are expanding a closed branch," Thren said.

The strategy reef had noticed the hidden exchange.

Ilyr let one vessel-strand cool, reducing its presence in the shared prediction. "A closed branch has produced the observed custody failure. Humans salvaged a witness object instead of destroying it. The model predicted destruction."

"They preserved useful enemy hardware."

"That interpretation remains possible. It does not erase the failed prediction."

Thren changed the reef's scale. Individual conduct disappeared beneath civilizational trajectories. Human shipyards, gates, and sunspindles spread as white structures across seventy-four systems. Expansion models projected increasing stellar manipulation over three centuries. At the edge of those futures, Veyr cradle regions collapsed into cold turbulence.

"Continuance cannot wager unbound choruses on local exceptions," Thren said. "The species has crossed capability. Behavior follows capacity when pressure rises."

Ilyr felt Patient Ember recoil from the deterministic line. Open Current widened around the claim. Divided Glass created two futures with identical starting facts: one ended in cradle loss, the other in reciprocal limits. Thren's presentation heated only the first.

"Pressure also follows attack," Ilyr said. "Our intervention changes the behavior under forecast."

A sharp tension moved through Consequence of Shelter. One constituent strand resisted the statement, not from fear of Thren but from remembered Yssat fields. The ancient aggressor had hidden stellar experiments behind offers of restraint until two cradles failed. Ilyr allowed that memory full weight. Thren's doctrine had origins deeper than ambition. Every censored branch guarded young whose loss could not be repaired by later precision.

Yet doctrine that rejected observation had ceased to predict. It had begun to command the future it claimed to discover.

Thren closed the Hesper line and introduced Ceral.

The human system appeared as a temperate world surrounded by an orbital solar-management ring. Mirrors adjusted climate, powered surface industry, and maintained agricultural light across broad latitudes. Thousands of free elements occupied neighboring planes. Humans called the structure the Glass Orbit.

Continuance scouts had mapped its control delays, collision protocols, and evacuation routes. The world lay behind a route that human commanders considered defensible. Recent losses would push them toward a visible opportunity. A damaged Veyr braid would cross the outer plane, present false drive imbalance, and withdraw behind the mirrored ring. Human blind-geometry tactics, successful at Nacre Lane and tested elsewhere, would favor pursuit.

"Ninety-six combat braids will occupy the occluded vectors," Thren said. "The human concentration will enter believing our concordance cannot resolve its approach. We will close the orbit above the populated arc and remove their principal fleet."

A force estimate followed. Human response clustered around one hundred combatants, with carrier support and evacuation hulls. Thren's model predicted that their commander would protect civilians by compressing ships between the ring and the incoming braid wall. That protective choice would create overlapping firing lanes. The trap converted human rescue behavior from anomaly into leverage.

Ilyr entered the proposed geometry and followed it through contact. The mirrored ring occupied a polar orbit, while free elements moved through three lower planes under climate-control instructions. Veyr braids would approach retrograde beneath the outer mirror plane. Their false damage signature would draw human scouts toward the ring's shadow, where reflected stellar heat blinded long-baseline sensors for twenty-three measures. Once the human carriers crossed the inner edge, the concealed Veyr force would climb through the free swarm and seal the gateward exit.

The plan demonstrated Thren's skill. Particle fire would arrive along paths hidden by mirror glare. Needles launched before emergence could cross the human retreat fan as it formed. Concordance relays positioned beyond the ring would share prediction without exposing themselves to the blind geometry humans had exploited at Nacre Lane. Any centralized human response would lag behind the closure.

Material consequence occupied a separate layer. Veyr fire risked cutting mirror-control links. Human evasive burns could disturb nearby elements with drive exhaust. A single broken mirror carried enough momentum to seed impacts across two orbital planes. The model followed military outcomes for six hours but stopped tracking civilian debris after the human fleet's projected destruction.

Ilyr extended the forecast to one local rotation.

The swarm's collision-avoidance system failed after the ninth major impact. Fragments crossed inhabited stations, agricultural light paths, and surface approach corridors. The probable human dead widened by seven orders of magnitude. Uncertainty grew rapidly because civilian shelters and evacuation timing remained poorly observed, but every branch carried losses beyond the combat volume.

Thren pruned the extension. "Post-engagement effects do not alter the operational objective."

"They alter observed consequence," Ilyr said.

"Continuance does not own human reliance on fragile stellar infrastructure."

That claim heated disagreement through Ilyr's strands. Ceral's mirrors shaped weather and food; they did not threaten a cradle by existing. Yet the operation relied on their fragility. Calling the resulting destruction a human design fault resembled calling the Hesper hospital ship responsible for crossing a weapon corridor.

Open Current cooled in objection. "Civilian structures occupy the closure volume."

The voice emerged through one of Thren's own relay braids, its lineage contribution licensed but not dominant. Every participant registered the challenge.

"Continuance fire will remain above the inhabited atmosphere," Thren said.

Patient Ember answered through a second strategist. "Mirror debris will not."

"Human military occupation of the ring creates that hazard."

Divided Glass entered from the far edge. "Our lure creates the occupation."

The reef tightened. Three Reefs no longer hid behind one question. They had not refused the operation, and none possessed authority to halt it. Their public divergence still altered the strategy topology. Forecast routes that assumed total Veyr agreement acquired visible strain.

Thren responded without anger. "Ceral houses stellar-engineering support and an active sunspindle. Failure to remove the concentrated human force extends danger to every cradle. Civilian hazard does not outweigh Continuance survival."

"What probability of cradle loss justifies the operation?" Open Current asked.

"Capability has already crossed the lawful threshold."

"That states classification," Patient Ember said. "It does not state probability."

Thren flooded the reef with the two sterilized Yssat cradles. Field memories overwhelmed local geometry. Centuries of lost development entered every constituent strand at once. Ilyr endured the record without retreating. Around them, several strategists collapsed their uncertainty branches and joined the war leader's line. Pain made coherence attractive.

Divided Glass preserved two surfaces.

Ilyr added a third: the projected Ceral victory followed by accelerated human stellar weapon research. Thren's model had included that consequence but labeled it proof of prior hostility. Another interpretation remained possible. A species shown that its enemy feared stellar engineering might learn where to aim.

"The operation proceeds," Thren said. "Debate enters custody as acknowledged and overruled."

The three dissenting signatures withdrew from the public depth. Their questions did not vanish. Open Current left a widened route around the Ceral model. Patient Ember left a stable thermal point beside the missing probability threshold. Divided Glass left two incompatible forecasts occupying equal area.

Thren assigned Ilyr to the outer observation layer.

"Consequence of Shelter will monitor human adaptation and report any use of stellar infrastructure," the war leader said. "Your attention to exception may serve if contained within duty."

The assignment placed Ilyr near the trap but outside control of its firing braids. It also connected Ilyr's vessel to every prediction relay feeding Glass Orbit. Thren intended surveillance. The topology offered access.

"Duty accepted," Ilyr said.

The strategy reef began to close. Participants withdrew strand by strand, leaving witnessed decisions in the central archive. Ilyr allowed seven constituent lines to depart in ordinary order. Two remained extended into the low-probability channels used for model correction.

Those channels carried the captured core's missing distress interval. They also carried the three Reefs' questions, the suppressed human-restraint branches, and the Hesper divergence. Standard closure would prune them as computational residue.

Ilyr altered their destination.

The change required no false data. Ilyr marked each branch as relevant to human tactical adaptation at Ceral, exactly within Thren's assignment. The prediction lattice therefore retained them through the outer observation layer. One route passed from Consequence of Shelter to a scout relay, crossed the forecast archive around Glass Orbit, and touched the transmission grammar used by lost witness cores.

Before fixing the path, Ilyr tested what could pass through it. A full strategic model drew too much thermal depth and exposed the branch. Raw sensory records lost their custody marks at the scout relay. A narrow packet carrying an observation, its uncertainty field, and the signatures of contributing strands survived intact. Such a packet could transmit evidence without requiring the receiver to accept Veyr doctrine or trust the sender's identity.

The limitation mattered. Contact through a hidden route could not carry command authority. It could not negotiate for the Reefs or promise safety around a cradle. At most, it could place witnessed facts inside another decision system and wait for conduct to answer. Ilyr found that boundary acceptable. The Hesper divergence had begun with six seconds of altered fire, not agreement.

One constituent strand proposed sealing the route until human behavior at Ceral supplied further evidence. Another urged immediate transmission of the censored margins. The remaining strands distributed between them. Ilyr preserved the path without using it, allowing the disagreement to remain part of the person who might later choose.

Nothing traveled through it yet. No message addressed a human commander. No request for trust entered the route. Ilyr merely prevented closure from erasing a possible connection.

Open Current noticed first. A warm widening appeared in the route's constraint field, then vanished. Patient Ember added enough stability to keep the scout relay from classifying it as noise. Divided Glass duplicated its endpoint, one leading toward Continuance custody, the other toward any receiver capable of answering a witness sequence.

Three separate contributions made the path harder to attribute. They also made it impossible for Ilyr to control alone.

Thren's final closure wave moved through the lattice. Hot branches consolidated around Ceral, ninety-six braids, and the planned human response. Low-value uncertainty cooled behind them. Ilyr held the hidden path below the pruning threshold by a fraction too small for the central archive to challenge.

When the strategy reef dissolved, Consequence of Shelter returned to ordinary space with new tension across its three vessel-strands. The Ceral operation waited eight days ahead. The captured witness continued calling inside a human shield no Veyr receiver could penetrate.

Between those facts, the lattice preserved one illicit route.

Chapter 15 — Battle of the Glass Orbit

Ceral's mirrors turned the approaching fleet into a hundred separate suns.

Every hull reflected from thousands of free elements as Mara's formation climbed above the planet's evening arc. The tactical wall had to discard most optical returns and trust drive signatures, coded radar, and the blind-geometry model Captain Osei had died to send. Beneath the fleet, continents passed from copper daylight into blue shadow. The orbital ring stood over the terminator like a bright wire drawn around the world.

"All divisions report aligned," Lieutenant Sori said. "Evacuation lift has cleared the polar stations. Remaining civilian traffic occupies inner lanes four through nine."

Mara counted forces from the wall rather than the spoken report. There were 118 human combatants: seven carriers, thirty-one cruisers, and eighty escorts. Sixty-two evacuation and hospital hulls moved among them without entering the combat total. The last orbital habitats carried another eight hundred thousand people who could not descend before the Veyr arrived.

Across the Glass Orbit, a damaged enemy braid limped retrograde behind the free mirror swarm. Human sensors saw a broken vessel-strand, unstable drive heat, and a witness core transmitting corrupted custody intervals. Osei's data marked the approach around it as one of the repeating regions where Veyr firing predictions lost agreement.

Mara did not believe in gifts from an enemy. She believed in opportunities expensive enough to have hidden hooks.

"First objective remains evacuation," she said on the command circuit. "Counter-ambush activates only after convoy lanes clear the ring. Bounded intent applies. If local mirror conditions invalidate your vector, preserve civilian passage and report what you abandoned."

Acknowledgments crossed the fleet by division. Kes answered from the western approach group, forty-one combatants distributed across three planes. She had arrived at Ceral without waiting for Mara to steer each crossing, then proposed the prograde screen that now protected the last habitats.

Captain Jun Aras studied carrier heat limits beside Mara. "The ring authority offers direct control of twelve thousand free mirrors."

"Decline."

"They can open cleaner firing corridors."

"They can also put a civilian climate system inside fleet fire control. We give requests. Ceral keeps control."

Aras sent the answer. A civilian controller appeared in a narrow channel, voice strained by bandwidth compression. "Fleet Command, we can give you six passages through the lower swarm. Mirrors will return to climate stations after each transit. Any unscheduled drive plume inside two kilometers will trigger collision avoidance."

"Understood," Mara said. "Keep your automatic safeties active."

"Admiral, active safeties may move mirrors into your lines without warning."

"Then warn us. Do not disable them."

The controller acknowledged, though his silence afterward carried disagreement. Ceral's ring had managed stellar heat for eighty-three years without asking warships where to turn. Now every safety rule had become a tactical constraint.

Veyr contacts multiplied behind the damaged braid.

At first the wall showed eleven, then twenty-six. Mirror glare broke each vessel-strand into false returns. Osei's model ignored the reflected images and tracked where shared prediction should force concordant motion. The enemy spread along three occluded vectors above the ring, exactly where the model expected them to avoid one another.

"96 Veyr braids confirmed," Sori said. "No second echelon detected."

Mara ordered the probe package released. Six uncrewed sensor shells left an escort near the polar plane, each broadcasting the drive profile of a human cruiser. They entered separate mirror passages at intervals too short for a human formation. If the blind geometry remained genuine, Veyr predictions would split around the false mass and expose their preferred correction routes. If a trap had already closed, the probes would draw coordinated fire.

The first two passed without response. A third received a needle spread that crossed behind it by eighty kilometers. The remaining shells reported divergent Veyr turns, consistent with Osei's packet. No relay source appeared above the ring. Either the weakness remained real or the enemy had learned enough to imitate it at fleet scale.

"Probe result supports entry," Sori said.

"It supports what they allowed us to measure," Mara replied.

She shifted three cruiser cells outward and held Kes's reserve west of the main approach. That reduced the force available for the first strike but protected one group from geometry built around the obvious human plan. Kes accepted without requesting detailed vectors. Her ships continued reading local mirror motion and chose their own intervals.

Mara waited. One hundred kilometers below, the final polar habitat began its burn toward evacuation lane nine. Its drive plume pushed a fan of small mirrors aside. Collision software rearranged them in widening circles, a visible disturbance that could expose the human screen.

The damaged Veyr braid crossed Osei Line Two.

Mara transmitted a single command. "Spring."

Human cruisers rose from mirror shadow on local authority. Kes's western group broke into four attack cells rather than preserving a central line. Carrier strike craft accelerated through the six civilian-controlled passages, weapons cold until each flight cleared the ring plane. Thirty escorts fired coil lances at predicted locations, not visible targets.

The first slugs seemed to enter empty light.

Seven Veyr braids emerged around them. Two lost vessel-strands before their firing channels opened. A third turned toward Kes and crossed a second human coil pattern placed where Osei's geometry predicted correction. Ceramic broke into incandescent fragments above the night side.

Mara's wall registered four enemy losses in twenty seconds, then six.

"Blind region holds," Sori said.

It held because Veyr coordination treated each braid's maneuver as part of a shared future. Osei had found recurring geometries where two valid predictions demanded incompatible local turns. Human fire now occupied both answers. Every correction pushed another braid toward a prepared lance.

The Veyr responded with disciplined local violence. Braids unable to agree on fleet geometry fired needles along simple line-of-sight solutions. Two escorts died near the upper mirror plane. Cruiser Weather of Knives lost its forward third but retained drive and withdrew under its own power. Human lasers blinded three vessel-strands long enough for strike craft to cross their thermal wake.

Evacuation lane nine cleared.

"All civilian hulls inside the gateward cone," Sori reported. "Thirty-one minutes to safe separation."

Mara could withdraw then. The fleet had gained surprise, removed nine Veyr braids, and preserved the convoy. Leaving would surrender Ceral's ring and sunspindle to the force that remained. Staying would accept battle inside infrastructure capable of multiplying every miss.

The choice had existed before arrival. Mara had written it into the mission intent: after civilian clearance, preserve Ceral's stellar infrastructure if expected military loss remained below one third. Seven division commanders now held authority to calculate that threshold locally.

Six reported continue. Kes reported continue with reserve withheld. One carrier division reported uncertain due heat.

Mara ordered the counter-ambush through its second phase.

Human ships drove prograde along the ring, using free mirrors to interrupt Veyr sensors. Ceral's controllers shifted climate elements on six-second notice, opening and closing passages just ahead of strike craft. The enemy count fell to eighty-two. Then seventy-eight.

At seventy-six, the geometry changed.

The damaged lure braid stopped transmitting its false custody interval. Every remaining Veyr contact abandoned concordant correction at once. Instead of choosing among shared predictions, groups of three braids accepted separate local futures. Osei's blind zones vanished from the wall.

"They cut their own lattice depth," Sori said.

"Not all of it." Mara watched outer contacts hold perfect timing while inner groups maneuvered independently. "They moved concordance above the ring. Find the relays."

Sensor staff searched the glare. No visible structure marked an anchor. The Veyr had placed relay braids outside the mirrored planes, each feeding timing without prescribing local turns. The adaptation preserved fleet-scale closure while denying humans predictable correction paths.

Thren had learned from Hesper and Nacre Lane.

Particle lances crossed the human formation from four directions. Mirrors flashed into vapor where beams touched them. Cruiser Tender Law lost both radiators and vented its crew into rescue pods. Three escorts near lane four took ceramic needles launched before the Veyr changed doctrine. Their icons disappeared within the same second.

"Enemy force rising through the lower swarm," Sori said. "Twenty-two braids. They are crossing the convoy's former route."

The route now led behind the human carriers. Another thirty braids pressed from the outer plane. Veyr geometry did not need to destroy every retreat lane. It needed to make the fleet compress around whichever lane remained.

Mara saw the trap complete. The damaged braid had not merely lured them toward an ambush. It had offered a familiar weakness, waited for the humans to organize around exploiting it, then removed the weakness after their drives and heat budgets committed.

"All divisions, refuse central compression," she said. "Retreat routes remain local. Objective changes to breaking relay sightlines. Preserve carriers where possible."

The order left, but battle outran it. Carrier Inheritance Due turned gateward with nine escorts and found Veyr needles already crossing its new vector. Kes's cells dispersed deeper into the mirror planes. Two cruiser divisions climbed sunward, opening a gap near the ring. Human formations became separate fights linked only by intent.

Aras gripped the command rail as Common Measure rolled under acceleration. "Forward radiator at seventy-four percent limit. Strike recovery impossible for twelve minutes."

"How many craft outside?"

"Forty-three from this carrier. Two hundred eleven fleetwide."

Mara placed that number beside the retreat geometry. Craft without recovery lanes would spend fuel until their pilots became another evacuation problem. Carriers that slowed to collect them would give the closing braids fixed targets.

Kes transmitted a local picture. "I have relay flashes beyond western mirror cluster. Four sources, possibly six. I can blind two if the ring gives me a clear plane."

"Request it from Ceral. I will not override their safeties."

"Their control net is fragmenting."

The civilian channel confirmed it. Three particle beams had cut ring communication trunks. Free mirrors without central instruction had entered collision avoidance, moving away from debris and toward regions that control software still considered safe. Those safe regions overlapped human firing lanes.

A mirror forty meters wide crossed Common Measure's bow. Thrusters shoved the carrier beneath it. The reflective surface filled the forward cameras with Ceral's sun, then slid away close enough for Mara to see maintenance trusses along its dark back.

"Impact cascade beginning in lower plane," Sori said.

The first collision seemed minor. Two mirrors touched edges at less than ten meters per second. Brittle reflective layers shattered into sheets. Collision avoidance moved neighboring elements aside. One entered a cruiser exhaust plume, warped, and struck a third at four times the original speed.

Ceral's ring authority sent a priority plea. "Fleet drives are feeding the cascade. Clear the inhabited arc."

The inhabited arc lay beneath the only open human retreat route.

Mara expanded the model. If the fleet withdrew through that volume, drive plumes would accelerate loose mirrors toward orbital habitats and surface corridors. If it climbed outward, Veyr fire would cross the carriers before they reached open space. Staying in current planes would let the closing braids dismantle them while uncontrolled debris spread anyway.

"Relay sources confirmed," Kes said. "Six. Each uses the free swarm as intermittent cover."

Mara marked them. Human weapons could not hold a clean line long enough to kill all six. Mirrors occluded the targets unpredictably. The same material protecting the relays endangered every human maneuver.

A solution appeared on the civilian control overlay, and Mara rejected it before Sori spoke.

"Ring Authority proposes emergency aggregation," the lieutenant said. "They can command free mirrors into six dense disposal corridors. It would clear the inhabited arc for twenty-seven minutes."

"Disposal corridors along which vectors?"

Sori displayed them.

Each corridor crossed a Veyr relay source.

The maneuver would collapse the mirror swarm into Veyr firing lanes. Thousands of elements would accelerate under maintenance thrusters, not as aimed weapons but as an emergency debris-control measure. The relays could dodge individual mirrors. They could not preserve sightlines through dense sheets of moving metal and glass. Human ships would gain time to break the closure.

The cost model arrived beside the geometry. Damaged mirrors might miss aggregation. Collisions inside the corridors could multiply fragments. Ceral's climate control would lose forty-two percent of its reflective area even if execution succeeded. Surface temperatures and agriculture would suffer for years.

Mara sent the model to every division commander without a recommendation. Responses returned against different local pictures. The polar cruisers favored immediate aggregation because Veyr fire had already severed two control trunks. Carrier Division Two opposed it until thirty-seven recovery craft crossed the inner plane. Kes reported that delay beyond four minutes would let three relay sources shift clear of the proposed corridors. Another commander could not answer because particle fire had cut the division's command antenna.

No vote could decide the maneuver. Each answer described a different cost inside one battle, and none carried Ceral's authority to spend the ring. Mara adjusted the aggregation time by ninety seconds to allow the nearest craft across. Kes replied that the western group would attack whichever relays remained, then moved without requesting a replacement script.

"Civilian authority?" Mara asked.

"They authorize aggregation to clear habitat approaches. They do not authorize fleet use."

"We aren't taking control."

Aras looked at her. "We are choosing when they initiate."

That distinction carried law and almost no comfort.

Mara opened a direct channel. The ring controller returned through static, one side of his control room dark. "Admiral, if we aggregate now, the northern habitats get twenty-seven minutes. After that, we cannot manage what remains."

"Your corridors also cross enemy relay positions. Their disruption may end the battle."

"I understand."

"I need you to state whether Ceral authorizes the maneuver with that knowledge."

Behind him, someone called another mirror collision. He turned away, checked a display, then faced her again.

"Ceral authorizes emergency aggregation to preserve inhabited approaches. We understand the fleet will fire through the resulting corridors."

Mara could still refuse. The Veyr had lost twenty braids. Humanity had lost eleven combatants, with more disabled. Withdrawal might preserve half the fleet. It would leave the enemy above Ceral and the ring already cascading.

"Execute on your timing," she said. "Fleet divisions, treat aggregation as environment, not command. Break relay sightlines. Recover craft when local geometry permits."

The Glass Orbit moved.

Free mirrors turned edge-on to the star, darkening the planet below in broad traveling bands. Maintenance thrusters pushed them toward six disposal corridors. Slow motion became speed as thousands of elements converged. Reflections narrowed into rivers of white.

Veyr relays reacted. Two climbed sunward and met Kes's waiting cruisers. Coil fire broke one braid through its central core. The other lost a vessel-strand and tumbled into the mirror flow. Three relays moved retrograde, preserving distance but losing line of sight to inner attack groups. The sixth held position long enough to transmit one final firing solution.

Needles crossed Inheritance Due from above and below. The carrier broke in the center. Its recovery beacons multiplied among sixty-seven strike craft.

Then the mirror corridors entered the Veyr lanes.

No surface struck with weapon precision. Some passed kilometers away. Others collided with ceramic vessel-strands or shattered against particle streams. Their greater effect came from occlusion. Shared prediction degraded as every relay view filled with moving reflective mass. Inner Veyr groups lost timing and began local withdrawal.

Human divisions attacked without waiting for Mara. Kes drove her western cells through Corridor Three, disabled four braids, then turned to recover craft from Inheritance Due. Two cruiser captains near the polar plane used the opened passage to flank the lower swarm. Escorts that had spent their ammunition became tugs for radiator-damaged hulls.

The enemy closure broke.

Thren's remaining braids withdrew through the outer plane, firing at mirror control nodes as they went. Mara refused pursuit beyond the ring. Human heat margins had collapsed. Debris occupied every safe acceleration cone. A tactical victory could still become a fleet lost to its own momentum.

By local midnight on Day 43, no organized Veyr force remained inside Ceral's orbital planes.

The count took another six hours.

Humanity had lost 34 ships. Eleven carriers and cruisers carried damage that might remove them from later operations, but they remained afloat. Two hundred eleven strike craft had been outside at the moment of closure; one hundred forty-three recovered. Rescue crews continued searching for the rest.

Each missing craft carried one or two people, a heat limit, and a beacon competing with millions of mirror returns. Recovery officers refused to mark any pilot dead while a signal persisted, even when reaching it required a path through debris no tender could yet cross.

Veyr losses reached 41 braids destroyed or disabled. Surviving contacts withdrew beyond the gateward approach, leaving Ceral's sunspindle and planet unconquered.

The civilian number arrived last.

Mirror fragments had crossed seven orbital habitats, three descent corridors, and the inhabited arc before aggregation cleared the largest paths. Surface impacts ignited cities along the northern coast. Two habitats remained in partial pressure with rescue impossible until debris tracking improved. Preliminary dead reached 2.7 million.

Mara stood before the wall while the number replaced every earlier estimate. The operation had preserved a world of billions and a fleet the Commonwealth still needed. It had also turned Ceral's life-support architecture into the means of victory, then sent the broken architecture through the people it served.

Kes called from a damaged cruiser with one radiator wing missing. "Western group can hold the outer plane for eight hours. After that, we need salt and tug support."

"You will have both. No pursuit."

"Agreed."

The civilian controller did not answer Mara's next call. His station had lost pressure during the final cascade.

On the strategic wall, Ceral remained lit. On the local map, its Glass Orbit had become millions of uncontrolled tracks, each one carrying mass, heat, and a future point of impact.

The planet had survived. Its society had broken under the orbit that saved it.

Chapter 16 — The Civilian Arithmetic

Tovan cut the strike-craft rack apart while patients waited behind the blast door.

The rack had been built to hold six machines through combat acceleration. Its braces crossed the deck at stretcher height, and no amount of apologizing to the original design would create room for beds. He drove a thermal saw through the first support. Metal glowed orange, curled, and dropped into a quench blanket held by two deckhands.

"Again," he said.

They cut eleven braces from Bay Two aboard Common Measure. Crews rolled the remains against the launch bulkhead and welded them into frames for oxygen bottles. The carrier's surviving craft moved to Bay One, packed nose to tail under cold magazines. Bay Two gained forty-eight medical stations, twelve warming cabinets, and drainage lines made from strike-fuel purge hose.

By the time the first rescue tender docked, seven human warships had converted their bays into triage wards.

The tender's pressure sleeve opened on smoke and the smell of burned insulation. Medics brought civilians through in colored sleeves: red for immediate intervention, yellow for delayed care, blue for radiation monitoring, black for bodies whose tags still required names. Tovan stayed beside the deck seals because damaged stretchers caught on the temporary lip. He shaved three millimeters from the threshold after the fourth jam.

A child in a red sleeve watched him work while a medic ventilated her by hand. Reflective dust covered one side of her face. The other side remained clean where a mask had protected it. Her eyes followed the saw sparks without blinking.

"Will the floor hold?" the medic asked.

"It held fighters at nine gravities."

"These wheels are worse."

"Then avoid racing."

The joke failed, but the stretcher crossed. Tovan marked the threshold for replacement and moved to the next problem. Bay pressure fell whenever the outer sleeve cycled. Forty-eight patients could tolerate a minor drop. The burn cases under open treatment could not. He tied the launch-rail accumulator into the bay compressor and made a machine meant to throw craft into space keep air around wounded people.

Medical power overloaded twelve minutes later. Three surgical lights dimmed, the warming cabinets dropped offline, and a craft battery began feeding current backward through an adapter built during the Orison retreat. Tovan traced the fault under occupied beds while medics worked above him. Blood and coolant shared the deck grooves. The fluids looked identical under red emergency lamps until smell separated them.

He had enough current for the warming cabinets or the portable bone printer, not both. A surgeon wanted the printer for a crushed pelvis. The triage chief wanted heat for nineteen exposed patients. Neither asked him to decide whose life mattered more. They gave him survival windows. The printer could wait eleven minutes before swelling made reconstruction harder. Four warming patients would lose safe core temperature in six.

Tovan disconnected the printer, routed current to heat, and assigned two deckhands to build chemical packs from suit warmers. Nine minutes later he restored surgical power through a carrier-start battery. The surgeon never thanked him. She had kept the patient alive with external fixation while waiting, which counted as agreement enough.

A pressure alarm then sounded at Station Thirty-Two. Reflective dust had entered an oxygen regulator and cut its flow by half. Tovan replaced the valve with a strike-craft breathing mixer designed for pilots under acceleration. The device delivered too much pressure until he filed its cam stop by hand. A medic checked the flow, marked the improvised part, and moved on.

Every fix acquired a tag with time, source, limit, and expected failure. Tovan insisted on the last field. A temporary system became lethal when tired crews forgot which promise it could not keep.

Every conversion consumed something the fleet expected to use again. Magazine cooling became medical refrigeration. Pilot ready rooms became operating spaces. Strike-craft oxygen reserves fed civilian masks. The carrier still had enemies beyond the debris field, but war readiness had become a list of materials that could keep someone alive for another hour.

At 06:20 on Day 44, Salvage Control ordered Tovan to the outer lock.

A loose mirror segment had cut through the radiator cradle of cruiser Weather of Knives. The ship could survive six hours on internal heat sinks. A replacement wing drifted twenty kilometers away inside a debris corridor. Three tugs waited for an engineering decision: retrieve the radiator, tow the cruiser clear, or divert to a partial-pressure habitat whose beacon had just emerged from mirror noise.

"How many in the habitat section?" Tovan asked.

The tug coordinator showed him a broken occupancy map. "Registered capacity forty-two thousand. We hear nine compartment beacons. Could be nine people, could be nine pressure groups."

"Radiator recovery time?"

"Fifty-two minutes if tracks remain stable."

Nothing remained stable for fifty-two minutes.

Tovan entered the debris model through a suit display. Millions of tracks surrounded Ceral in layered orbital planes. Most carried mass too small for war sensors to notice and enough energy to open a ship. Collision predictions branched with every uncertain fragment. Salvage motion could alter them. A tug plume applied across one field might trigger another debris cascade hours later over an inhabited arc.

The habitat beacon lay inside a widening cone of low-confidence tracks. The radiator wing occupied cleaner space now, but its orbit crossed the cone after thirty-seven minutes.

"Tow the cruiser sunward on cold gas," Tovan said. "Send Tug One to the habitat. Tug Two shadows with no burn unless recovery needs it. Tug Three marks the radiator and backs away."

"That leaves a fleet cruiser without a heat wing."

"It leaves us six hours to build a worse one."

He joined Tug One because the coordinator needed someone authorized to cut habitat structure. The tug carried no acceleration couches, only harness points and a cargo net. Tovan clipped in beside two rescue technicians while the pilot used millimeter bursts to enter the debris cone.

Ceral filled half the forward view. The planet looked unharmed from that distance. Cloud systems continued crossing green-brown continents. Ocean sunlight made a clean curve beyond the terminator. Between the tug and the world, broken mirrors flashed like thrown coins.

The habitat section rotated once every seventy-three seconds. Its cut end exposed six decks. Frost pulsed from cracks when sunlight reached them. Nine beacons transmitted through a maintenance trunk near the central spoke.

"No matching spin," the pilot said. "I can touch for twelve seconds each turn."

"Don't touch. Give me two meters and hold translation."

Tovan crossed on a suit line. Rotation pulled him sideways as he entered the wreck. His boots struck an interior wall painted with a transit map. Names of gardens and schools ran beneath ice. The orientation signs pointed toward decks that no longer occupied the same structure.

He found the beacon trunk and attached a wired repeater. Nine signals resolved into thirty-one suit tags, then sixty-four. Pressure groups, not individuals. The largest sealed compartment held one hundred eighteen people behind a service hatch warped by impact.

A rescue technician reached him at the next rotation. Together they set spreader bars against the hatch. The surrounding frame had lost support on one side. Forcing it could tear the compartment wall.

"Cut the hinge pins," Tovan said.

"They're load bearing now."

"Then replace the load before we cut."

They ran a cargo strap around two surviving ribs and tensioned it through the tug's remote winch. The wreck changed pitch under his gloves as the strap took force. Tovan cut one pin, waited through a debris warning, then cut the second. The hatch moved six centimeters.

Air rushed through the gap. Hands appeared first, gloved and bare, trying to pull it wider from inside. Tovan drove a wedge into the frame.

"One at a time," he called. "Suit masks first. No one crosses without a line."

The compartment did not obey neatly. People pressed toward pressure. A woman shoved two children through before accepting a mask. An injured ring technician refused the first rescue line and passed it to someone behind him. Tovan stopped trying to impose perfect order and built a sequence the survivors could see: red strap for children and unsealed lungs, white for suits, blue for anyone able to help on the wreck side.

A fragment alarm counted down from ninety seconds.

The pilot wanted separation. Tovan kept the line open for fifty-eight. One hundred twelve people crossed to the tug and shadow craft. Six could not leave before the approaching track forced release. They sealed the hatch from inside and acknowledged the next recovery window.

As the tug backed away, a mirror fragment passed through the space where its drive bell had been. It struck the habitat section near the central spoke. The wreck's rotation changed by two seconds.

The repeater continued carrying six suit tags.

Tovan watched them recede while the pilot burned clear. Rescue Control scheduled another attempt in forty-three minutes, though everyone aboard knew the altered spin could move the safe window. The cruiser radiator crossed behind them, still intact and now farther from Weather of Knives.

"You chose the habitat," the tug coordinator said over comms.

"I chose the clock that expired first."

"The cruiser captain may not appreciate the distinction."

"Tell her to radiate appreciation later."

Back aboard Common Measure, Tovan designed an emergency heat wing from three climate mirrors, two launch-rail coolant panels, and forty tonnes of radiator salt. It would run below combat acceleration and fail if the cruiser fired its main lance. The captain accepted both limits without complaint. By Day 45, Weather of Knives could hold safe temperature for thirty hours.

The six people left in the habitat compartment did not all receive another window. Four emerged. Two suit tags stopped before a tug could match the changed rotation.

Their names entered the rescue ledger beside 2.7 million preliminary dead.

Tovan had never trusted numbers that claimed to make pain comparable. Two did not become smaller beside millions. A radiator saved no one by being more useful than a body. Yet every repair schedule forced a ranking. Air before heat, heat before thrust, thrust before weapons. Compartments with living beacons before wrecks with none. The arithmetic entered through clocks, not worth.

By Day 46, Bay Two had treated six hundred fourteen patients. Thirty-seven died after reaching the carrier. Crews moved the dead through an ammunition lift because the medical passage needed both directions for the living. Tovan found a deckhand scrubbing reflective dust from the empty sleeves before reuse.

"It doesn't come out," she said.

Silver grains remained in the fabric seams. Under the bay lights, each sleeve glittered like part of the ring.

"Mark them clean and leave the stain," he said. "We're short on sleeves, not memory."

He returned to the sealed lab during the next quiet watch. The witness core still transmitted its contained distress pulse into black shielding. Its nine ridges had settled into slower exchange after four days aboard ship. Sanaa Veyl kept two rotating assistants on thermal logs and refused every request for remote access.

A new line on the custody screen caught Tovan's attention.

EXTERNAL MODEL REQUEST: COMMONWEALTH STELLAR DYNAMICS GROUP.

The request carried war-cabinet priority and named a team of sunspindle physicists. It asked for the core's cradle-field coordinates, failure thresholds, coupling duration, and response to synchronized magnetic forcing. The phrasing sounded like safety research until Tovan reached the requested outputs. One field asked for minimum energy required to produce irreversible coherence loss across all nine mapped sites.

"Who approved this?" he asked.

Sanaa closed the lab hatch behind her. She looked older than she had four days earlier, though the ship clocks insisted otherwise. "Fleet Science authorized release of the nonbiological model. I refused the site coordinates."

"Can they derive them?"

"Not from what I released. They may have other astronomy."

Tovan opened the engineering attachments. The physicists had also requested sunspindle control response curves from Meridian, Talassar, and Ceral. Those records fell under his broader refit access because he had built emergency interfaces for all three spindle standards. His repair team had answered two queries already: capacitor discharge timing and loop-coil tolerance under synchronized overload.

"We gave them the hardware half," he said.

"You gave them published safety data."

"With a war-cabinet priority code attached."

Sanaa did not soften the answer. "Yes."

Tovan opened a live query to the Stellar Dynamics Group. A physicist answered by text after nine minutes, identifying herself only by duty position. She confirmed that her team modeled destructive interference so future spindle standards could avoid it. When Tovan asked why the model required minimum irreversible energy, the reply cited contingency analysis under emergency statute.

He sent a simpler question: WHAT PHYSICAL SYSTEM WILL USE THE RESULT?

The response took longer. NO DEPLOYMENT SYSTEM ASSIGNED TO THIS GROUP. OUTPUT SUPPORTS CABINET OPTIONS REVIEW.

That answer had the smooth finish of a sealed panel. The physicist might know nothing beyond her assigned calculation. She might believe the work prevented accidental cradle damage. Tovan could not see intention through a duty title. He could see interfaces. The group's request connected cradle coordinates to human loop timing. Engineering responses connected timing to capacitors and control governors. Logistics transfers connected capacitors to laboratories with guarded docks.

He pulled the authorization chain. Fleet Science had separated the work into five compartments. Stellar Dynamics held field equations. Materials Command held coil tolerance. Three shipyard teams held package design without target coordinates. Medical Forecast had requested casualty assumptions for a failure affecting "nonterrestrial coherent life." No compartment displayed the complete mechanism.

"They built the secrecy like a pressure bulkhead," he said. "Each section can fail without telling the next what escaped."

Sanaa read the authorization chain. "Compartmentalization may protect the work from Veyr intelligence."

"It also protects every worker from the sentence describing what they are making."

The core pulsed behind the inner wall. Forty-one measures, pause, thirty-seven. Tovan brought up the stellar maps from their first decoding session. Nine life regions occupied young magnetic fields. One dense cluster carried more active constituent patterns than the others. Sanaa had labeled it Mazarine based on captured navigation references, then sealed the identification from general archive.

"Tell me what synchronized forcing does," he said.

"Normal spindle operation remains below the modeled danger threshold. Emergency venting does too, provided safety governors remain active."

"They asked about overload."

"I know."

"Then answer that."

Sanaa placed the human loop geometry over the Mazarine map. Three forcing sources appeared at different stellar longitudes, each individually too weak to collapse the development region. Their synchronized fields reinforced across the outer magnetic environment. Coherent structures began to shear apart in the model.

"The core does not provide a recovery pathway after full loss," she said. "At sufficient phase alignment, an overload could sterilize Mazarine."

The exact phrase entered Tovan like cold gas through a suit leak.

"How sufficient?"

"Within human engineering. Not with one civilian spindle under ordinary control. Multiple packages, modified governors, precise timing, and access close enough to couple into the cradle field."

"How many packages?"

"The model request asks us to solve that."

He read the list again. Capacitor discharge. Coil tolerance. Coupling duration. Minimum irreversible energy. None required a weapon designer to label it as such. Each item belonged to ordinary engineering if separated from the others.

"Did Mara request this?"

"The authorization comes from the civilian war cabinet through Fleet Science. I do not know who proposed it."

Tovan thought of the Glass Orbit moving into six corridors. No one had aimed a mirror as a weapon. A civilian authority had cleared rescue paths. Mara had chosen timing. Fleet divisions had fired through the result. Responsibility remained distributed without becoming imaginary.

Here, distribution concealed purpose. One group modeled stellar fields. Another collected control curves. Repair crews supplied tolerances. No individual request contained extermination. Together they formed a maintenance schedule for it.

"Can we refuse?" he asked.

"I have refused biological coordinates pending legal review. They can compel release."

"Can we corrupt the model?"

Sanaa's expression hardened. "Then they will make decisions with false physics. If anyone attempts this, uncertainty may kill the young or human crews in ways we cannot predict."

He knew she was right. Bad data did not produce mercy. It produced unmeasured failure.

Tovan checked his team's outbound work. Five control-response packets had left engineering. Two capacitor assemblies salvaged from damaged cruisers were scheduled for Meridian research. A ring-stabilization simulator built to prevent another Ceral cascade could also calculate phase alignment among stellar magnetic loops.

The repair work had no moral category inside inventory. Components carried mass, temperature, condition, and destination. A capacitor did not know whether it would restart a gate, stabilize a habitat, or help destroy a cradle. That ignorance belonged to hardware. People did not get to borrow it.
