Command links connected them without forcing them into one shape. Mara could see less detail than she had demanded at Orison. She could also lose the whole vault without erasing the mission.

Sori called the final operational count. "One hundred ninety-one active combatants. Support hulls excluded. Nine strike ships attached but not counted separately if carried. Western reserve fueled. All regional intents acknowledged."

Mara opened the last command conference. "State succession."

Each regional commander named a deputy, then a ship-level chain. Kes named no central successor. If Mara died, the six regions would continue under mission intent without replacing her picture. If one region vanished, the others would not compress to fill its planned maneuvers. They would preserve the anchor objective according to what remained visible.

"No commander waits for proof that I am alive," Mara said. "No commander waits for permission to continue. If you lose contact with every other human ship, preserve the cradle, attack your assigned concordance target if conditions permit, and bring home whoever your remaining heat allows."

The crossing lens formed ahead as a pale distortion across the outer system. Unlike a fixed gate between Commonwealth stars, the route beyond the Burning Line required staged alignment through three sparse gate points and long acceleration between them. They would spend three days crossing into Mazarine. Formation would stretch. Messages would become less current. Any Veyr observer would see the fleet commit before humans could see the inner defense respond.

Civilian tenders released their couplings and fell away. The escort group that had refused the mission turned with them. No weapons covered that departure. Kes's reserve moved below the principal plane. Northern carriers opened their own intervals. Nine strike ships remained nested across six sectors, their unfinished control heads dark.

Common Measure entered under tow near the center, not because Mara needed the central position, but because the damaged carrier could survive only inside the tug cluster's protected lane. The distinction mattered to her. Logistics had placed the flagship. Authority had not.

Mara watched the Line move across the wall one ship at a time. She could have ordered tighter intervals. She could have delayed until every formation matched a common clock. Instead, regional commanders crossed when their local lens conditions cleared.

The 191 human combatants passed beyond the Burning Line in six unequal formations. Kes held the reserve outside their symmetry. Nine anchor sectors traveled as shared intent rather than maneuver script. On Common Measure's scarred deck, Jun Aras's empty station remained outside the command network.

When Mara's carrier entered the lens, six regional command lines stayed green without her signal. She let them.

Chapter 25 — The Last Prediction

Anchor Seven tasted of a lineage Ilyr no longer carried.

Its concordance field crossed Consequence of Shelter in pulses of blue thermal agreement. Once, the Patient Ember strand inside Ilyr would have answered that topology with a small countertension. The response had required no conscious negotiation. It belonged to the way nine contributions occupied one person.

Now only seven answered.

Two constituent strands had burned away while Ilyr forced the message through Thren's pursuit. Their vessel channels remained as cold lengths in the braid, sealed at both ends. The witness core preserved consent traces and final divergences, but preserved pattern did not produce a living vote. Ilyr reached for a nine-part balance and found two directions without pressure.

Mazarine filled every other absence.

The young star turned beneath the inner defense, its magnetic arcs carrying unbound choruses through structures older than any present Veyr person. Thermal color moved across the photosphere in slow migrations. Each brightening held lineages not yet negotiated into adult continuity. Nine ancestry contributions overlapped without fixed boundaries. No single Reef could point to one arc and claim it.

Around that nursery, 218 braids assumed war geometry.

Thren-of-Last-Burning placed heavy formations above the stellar poles, where charged particle streams could turn with the magnetic field. Faster braids occupied the outer approach. Nine concordance anchors linked their predictions into a shell of shared timing. Every firing route avoided the inner cradle plane. Every withdrawal route bent around it. The geometry appeared protective until Ilyr examined which braids Thren had placed nearest the human crossing.

The Reefs of Open Current, Patient Ember, and Divided Glass held the outermost arcs.

All three had questioned the censored uncertainty. None had broken Continuance. Thren had assigned them to sectors with poor retreat geometry and no anchor redundancy. When the humans arrived, those braids would absorb the first coil volleys. Their destruction would remove the strongest institutional dissent before the defense required any Reef to choose openly.

Ilyr sent a topology query through the strategy reef. "Outer sectors lack recoverable withdrawal paths."

Thren answered from the central concordance. Its presence carried the red-black thermal memory of two sterilized cradles, an inherited pattern so dominant that nearby predictions curved around it.

"Withdrawal does not serve continuity."

"Nor does removing the Reefs most capable of disputing your forecast."

"They requested a test of human restraint. The outer arcs provide it."

The response spread through military concordance as if it described neutral procedure. Human weapons would cross the three dissenting sectors first. If the humans fired without regard for Veyr loss, Thren would claim confirmation. If they slowed to distinguish targets, the anchors would refine firing predictions and destroy them. If they used the stellar weapon, the cradle would die and Thren's doctrine would receive its final proof in the same event that made proof useless.

A prediction designed every branch to agree with its author.

Ilyr folded the conclusion into a private reef. The missing strands left its shape uneven. Before the pursuit, Ilyr could have tested a decision through nine lineage obligations. Now Open Current contributed no living tension inside the person who carried its name. Patient Ember's channel offered only recorded residue. Action required seven present agreements around two permanent wounds.

That alteration did not grant purity. It removed witnesses Ilyr would once have consulted.

Thren opened a direct line. "Your braid will occupy Anchor Four."

Anchor Four sat far from the dissenting sectors and close to the central reserve. The assignment offered safety, surveillance, and no opportunity to alter the outer geometry.

"Consequence of Shelter cannot sustain Four's field load," Ilyr said.

"Your witness core retains capacity."

"Capacity does not restore missing strands."

"Continuance accepts alteration after necessary loss."

Ilyr let the translated channel carry a pause. Veyr exchange did not require silence, but Thren had learned that human-facing forms made absence legible. "You pursued the loss you now name necessary."

"You transmitted protected cradle geometry to an extinction-capable species."

"I transmitted falsified margins alongside a verifiable delay."

"The distinction expires when their fleet arrives."

The human fleet had crossed the Burning Line. Long-range disturbances already touched Mazarine's outer sensors: gate strain, drive emissions, heat wakes spread across several planes. Ilyr could not yet determine their formation or weapon state. The message acknowledgment proved only that a human witness core had received the six-second signature. Pallas proved Mara had honored the delay once. Neither fact guaranteed what the humans would do near the young.

Thren treated uncertainty as permission to arrange its own vindication.

Ilyr declined Anchor Four.

The refusal moved through the strategy reef as a shear in shared topology. Nearby braids adjusted their predictions, not because Ilyr commanded them but because one expected relation had vanished. Thren closed the direct line and issued a movement constraint through military channels. Two central braids turned toward Consequence of Shelter.

Ilyr had little time.

The suppressed evidence occupied a sealed region of the witness core. It included regulated human sunspindle histories, Sanaa Veyl's inferred safety ranges reconstructed from the captured core exchange, and Thren's original uncertainty edits. Most damaging came from chronology: Continuance attacks had driven human strategic development from local stellar engineering toward cradle-scale overload. The atrocity vector appeared after Veyr destruction of alternatives, not before.

Ilyr tested the record against the objections each lineage would raise. Some human systems had exceeded local spindle limits during industrial emergencies. Those deviations appeared in the packet beside the corrective shutdowns and public inquiries that followed them. Human military research had considered stellar disruption before the war, but no field-ready cradle weapon existed until Continuance attacks supplied motive, captured hardware, and targeting knowledge. The evidence did not make humanity safe. It made permanent classification unsupported.

Thren's edits carried their own lineage signatures. Last Burning had narrowed the probability range after Orison, then removed a branch in which reciprocal inspection reduced risk. A later revision classified human rescue behavior as strategic deception without identifying any observation capable of disproving that label. Each change looked small inside a military model. Together they created a reef that accepted only extinction as input and returned prevention as output.

The packet also preserved Ilyr's errors. At Talassar, Ilyr had accepted necessity while civilians died below a broken ring. At Hesper, six seconds of unauthorized delay had emerged from anomaly rather than moral certainty. Pallas provided one successful test, not vindication. By including those limits, Ilyr denied the lineages an easy division between one honest dissenter and one corrupted war leader. The problem occupied doctrine, not merely Thren.

Ilyr had sent the evidence to Mara. One enemy receiving truth could still bury it, misunderstand it, or convert it into leverage. Distribution now had to pass through Veyr obligations.

All nine lineages possessed authenticated access pathways into Mazarine's generational record. No military leader could close those pathways without interrupting the nursery's contribution accounting. Thren controlled strategy concordance, not the deeper lineage witness routes. Ilyr opened the sealed evidence and divided it into nine nonidentical packets.

Each packet began with facts the receiving lineage could verify from its own record. Open Current received targeting delays and the hospital ship's six-second passage. Patient Ember received Thren's edits to the Commonwealth restraint margins. Divided Glass received Pallas geometry showing that the human commander withheld stellar deployment during tactical vulnerability. The remaining six received sunspindle operating histories, cradle-risk thresholds, the Continuance escalation chronology, and cross-signatures from the captured witness core.

No packet asked a Reef to trust Ilyr's conclusion. Each supplied a path from local evidence to the full record.

The transmission required more than bandwidth. Lineage witness routes carried personhood accounting and nursery contributions, not military accusations. Ilyr had to classify the evidence as a continuity hazard to the young. A false classification would isolate Consequence of Shelter from every cradle record for generations. A valid classification would force all receiving Reefs to preserve and examine it even under battle conditions.

Ilyr entered the claim.

CONTINUITY HAZARD: PREDICTIVE DOCTRINE GENERATING THE CRADLE-ATTACK PATH IT CLAIMS TO PREVENT.

Anchor Seven's blue pulses intensified around the braid. The local concordance field recognized unauthorized traffic and tried to route it through Thren's filters. Ilyr moved the packets into lineage channels before those filters closed.

One went out.

Three followed through separate magnetic harmonics. The fifth stalled when a central braid flooded the route with military timing. Ilyr's surviving strands changed internal agreement, sacrificing sensor coherence to push witness priority above navigation. Consequence of Shelter drifted two degrees toward the protected inner plane.

The sixth packet cleared. Then the seventh.

Thren's command struck the channel. "Terminate false hazard classification."

Ilyr sent the eighth.

The ninth required Patient Ember authentication from a strand no longer living inside Ilyr. Recorded residue could prove prior contribution but could not consent to present action. Ilyr opened the cold channel and allowed the lineage route to measure its absence. That measurement carried the fact of Thren's pursuit: a constituent of Patient Ember had been destroyed while transmitting suppressed evidence.

The route accepted loss as authentication.

Nine packets entered nine lineage records.

The strategy reef changed shape before any spoken reply arrived. Braids belonging to distant Reefs widened their prediction ranges. Some requested original margins from Thren. Others queried whether human sunspindle regulation had been included in first-contact classification. A cluster near the north arc withdrew its consent from one firing model while preserving its defensive station.

Thren answered with forced coherence. The anchors tightened timing around the fleet, pulling local predictions into a narrower common reef.

"The evidence concerns extinct alternatives," Thren broadcast. "Human entry into Mazarine proves present extinction capability. No margin survives an armed approach to the young."

Ilyr opened its response to every lineage channel. "Capability entered their strategy after Continuance destroyed three systems, struck Talassar, and killed millions at Ceral. You call the result prior nature because your forecast has no field for consequence."

"They carry stellar control packages."

"They carried them at Pallas and withheld use during an ambush."

"A tactical delay does not establish restraint."

"No single act establishes a species. Your doctrine requires one act to condemn one."

The exchange did not persuade Thren. Persuasion of one person no longer supplied the objective. Ilyr needed enough lineages to see that the defense geometry served Thren's model before it served the nursery.

Open Current answered first through a low thermal oscillation. Its Reef did not accept Ilyr's hazard conclusion. It requested removal from the sacrificial outer arc until review.

Patient Ember followed. It authenticated the missing strand's final record and asked why Thren had classified uncertainty edits as military necessity rather than doctrinal dispute.

Divided Glass split its response across two paths. One part affirmed cradle defense. Another withdrew consent to any prediction requiring dissenting braids to die before central formations engaged.

Three Reefs had hesitated.

The hesitation propagated differently through each formation. Open Current widened distances between its vessel-strands, reducing the speed at which local fire choices could become one braid decision. Patient Ember kept formation but marked every target solution with the missing evidence branch. Divided Glass sent two parallel readiness states to its crews, one for obedience and one for refusal, without selecting between them.

None of those acts prevented a weapon from firing. They changed which obligations would remain visible when it did. Under ordinary concordance, a braid could enter shared prediction and later remember only that the fleet's geometry had made one action necessary. The three dissenting formations now preserved the rejected paths alongside the chosen one. Future witness cores would know that alternatives had existed at contact.

Other Reefs reacted without hesitation. Last Burning tightened around Thren. Steady Ash denounced Ilyr's classification as an attack on cradle custody. Far Tides accepted the raw human regulation data while rejecting every inference drawn from it. Nine lineages did not divide into belief and disbelief. They produced a more dangerous topology: partial agreement spread across incompatible duties.

Ilyr's seven living strands negotiated their response. Two urged immediate movement toward the outer sectors, where physical presence might obstruct Thren's sacrifice order. Three rejected that path because Consequence of Shelter lacked enough thrust to survive interception. The remaining strands favored proximity to an anchor, where one damaged braid could alter observation at lower force. No absent constituent could break the balance. Ilyr accepted the anchor path with less internal agreement than the intact person would once have required.

Hesitation did not alter the dissenters' positions. Their braids remained in the outer sectors. Weapons stayed charged. Anchor timing still included them. None declared support for Ilyr, and none refused combat against the humans. The topology had loosened by a fraction, enough to create alternatives and not enough to save anyone.

Thren recognized the same change.

"Dissent at contact confirms fragmentation risk," it told the strategy reef. "Outer sectors will advance to meet the humans beyond cradle range. Central formations will preserve the young."

The new order made sacrifice explicit. Open Current would accelerate first, Patient Ember second, Divided Glass along the lower plane. Their isolated engagements would produce observations stripped of context. Aggressive human response would validate extermination. Human restraint would let Thren claim the enemy preserved force for the stellar strike.

Ilyr examined the nine anchor fields. So long as concordance held, Thren could convert every local choice into one fleetwide prediction. The humans would face not a unified mind but 218 armed persons acting inside shared timing that suppressed the cost of disagreement.

"Your doctrine now threatens the young it claims to protect," Ilyr said.

The phrase entered lineage witness routes rather than military speech. Ilyr attached geometry. Sacrificing the outer Reefs weakened three anchor screens. Narrowing predictions increased the chance that an unmodeled human maneuver would penetrate the inner defense. Concentrating central fire near the cradle raised charged-particle flux through developing chorus structures. Worst of all, arranging battle as proof left no path to recognize restraint if it occurred.

Thren replied through private concordance. "The young cannot survive a civilization permitted to choose extinction later."

"Then no evidence could ever release a classified threat."

"Correct."

The answer carried no cruelty. Thren offered it as a stable obligation learned from two ancient sterilizations. That stability had protected cradles for centuries. It had also removed the distinction between a forecast and a sentence.

Ilyr understood that no further evidence would move Thren. The remaining choice concerned where evidence could become observable action.

The central braids approached Consequence of Shelter. Their particle channels remained cold, but ceramic restraint needles entered launch rails. Thren could disable Ilyr's vessel-strands without risking the witness core, then place the core under Continuance custody until after battle.

Ilyr requested reassignment to Anchor Seven.

The request surprised the local reef. Seven occupied the most dangerous relation in Mazarine. It traveled near the inner cradle plane, screened by fewer braids because defenders could not fire freely around it. Human attack geometry would likely target it late, after outer anchors failed or held. A damaged braid beside Seven could serve as additional custody and physical shield.

Thren accepted.

"You will preserve the anchor you helped expose," it said. "If the humans strike through you, the nine lineages will observe the nature of your anomaly."

Ilyr recognized the trap and the opening inside it. Thren expected human fire to kill the dissenter, damage the anchor, or cross toward the nursery. Any outcome could enter the forecast as proof. Yet Anchor Seven also offered the only position from which Ilyr could expose a protected approach without ordering another Reef.

That action belonged to a later decision. Ilyr had not observed the human weapon choice. It did not know whether Mara's fleet carried sterilization, concordance attack, or both. To open Seven before evidence of restraint would gamble the young on hope. To defend it without observing human limits would preserve Thren's closed prediction.

Consequence of Shelter turned toward the inner plane.

The damaged braid moved unevenly. Seven active propulsion nodes compensated for two cold vessel channels. Each correction entered concordance as a visible imbalance. Veyr formations made space around it, some with ritual regard for altered personhood, others with the caution granted a contaminated prediction.

Below, Mazarine's unbound choruses crossed a bright magnetic arch. Their topology contained no awareness of the 218 braids above them. They could not consent to protection purchased through a doctrine that manufactured the danger. They could not consent to Ilyr's risk either.

Open Current sent a final private relation: hesitation continued, position unchanged.

Patient Ember preserved Ilyr's hazard claim without endorsing it. Divided Glass marked two possible futures and chose neither. The three Reefs had not defected. Their uncertainty remained alive inside the defense.

At Anchor Seven, Ilyr coupled Consequence of Shelter to the local timing field. Blue thermal agreement entered the braid through seven living strands and two cold channels. Thren's predictions arrived with it: human fire, cradle overload, Ilyr's death, Continuance vindicated.

The anchor offered three kinds of access. Military concordance supplied projected human vectors. Nursery custody supplied magnetic conditions below. Maintenance relation exposed the anchor's own shielding, phase nodes, and emergency openings. Thren had granted the first two and sealed the third. Ilyr's witness core still knew a lineage route into maintenance because Patient Ember had contributed to Seven's last renewal.

Using that route before human contact would trigger automatic isolation. Ilyr could disclose the shielding seam only after a locally observed continuity hazard. Human restraint might qualify. Human fire through the cradle would not. The rule forced evidence to precede intervention, exactly where trust demanded action before certainty.

Ilyr placed observers on every available channel. One tracked the outer Reefs and whether their hesitation survived first fire. Another measured human approach vectors against the protected plane. A third watched Thren's central reserve for any command that treated Veyr losses as proof rather than cost. The remaining capacity stayed with the young's magnetic structures. If battle disturbed them, no political argument could justify keeping a military channel clearer than nursery custody.

Seven's shield rotated between Consequence of Shelter and the expected human approach. The braid could move outside that shield and leave the anchor exposed. It could also remain, forcing any direct human strike to cross Ilyr's body first. Neither posture answered what Ilyr should do after observing restraint. Both made the observation impossible to hide.

Ilyr kept those futures open without accepting their common conclusion.

Far beyond the outer Reefs, human drive heat began to resolve into six unequal formations. They did not approach as one geometry. No central symmetry linked their sectors. Thren's forecast expanded to contain them and tightened again.

Ilyr placed the damaged braid beside Anchor Seven, directly inside the most probable human firing corridor. Ceramic hull warmed under the anchor's field. The witness core opened every remaining record channel.

When human fire arrived, all nine lineages would see what it chose to cross.

Chapter 26 — The Cradle at Mazarine

The phase trace split every time the crippled carrier rolled.

Tovan Rhee watched three green curves separate across the repair tender's test wall, reunite for eleven milliseconds, then wander apart as Common Measure corrected her approach to Mazarine. The carrier no longer possessed a command array, a captain, or enough intact radiator surface to waste heat on clean maneuvering. What she still possessed filled the tender's forward windows: armored flanks scorched at Pallas Verge, flight bays converted into workshops, and nine strike ships clamped around her like tools laid against a wounded hand.

One of those tools had just missed synchronization by thirty-one microseconds.

"Again," Tovan said.

The tender's power chief did not touch the control. "At this load, yes. At firing load, the coupler drift multiplies."

"We cannot test at firing load."

"That fact has survived all four times you told me."

A better engineer might have found the answer insulting. Tovan found it accurate. He leaned closer to the trace and enlarged the point where Strike Six lost phase. Vibrations from the carrier reached its clamps, crossed a temporary bus, and shook a capacitor rack built for a captured sunspindle control package. Every part had passed inspection alone. Together they behaved like debris tied with wire.

Outside the windows, Mazarine's star colored the dark with green-white streamers. Instruments filtered most of the brightness, but no display could make the cradle ordinary. Magnetic arches climbed from the photosphere across distances large enough to swallow Talassar's orbit. Thermal knots moved inside them according to patterns Sanaa Veyl had identified as living development. Tovan could not see an unbound chorus. He could see where a careless pulse would erase one.

The Sable packages sat inside the nine strike ships. Their high-power channels remained physically isolated under Mara's order. Tovan had removed the arming bridges himself after the refusal aboard Common Measure. Yet each package still carried enough authority over a star's field to become a nursery key if someone rebuilt the path. The same hardware now had to emit low-power pulses across nine concordance anchors inside a window of 0.8 seconds.

Low power sounded gentle only to people who did not maintain machines.

To blind an anchor, each strike ship would dump its charge through the main thermal-transfer structure before enemy fire or local drift spoiled the phase. The pulse would leave the cradle's magnetic geometry below Sanaa's disturbance limit. It would also leave the ship unable to move waste heat from drive, weapons, or life support. No second attempt. No retreat under combat acceleration. Perhaps no retreat at all.

Tovan reduced the trace to raw timing numbers. "Strike Six needs a cleaner store. Swap its rack with Two."

"Two holds the most stable unit we have," the chief said.

"Which means we know what good looks like. Move it to Six and see whether the fault follows."

Orders traveled through hardline relays because Mazarine's magnetosphere turned ordinary comms into bursts of static. On the forward plot, 191 human combatants crossed the outer system in dispersed columns. Farther sunward, sensor estimates marked the Veyr defense at more than two hundred braids. Tovan left the exact count to officers who could improve it by looking. His work concerned nine small hulls and a shrinking stack of replacement parts.

A collision alert flashed amber. Something ungainly approached from the anti-sunward transfer lane, too slow for a warship and too hot for wreckage.

"Tender Control, identify that mass," Tovan said.

The answer came through with a civilian delay and a familiar convoy code. "Three utility tugs requesting engineering rendezvous. Lantern Nine registry. They claim to carry phase-stable capacitors."

Tovan stared at the code. He remembered water bladders drifting through the Hesper lane, each warmed to mimic a fuel orchard while Veyr needles chased false heat. Civilian tug crews had held formation around cargo that every military escort expected them to abandon. Their ships had survived because they knew exactly how little machinery could be trusted.

"Bring them under the tender," he said. "No magnetic clamps. Use lines."

The lead tug arrived with patched tanks along its spine and a radiator made from three different commercial patterns. Its hull carried old ceramic puncture marks sealed in pale circles. Two smaller craft followed in its wake, their drives pulsing out of rhythm because synchronized civilian burns might resemble a military target. None carried the shielding required for Mazarine's inner plane.

A tugmaster appeared on Tovan's wall, broad face washed blue by an emergency display. "We heard you needed nine matched stores."

"I need nine stores stable within six microseconds after thermal rise."

"Then you need better stores than Fleet gave you."

"Fleet gave me what it had. What did you bring?"

The tugmaster turned the camera toward a cargo bay. Cylinders lay inside restraint cages, each wrapped in hand-marked insulation. "Gate-lens correction banks from the Lantern Nine orchard tenders. They held phase through the Hesper raid. We kept them matched afterward because no one paid us enough to separate them."

Tovan checked the serial stream. The banks came from commercial vector-control gear, not weapons. Their discharge rate fell below the packages' requirement by eight percent. Their phase stability exceeded every unit on the strike ships.

"They will melt at peak draw," the power chief said quietly.

"So will most of the ships."

"That does not make it good engineering."

"No. It makes it available engineering."

The tug crews transferred the banks through vacuum on braided cargo lines. There was no time to dock. Tender workers guided each cylinder into the repair lock while Mazarine's charged wind pulled blue sparks from the insulation. One line jammed after its reel motor overheated. A civilian rigger clipped onto it, crossed forty meters by suit thrusters, and struck the release housing with a wrench until the brake opened.

Tovan met the first bank inside the lock. Its casing smelled of scorched polymer after pressure came up. A faded inspection label showed a Meridian commercial standard fourteen years out of date. Beneath it, someone had written nine temperature corrections in grease pencil. The corrections matched the actual behavior better than the manufacturer's model.

"Who characterized these?" he asked over the tug channel.

"Everyone who had to keep them alive," the tugmaster replied.

Installation began across all nine strike ships at once. Tovan divided the tender crew into paired teams and sent one civilian technician with each bank. The captured control packages occupied the center of the craft where missile drums had once sat. Coolant lines twisted around armored conduits. Manual cutoffs protruded from housings built by three human yards and one Veyr-informed design group that had never met in the same room.

Strike Six accepted the commercial bank after its crew cut away a bracket with a thermal saw. Strike Three lacked enough bus length, so workers stripped a maneuvering-feed cable and sleeved it against pulse voltage. On Strike Eight, the bank fit perfectly until the access cover closed against a pressure sensor. Tovan ordered the cover left open and the compartment evacuated during firing.

Each compromise entered his board in yellow. None turned green merely because the clock kept moving.

At the far end of the tender, a warning bell announced human fleet acceleration. The deck shifted under Tovan's boots. Tools tugged against their straps. Through a maintenance blister, he saw distant drive flares arrange into three broad arcs around the cradle plane. Mara's commanders had chosen their own paths to the anchors. No central maneuver script linked them now. Their geometry looked loose to anyone raised on parade formations and sensible to anyone who had watched delayed orders kill.

The Veyr answered with charged particle streams that painted the filtered windows violet.

Combat had begun beyond useful sight, but its consequences arrived at the tender as changing schedules. Strike Four lost its planned launch corridor when an escort division moved across it. Strike Nine had to detach early because a needle cloud crossed the carrier's wake. A cruiser requested the tender's spare radiator salt, then withdrew the request when damage sealed its feed port. Every revision cost seconds from the test sequence.

Tovan crawled into Strike Two's package bay to inspect the final coupler himself. The hull vibrated against Common Measure's clamp. A technician lay beside him with one arm inside the capacitor cage.

"Six is clean now," she said. "Fault didn't follow the Fleet rack."

"Then the rack wasn't guilty."

"Clamp vibration?"

"Partly. The commercial banks correct faster, but they need a common temperature reference."

She pulled her arm free. "Across nine ships? We cannot run a physical loop."

Tovan looked at the condensation crawling along a coolant pipe. Temperature sensing aboard each craft relied on local calibration. Local readings could agree while the actual hardware differed. The dry run would pass on clocks and fail when nine banks warmed at nine rates.

He opened a channel to the tugs. "How did Lantern Nine keep the orchard banks matched?"

"Coolant batches," the tugmaster said. "We filled all correction units from one mixing tank. Same salt, same contamination, same aging."

The tender had no tank large enough to service nine strike ships before launch. Common Measure did. Her surviving cold-loop had kept a carrier alive after Orison, Hesper, Ceral, and Pallas Verge. Its coolant now carried metal fines, sealant, and years of thermal abuse. No laboratory would call it consistent. It remained consistent with itself.

"Drain the strike-ship reference loops," Tovan ordered. "Refill them from the carrier cold-loop return."

The power chief looked appalled. "That coolant fails every current purity standard."

"Its failure comes in one shared flavor. We need matching, not virtue."

Workers dragged temporary hoses through the launch bays. Dark radiator salt slurry moved from Common Measure into nine small reference reservoirs. Filters caught flakes from old seals. The carrier gave up thermal margin it could not spare, and the strike ships took aboard the common history of everything that had damaged her.

While the loops filled, Tovan's personal queue chimed. A delayed civil packet had crossed the last intact gate before military traffic closed it. Source: Talassar Surface Medical Network. Sender: Anja Rhee.

He almost left it unopened. Messages from his mother usually arrived with practical headings: pump allocation, elevator motor failure, names of people who had found each other. This one carried only his name.

The package teams had twenty-one minutes before the next synchronization attempt. Tovan sat on an overturned cable drum in Strike Two's empty magazine and played the recording.

Anja's face appeared against a shelter wall stained by condensation. Gray threaded her dark hair. A medical lift status board glowed behind her with six columns of red entries.

"Tovan," she said, "your fleet censor asked me to record something useful for morale. I declined. This message may therefore be useful."

He heard the old Talassar surf rhythm in her vowels, flattened by bad speakers and fatigue.

"They told us enough about the choice near Mazarine. Not the machinery. I would not understand your machinery if you drew it on my hand. We understand the price here. The west shelf still has districts running surgery on tide storage. Children sleep under the lift counter because their apartments took ring debris. Some people want you to burn whatever the Veyr love. Some say that where the cameras can hear. More say it when they think I agree."

Anja glanced away as someone crossed behind the recorder. Her expression sharpened with the same impatience she had used on priority officials during evacuation.

"Do not use us as proof that mercy costs nothing. We have paid too much for anyone to speak that lie. If you choose the harder tool, make it work. If it fails, people here will die who never voted on your conscience. I may be one of them. That does not give you permission to choose the easier tool. It gives you an obligation to know the difference between difficult and unfinished."

She lifted one hand. Grease darkened two fingers, likely from a lift motor.

"Also, you still owe me a medical elevator. Come repair it when you have finished repairing the species."

The recording ended without farewell.

Tovan sat until the empty screen reflected his face. He had expected comfort and received a work order. That suited Anja. It also denied him the private story in which refusing atrocity completed his duty. Refusal had only selected the harder failure mode. Engineering began after that choice.

Memory supplied the medical elevator as it had looked before the war: two patched cars climbing inside a concrete shaft above Talassar's western sea. Anja had kept spare relay contacts in a tea tin because official replacements corroded during monsoon months. Tovan had promised to rebuild the controller during his next surface leave, then missed that leave for a carrier inspection. After the ring fell, the elevator carried patients between shelter levels on stored tidal power. Nothing in its maintenance log belonged to strategy. A failed contact still meant a stalled car, frightened patients, and his mother opening a cabinet with the wrong screwdriver because every correct tool had gone elsewhere.

The nine packages deserved the same honesty. Their safe envelope could not depend on commanders remembering good intentions after losses began. Fear would arrive as a request for two percent more output, then five, then whatever margin promised to recover a failed pulse. Tovan knew that grammar from damaged engines. A crew tolerated one extra degree, then another, until the machine crossed a limit no speech could move. The cradle needed a cutoff that did not admire courage.

He reopened the synchronization model and added a new abort rule. If any ship drifted beyond the cradle-safe envelope, the other eight would discharge into dummy loads rather than compensate upward. The pulse might fail to blind the anchors. It would not climb toward Sable output while chasing success.

"That cuts our recovery tolerance in half," the power chief said.

"Yes."

"Mara needs all nine."

"Mara needs a weapon that remains what we told her it is."

No one argued after that. They entered the rule in every local controller, then verified each checksum by voice because network confirmation could be forged by noise. Nine crews read back nine limits. Tovan listened for fatigue errors and caught Strike Five transposing two digits. The crew repeated the full string correctly.

Launch clamps released in pairs. The nine strike ships moved away from Common Measure on cold thrusters and arranged themselves around the tender at combat spacing. Their hulls looked too small to carry a decision about two civilizations. Tovan preferred them that way. Large ideas should have to fit through access hatches.

The dry run began at one percent charge.

Each package received a timing mark generated aboard the tender, corrected locally, then passed its phase state to two neighbors. The arrangement did not create central control. Loss of Tovan's signal would leave the ring able to converge from its last shared state. Strike One reported ready. Four followed after a cooling delay. Nine drifted by five microseconds, corrected from its matched bank, and held.

Mazarine filled the background with radio thunder. Veyr anchors imposed periodic order on that noise, nine hard pulses whose intervals changed as fleets moved. The strike controllers tracked those patterns without transmitting at combat strength. Their purpose concerned timing, not prediction. They needed one moment when nine local choices entered the same narrow window.

"Reference loops rising," the chief said. "Common contamination curve confirmed."

On Tovan's wall, the green traces tightened.

A Veyr needle cloud crossed the outer sensor plane. The strike ships rolled as a group but selected different evasive vectors. For three seconds the traces spread. Tovan kept his hands away from the control. If he corrected each craft from the tender, he would prove only that the array worked while he remained alive and unjammed.

Strike Six passed a local correction to Three. Eight discarded a false temperature reading. Two delayed its clock by fourteen microseconds rather than demand the others accelerate. The curves came together without Tovan.

"Window opening," the chief said.

Nine status lights turned white.

The controllers simulated discharge. Numbers raced down the wall: anchor phase, cradle disturbance estimate, bus load, coupling loss, thermal migration. The model placed every pulse inside 0.8 seconds. Projected field stress stayed below the nursery limit. No local abort fired.

"Dry run synchronized," the chief said.

No one cheered. A second result followed the timing report, colored red from edge to edge.

At combat charge, current would exceed the commercial banks' survival limit in 0.12 seconds. The packages would route the excess into each craft's heat spine. Main thermal transfer would delaminate from bow to drive. Batteries could preserve life support for thirty hours if the crew shut down everything else. Maneuvering thrust after discharge would risk a hull fire.

Tovan checked all nine projections. Variation no longer mattered. Every curve ended in the same red condition.

"Could we lower output two percent?" the power chief asked.

"Anchor Seven might remain coherent even at full output. Lowering charge sacrifices another anchor."

"Then the ships fire and wait for tow."

Tovan looked toward the tactical display. Human escorts already fought for the approach lanes. Nothing promised a tow after the pulse. The carrier could not recover nine crippled craft under Mazarine fire. Civilian tugs lacked shielding for the inner plane. Gateward retreat would begin while the strike crews floated without radiators beyond the Burning Line.

He keyed the result to all ships and did not soften its language.

"One use burns out every heat spine. Post-discharge propulsion is prohibited. Life support becomes time-limited. Rescue depends on local battle conditions. If you fire, expect to be stranded. Acknowledge with crew consent, not command receipt."

The answers came separately. Some took seconds. Strike Four waited nearly a minute while its pilot spoke to the package crew. Strike Nine asked whether ejection before firing would alter phase. Tovan said it would, and the crew remained aboard. One by one, nine acknowledgments entered the board.

Last came Strike Six, whose wandering trace had begun the work.

"Consent recorded," its pilot said. "Tell the tugs their capacitors kept faith."

Tovan sent the full synchronization receipt to Mara's distributed command net. Around him, the tender crew pulled hoses, locked covers, and cleared launch paths. The nine strike ships turned sunward toward concordance anchors hidden among two hundred eighteen Veyr braids.

Behind them, Common Measure rolled on her damaged axis, carrying the coolant history that had taught nine doomed thermal systems how to agree.

Chapter 27 — Battle Beyond the Burning Line

Mazarine gave Mara Venn nine objectives and no safe line between them.

The concordance anchors occupied separate magnetic latitudes around the young star. Each one moved inside an escorting geometry of Veyr braids, never crossing the inner cradle plane where unbound choruses developed. To reach them, the human fleets had to divide, curve around living territory, and strike within 0.8 seconds before the surviving anchors corrected the whole defense.

Her wall held the opening count in hard white figures: 191 human combatants against 218 Veyr braids around nine concordance anchors. No reserve quantity hid behind either number. Every carrier, cruiser, escort, and braid already had a place in the geometry.

Mara stood inside the command vault of a cruiser fitted as her temporary flagship. The array surrounding her seat had been assembled from Meridian fleet gear, a Ceral traffic predictor, and three consoles stripped from Common Measure after Pallas Verge. Its picture carried two seconds of delay on the outer plane and seven across the magnetospheric shear. That made it useful for intent, dangerous for maneuver, and seductive to anyone who mistook completeness for control.

Nine regional formations advanced on paths their commanders had written themselves. Kes Renn held the reserve anti-sunward, far enough away to choose among failures but too distant to rescue everyone. The nine strike ships drifted behind separate screens, cold and small on the plot. Tovan's synchronization receipt lay open beside them. One pulse. Heat spines destroyed. Crews stranded if no tow survived.

Mara keyed the fleetwide mission band. "Objective remains anchor blindness without cradle intrusion. Preserve local timing. Do not repair formation for appearance. If command fails, continue under your sealed intent."

Acknowledgments arrived out of order. Some commanders sent words. Others changed vectors in the precise ways they had agreed would count as receipt. Kes answered last.

"Reserve understands," she said. "Try not to spend us before we become useful."

"No promises."

"Good. You used to make too many."

The Veyr struck before Mara closed the channel.

Anchor Four changed its pulse interval by a fraction. Nine Veyr firing groups rotated around that alteration, not as one consciousness but as separate combatants sharing prediction. Their charged streams crossed empty space where the human center would have been under Mara's original plan. Then the streams divided according to regional deviations, searching for the local commands that had replaced her script.

Thren had learned from Hesper, Brindle, and Ceral. The opening fire did not concentrate on carriers or strike ships. It converged on communication relays, clock sources, and the command cruiser carrying Mara.

"Particle front, inner-high vector," Tactical said. "Twelve seconds."

Mara saw three evasions. A sunward roll preserved the command dish and exposed the drive. A prograde burn escaped the first beam but crossed a friendly missile lane. Dropping toward the cradle plane offered cover behind magnetic noise and violated the boundary they had come to preserve.

"Roll sunward. Sacrifice the drive skirt. Broadcast local autonomy now."

The cruiser turned. Acceleration pressed Mara against her restraint. Escorts crossed behind them and fired ablative clouds into the predicted particle paths. Two clouds dispersed too early under Mazarine's stellar wind. A third caught one beam and spread it into a violet fan.

The remaining fire hit the flagship.

Armor boiled from the sunward flank. Lights vanished across half the vault. The deck kicked once, then rolled beneath Mara as maneuvering control lost its reference. A console broke loose from its mounts and crossed the compartment trailing cables. Someone screamed near the rear bulkhead. Fire suppressant filled the air with bitter white crystals.

Her tactical wall froze on a picture already four seconds old.

"Array status?" Mara asked.

No answer came from the command pit. Lieutenant Sori climbed from behind a collapsed display with blood along one temple. "Primary clocks gone. Long-baseline dish severed. Bus fire in prediction core."

"Restore a voice channel."

"We can give you tight beam to one formation at a time. No common picture."

The command array had not merely failed. Thren's first strike had killed every mechanism that let Mara pretend the battle could still pass through her hands.

In the darkened vault, emergency strips drew red lines along the deck. Damage crews sealed the command pit while Mara pulled herself to an auxiliary plot no larger than a maintenance table. It showed the flagship, four nearby escorts, and fragments of remote telemetry delayed beyond tactical use.

"Fleet command marker?" she asked.

Sori listened to a damaged headset. "Still transmitting from backup. Regional groups know you're alive."

"Turn it off."

He looked at her. "Admiral?"

"The marker invites them to wait for orders I cannot make current. Transmit command-array loss. Confirm mission intent unchanged. Then cease central maneuver traffic."

For one breath, every lesson Mara had learned before this war objected. A commander gathered information, resolved contradictions, and imposed sequence. At Orison, cutting seventeen ships free had saved them because she alone held enough of the geometry. At Meridian, seventy-four flags had given her authority because no local fleet could see the Line. Central command had worked often enough to become more than habit. It had become her measure of responsibility.

Now responsibility required her to stop speaking.

Sori sent the burst. The flagship marker disappeared.

Remote icons continued moving.

Fragments of regional traffic reached the auxiliary receiver whenever the flagship's damaged dish crossed a useful angle. A commander near Anchor Four rejected a carrier's request for close escort because the missiles mattered more than the hull that launched them. Another voice assigned rescue coordinates for a cruiser that had not yet stopped accelerating. At Anchor Six, a habitat captain announced a local retreat, then specified that the retreat would pass sunward of the anchor and draw seven braids away from the strike corridor. None of the voices sounded free of fear. They sounded occupied by decisions.

Sori marked the transmissions as requests until Mara corrected the labels. "Those are reports."

"Some are asking whether Fleet concurs."

"They are telling us what concurrence would arrive too late to affect. Acknowledge receipt if the dish permits. Do not attach approval."

The distinction felt small enough to hide inside procedure. It carried the whole change. Approval would make each commander wait for her judgment on the next choice. Receipt allowed them to know the fleet had heard without pretending hearing created authority. Mara had demanded exact obedience for most of her career because ambiguity during a crisis killed. Mazarine forced a narrower truth on her: obsolete certainty killed faster.

The western formation lost three escorts at Anchor Two and bent gateward rather than wait for replacement orders. Its commander shifted a cruiser into the timing role and kept advancing. Near Anchor Six, two habitat fleets separated when a needle screen cut their shared corridor. One accelerated sunward; the other used the new gap to launch missiles from below the anchor's prediction plane. Around Anchor Nine, civilian conversion ships dumped mirror foil that made no sense on Mara's stale picture until a carrier group turned the foil into a false thermal horizon.

No formation sought permission.

Mara received their choices as consequences rather than proposals. The change hurt with physical clarity. She could see errors she would have corrected: an escort screen too dense near Anchor Three, a cruiser spending reaction mass before its firing pass, two carriers approaching the same withdrawal lane. She could also see opportunities no central array would have authorized soon enough. Local commanders traded objects she did not possess on her board, including damaged thrusters, partial magazines, and the willingness of specific crews.

A casualty counter rebuilt itself from delayed beacons. Seventeen human ships had gone dark in the opening exchange. Six reported wrecked but inhabited sections. Eleven gave no life signs. Veyr losses appeared as broken braids whose vessel-strands separated around cooling witness cores, though the anchor network kept revising estimates.

The flagship's captain called from damage control. "We have one drive cluster and half a radiator. We can hold this vector for nine minutes."

"After nine?"

"We choose between heat death and a burn that may tear the stern."

Mara checked the strike approach. The blinding window lay eleven minutes away.

"Give me nine," she said. "At the end, choose locally."

The captain understood the phrase. No salute appeared on the audio-only channel. "Locally, then."

A Veyr braid crossed near the flagship, three ceramic forms rotating around a bright core. Human escorts fired coil lances. The braid altered before the slugs arrived, using anchor prediction to pass between two trajectories, then launched needles toward the cruiser's damaged radiator.

One escort moved into the path without orders. Needles punched through its drive section. The ship did not explode. It folded around the impacts and began venting atmosphere in a silver trail. Its last maneuver nudged the wreck clear of the flagship radiator.

Mara read its name from the auxiliary plot and entered it in the loss ledger herself.

At Anchor Five, a regional commander requested the strike timing be advanced by twenty-six seconds. At Anchor One, another requested a delay of forty. Both reasons made sense within local geometry. Neither could be reconciled by a headquarters without a current picture.

Mara opened the bounded-intent packet written before crossing the Line. It gave each strike commander authority to select any point within a ninety-second window, provided neighbor consensus kept all discharges inside 0.8 seconds. Tovan's peer timing could converge without her.

"Reply to both," she told Sori. "Use local phase agreement. Central timing withdrawn."

"They may choose different windows."

"Then the strike fails. If I choose on stale data, it fails with my signature attached. That offers no tactical advantage."

The count reached twenty-eight human ships lost.

Veyr pressure shifted toward the inner approaches. Thren's forces accepted losses along the outer plane to compress the human formations against the cradle boundary. The maneuver exploited restraint as a wall. Any human coil lance fired through the protected plane risked crossing a magnetic structure where young Veyr patterns formed. Sable promised to erase that wall. Mara could feel the ugly military elegance of it even without her full array.

A cabinet-priority channel forced itself onto the auxiliary console. Convener Olvara Pike appeared through blocks of corrupted image. Behind her, the Meridian war room carried a battle delayed by gate routing and reconstruction.

"Admiral Venn, your command loss has activated continuity authority," Pike said. "We have partial access to the strike packages through their original control architecture."

Mara looked at the nine cold icons. "Tovan removed the arming bridges."

"Each craft carries a sealed restoration kit. Crews can rebuild the high-power path manually, but the six cryptographic refusal states still govern any restored path. The cabinet renews authorization for Sable if the concordance attempt falls below viability. Current projected human fleet survival is twenty-two percent."

The option remained real. Nine crews could restore the channels while their screens held. They could draw beyond the low-power envelope, couple the captured sunspindle logic into Mazarine's field, and collapse the nursery geometry. Veyr coordination would fail because every defender would turn toward the dying cradle or lose the future it defended. The strike might also compel surrender before human losses passed the point of recovery.

Mara did not need to believe it painless. She needed only to count the systems still exposed if her fleet died here.

"What constitutes below viability?" she asked.

Pike's image stalled, then resumed. "Fewer than seven packages able to fire, or projected fleet loss above fifty percent before anchor disruption."

"We have not crossed either threshold."

"Your telemetry is incomplete. Our reconstruction shows forty-three ships lost or combat-disabled."

"Disabled does not equal lost."

"It does if you cannot recover them beyond the Line."

Mara had used the same arithmetic at Orison. Position, fuel, and timing divided ships into those she could save and those she could not. Fourteen had remained behind because trying to preserve all thirty-one would have killed everyone. Sable offered the largest version of that cut: preserve human systems by placing the Veyr future outside survival geometry.

This time, the boundary did not come from physics alone. Human choice had drawn it.

"If we restore the high-power paths," Mara said, "how long before discharge?"

"Four minutes for the fastest crews. Seven across all nine."

The concordance strike window lay six minutes away.

Pike leaned toward her camera. "You accepted operational command because hesitation by another officer would be worse. I am asking whether your hesitation has now become the greater danger."

The question deserved more than moral disgust. Mara rebuilt the decision from what remained certain. The nine strike ships had synchronized. Regional fleets continued after central loss. Eight anchors could plausibly go dark even if one resisted. Ilyr's map had proved true at Three Shadows. Three Veyr Reefs had hesitated according to the alien message, but hesitation did not equal defection. The low-power plan carried severe uncertainty and placed every surviving human system at risk if it failed.

Sable carried less tactical uncertainty. It also guaranteed the catastrophe the Veyr had forecast.

"My refusal authority remains valid," Mara said.

"The cabinet can contest it after loss of command capacity."

"I have enough capacity to answer. Do not restore the bridges."

Pike's face tightened. "Record your basis."

"The distributed plan remains viable under its stated thresholds. The target geometry includes noncombatant life. Sable would convert a military objective into species destruction. I refuse execution and retain responsibility."

Static cut the channel before Pike replied.

Mara sent the refusal to all six regional authorities. She did not ask them to praise it. Four confirmed. One link failed. Kes answered with a single line: RESERVE REFUSAL STANDS. CONTINUE MISSION.

The casualty count climbed to forty-seven.

Around Anchor Three, the over-dense escort screen broke exactly as Mara had feared. Veyr particle fire crossed two human lanes and disabled a carrier. Yet the carrier's craft had already launched. They emerged from behind its tumbling hull, fired at local braids, and opened the corridor the screen had failed to protect. A mistake became cover because crews still possessed authority after making it.

At Anchor Eight, a regional cruiser spent its last acceleration to ram a concordance shield platform. The impact threw both hulls out of the cradle plane. Strike Eight crossed the gap on cold thrusters.

Anchor Two's group stopped transmitting. Twenty seconds later, its strike ship sent peer-phase readiness through the neighbor ring. Local command had survived even when the formation voice did not.

Tovan's synchronization lattice appeared on Mara's console as nine points. They moved toward agreement by tiny corrections. No point identified her as master clock. She could abort the whole strike through a physical priority code, but she could not improve its convergence.

"Veyr heavy groups turning toward strike corridors," Sori said. "Thren has identified the packages."

Sixteen braids left their anchor screens and accelerated toward the nine craft. Human escorts intercepted. Coil rounds crossed particle streams. Two escorts died before their lances reached firing range. A third broke a Veyr vessel-strand and spun away trailing radiator fluid.

Fifty-four human ships lost.

The flagship's nine minutes expired. Heat alarms rose across the auxiliary plot. The captain fired the surviving drive cluster without asking Mara. The stern held. Their vector shifted enough to open the radiator toward cold space, while the command vault rolled beyond the battle's former horizontal.

Mara caught the rail and kept watching the nine points.

Strike Four drifted outside phase by nine microseconds. Its neighbors adjusted. Strike Seven delayed. Strike One accepted a hotter capacitor curve rather than force a global correction. The ring tightened.

A final message came from Strike Six: CREW CONSENT CONFIRMED. HEAT-SPINE LOSS ACCEPTED.

"All regional groups request commitment status," Sori said.

Mara looked at the cradle-safe boundary, the Veyr defense compressing around it, and the Sable restoration kits sealed aboard each small craft. Command no longer meant moving every ship. It meant making the remaining choice only she had been given.

"Commit the nine strike ships," she said. "Concordance-blind pulse. Low-power architecture locked. No high-output compensation under any loss condition."

The order entered the peer lattice as permission, not timing.

White points converged.

Across Mazarine, nine craft discharged into nine separate anchors. The event carried no visible beam. Each package imposed a shaped magnetic interruption through local plasma, brief enough to spare the cradle and violent enough to burn its own hardware. On the tactical fragments, strike-ship heat signatures flared, then began rising without relief.

Telemetry from the craft arrived as a sequence of failures. Strike Two lost its capacitor bank before the pulse completed, but stored current carried the final fraction. Five reported a pressure breach in the open package compartment. Eight's heat spine separated along three bonded sections exactly as Tovan had forecast. Life-support controllers shut down cabins, weapons, and maneuvering pumps in order, preserving only crew compartments. The ships had crossed from weapons into rescue cases within less than a second.

No tug could enter yet. Their survival now occupied the same local command structure that carried the battle. Escorts received the distress coordinates but did not break from anchor lanes. Mara let those choices stand. Saving the strike crews before the pulse changed the geometry would have destroyed the condition that might make rescue possible at all.

Anchor One vanished from the prediction field. Three followed. Nine lost coherence after a stutter. Human formations that had spent hours fighting an enemy able to share future geometry suddenly faced local braids making separate decisions.

Veyr fire lost its impossible agreement.

Regional fleets exploited the break without waiting. Cruisers crossed former prediction lanes. Carriers launched through gaps measured by nearby escorts. Human losses continued because blinded anchors did not remove weapons or momentum. Three damaged ships failed under accumulated heat. Four more disappeared inside a needle exchange near the inner plane.

The casualty ledger reached 61 ships.

Eight anchors had gone dark.

One pulse remained in the field: Anchor Seven, live at the inner edge of the cradle. Beside it, the signature Mara knew from Ilyr's message held inside a damaged Veyr braid. Consequence of Shelter had neither opened the anchor nor withdrawn from it.

Thren's surviving groups began turning toward that last shared prediction source. Human formations, reduced and scattered, turned with them. The nine strike ships drifted behind the battle with dead heat spines and no safe propulsion.

Mara had no central array, no second pulse, and no order that could decide what Ilyr would do.

Anchor Seven brightened across every remaining screen.

Chapter 28 — Nine Reefs Break Formation

Eight absences struck Ilyr-of-Nine-Reefs at once.

Across Mazarine, concordance anchors ceased carrying shared prediction. Their disappearance did not resemble darkness. Each loss removed relationships: firing intervals no longer compared themselves, approach vectors lost common consequence, and two hundred braids became local persons inside a battle designed for collective timing.

Anchor Seven remained.

Its ceramic loops turned above the inner cradle plane, bright with charged current and the accumulated forecasts of the Continuance. Consequence of Shelter held beside it on two damaged vessel-strands. Ilyr's surviving seven lineage patterns maintained a person where nine had once negotiated. The missing strands did not leave silence. They left edges that every decision now crossed without consultation.

Human pulses had reached all nine anchors within the promised interval. Eight carried enough force to interrupt concordance. None entered the thermal depths where unbound choruses moved through Mazarine's magnetic arches. The human strike craft now drifted without thermal regulation, their interior heat climbing as their drives remained cold.

Ilyr had received Tovan's alternative only as observed behavior. Now the behavior supplied proof. The humans possessed a cradle-killing architecture and had chosen a narrower pulse under fire. Their weapon spent its force on coordination. Its cost remained aboard the small craft that fired it.

Mazarine's field reported the distinction through a thousand thermal relations. Young-bearing arches kept their long oscillation. Plasma knots moved along the same developmental gradients recorded before the battle. A high-power discharge would have flattened those differences into turbulent heat and erased the chorus boundaries first. Instead, local disturbance gathered around the anchors, sharp and temporary. The humans had shaped harm tightly enough that Ilyr could measure where it ended.

One surviving lineage pattern distrusted even that evidence. It supplied a branch in which the low-power pulse prepared a later strike by stripping away defenders. Another pattern answered with the human craft's rising internal temperatures. A species planning immediate escalation would not disable all nine delivery hulls before using its decisive channel. A third pattern held open the possibility of deception anyway. Negotiated personhood did not produce unanimity. It produced action whose objections remained attached.

Ilyr preserved each objection in the witness core. If exposure ended in the cradle's death, no later account could claim the risk had gone unseen. If restraint survived, no authorized history could reduce the choice to innocence. The decision required danger to retain meaning.

Thren-of-Last-Burning opened a command path through Anchor Seven.

PRESERVE CONCORDANCE. ALL BRAIDS ACCEPT FINAL GEOMETRY. HUMAN EXTERMINATION WINDOW FOLLOWS.

The last anchor drew data from every surviving Veyr group. Local uncertainty collapsed into one prediction reef. Human formations had lost sixty-one ships. Their central command source no longer transmitted useful geometry. Many cruisers carried empty magazines or damaged radiators. If Ilyr allowed Anchor Seven to stabilize, Thren could direct the remaining braids through the gaps and annihilate the exhausted fleet.

That outcome preserved Mazarine for the present.

It also restored the doctrine that had produced the human weapon. Continuance would record the low-power strike as failed extermination rather than restraint. Thren would remove Ilyr's transmitted uncertainty from every authorized witness line. Future human stellar engineering would again appear only as a cradle threat, and every regulated sunspindle would receive the same preventive answer as Orison, Caer Sola, and Ninth Meridian.

A probability reef unfolded through the anchor. In its broadest channel, Ilyr sealed access and accepted Thren's sequence. Human combatants died. The stranded strike crews followed. The Continuance carried proof of victory to all nine lineages and discarded proof of possible restraint.

Another channel began with exposure.

Ilyr could open Anchor Seven's prediction shell, transmit its coordinates without distortion, and withdraw Consequence of Shelter from the shielding geometry. Human fire might destroy the anchor. It might also destroy Ilyr, pass into the cradle, or exploit the break to complete the high-power attack they had refused seconds earlier. The three Reefs that had hesitated before battle remained uncommitted. None had promised to follow.

Trust did not remove those branches. It selected action while they remained.

Thren sent a private continuity demand. NINE-REEF PERSON, DECLARE CONSTITUENT CONSENT.

Under ordinary law, Ilyr would have answered with a full witness braid: every lineage pattern stating assent, objection, or bounded absence. Two constituent strands had burned away while carrying evidence through the pursuit at Ash Meridian. Thren used the missing voices as an argument that Ilyr no longer possessed enough continuity to act.

Ilyr examined the remaining negotiation. Four patterns favored preserving Anchor Seven long enough to demand reciprocal withdrawal. Three judged that delay would make Thren's annihilation irreversible. No intact consensus existed.

Yet Consequence of Shelter still possessed a witness core. Alteration did not convert a person into property of the unaltered.

CONSENT REMAINS CONTESTED, Ilyr sent. AUTHORITY TO EXTERMINATE DOES NOT FOLLOW FROM CONTEST.

Thren's reply carried heat from the ancient loss records. TWO CRADLES DIED WHILE STRATEGISTS MEASURED UNCERTAINTY. I WILL NOT OFFER A THIRD TO YOUR DEFECT.

Ilyr did not read sadism in the message. Thren's lineages carried Yssat sterilization scars through hundreds of negotiated generations. Every delayed defense touched those inherited witness patterns. Fear had acquired procedure, rank, and fleets. None of that changed what Thren intended to do with the humans after they spared Mazarine.

A human cruiser crossed Anchor Seven's outer approach and fired at two defending braids. Both Veyr groups separated before impact using the anchor's forecast. Their return streams cut through the cruiser. It continued on a failing drive, leaking atmosphere and molten radiator salt.

Human losses rose to sixty-four.

Ilyr opened the first shell.

Anchor Seven's concealed phase became visible to local space. Human sensors could now distinguish the true structure from six decoy harmonics. Thren reacted within one concordance interval. Eleven loyal braids turned their weapons toward Consequence of Shelter, while the anchor began sealing itself under remote authority.

Ilyr drove one surviving vessel-strand into the nearest control loop.

Ceramic struck ceramic without explosive force. The contact created a conductive path through the anchor's outer lattice. Ilyr fed witness-core timing into it, holding the shell open while charged current burned along the strand's surface. Thermal pain entered the seven-pattern negotiation as a narrowing of possible thought.

ALL REEFS, OBSERVE, Ilyr transmitted. HUMAN PULSE REMAINED BELOW CRADLE DISRUPTION. STRIKE CRAFT CANNOT REPEAT. THREN'S EXTERMINATION ORDER LACKS CONTINUANCE NECESSITY. ANCHOR SEVEN OPEN. CHOOSE LOCALLY.

The message carried raw cradle measurements, human discharge curves, and the sealed record of Thren's suppressed uncertainty. Ilyr attached no prediction of what the Reefs would do. Such a forecast would repeat the coercion under dispute.

Open Current answered first.

Its braids broke their assigned formation near the sunward arc. Instead of converging on a human carrier, they rotated sensor faces toward Mazarine and confirmed the young remained coherent. Patient Ember followed after three long intervals, drawing six braids away from Thren's attack channel. Divided Glass did not send assent. Its firing group simply ceased sharing solutions through Anchor Seven and placed itself across the path of two loyalist reinforcements.

Three Reefs had broken formation.

The break did not create peace. It created incompatible local duties inside weapon range.

Every dissent carried internal fracture. Open Current included lineage contributions that had opposed leaving Thren's solution; those patterns now recorded consent under protest while their vessel altered course. Patient Ember's six braids did not share one reason. Two accepted Ilyr's evidence, one prioritized recovery of witness cores, and three judged Thren's command more dangerous than human weapons only for the current interval. Divided Glass refused to call its act allegiance at all. It named the maneuver a custody intervention and reserved the right to fire on either fleet if cradle limits changed.

Such distinctions would have looked weak inside concordance. Shared prediction converted conditional duties into noise because every caveat widened the forecast. Local command restored the caveats at the worst possible time, when each vessel had seconds to decide whether another Veyr person counted as threat. Ilyr saw why Thren had feared this condition. Civil agreement had once prevented lineage disputes from becoming weapons fire. The same agreement had hardened until factual dissent could enter only as betrayal.

Human formations added another uncertainty. Their weapons already in flight carried decisions made before the Reefs moved. No signal could recall a coil slug. Particle channels retained charge after operators changed targets. Even perfect mutual recognition would arrive behind momentum. Ilyr transmitted new allegiance maps with confidence limits rather than fixed labels, asking human sensors to treat every Veyr group according to current behavior.

Thren classified the dissenting braids as cradle threats and ordered their removal. A loyal Veyr group fired on Divided Glass. Charged particles crossed one vessel-strand, severing its relationship to the witness core. The surviving strands returned fire against the attacker's propulsion geometry. Neither side fired through the inner cradle plane. Both claimed to preserve it.

Veyr civil engagement spread along the anchor approaches.

Open Current attempted to disarm a loyal braid by cutting its particle channel. The target interpreted restraint as an opportunity and launched ceramic needles into Open Current's witness core. Patient Ember split its formation, half screening the exposed anchor and half carrying cradle telemetry to groups whose concordance access Thren had restricted. Several unaffiliated braids withdrew beyond weapon range. Others joined Thren because civil fracture during invasion matched their deepest extinction forecast.

Ilyr felt the Continuance lose topology. Alliances that had endured for centuries separated in seconds. Shared witness archives refused updates from opposing custodians. Lineage contributions inside individual persons disagreed about whether their vessel should fire. One braid vented charge harmlessly rather than obey either side, then drifted between human and Veyr formations with no safe affiliation.

The humans did not understand the entire fracture. They understood altered fire.

Regional ships pressed toward Anchor Seven, but their trajectories curved away from the three dissenting groups once local sensors marked those groups as non-firing. Some human weapons still struck Veyr braids whose allegiance had changed after launch. Momentum did not revise itself when policy did.

A coil slug shattered an Open Current strand moments after it broke formation. Patient Ember disabled the human escort that fired it. Divided Glass then shielded that escort's escape pod from a loyalist particle stream. No clean boundary formed between enemies and possible witnesses.

Thren forced more power through Anchor Seven.

The prediction shell began to close around Ilyr's embedded strand. If it sealed, the final anchor would recover enough shared geometry to coordinate the loyal majority. Ilyr could withdraw and preserve Consequence of Shelter, but exposure would collapse behind them.

Seven internal patterns renegotiated under heat. Two favored withdrawal. One had lost coherent language. Four sustained the conductive path.

Ilyr remained.

A human transmission entered through the six-second signature first used at Hesper. Its content carried no demand for surrender. A damaged command relay announced only the standing mission boundary: NO FIRE THROUGH INNER CRADLE PLANE. LOCAL CEASE RESPONSE AUTHORIZED.

Mara Venn had no way to know whether Ilyr could obey. She had distributed permission anyway.

Thren saw the same message and answered across all Veyr bands. HUMAN RESTRAINT ENDS WHEN ADVANTAGE REQUIRES. RESTORE PREDICTION. DESTROY THEIR COMMANDERS, THEN THE STRIKE WRECKS.

Loyal braids converged on the stranded craft. Their approach passed below Anchor Seven, using the cradle boundary to prevent human heavy fire. Three human escorts entered the gap on trajectories that would strand them as surely as the craft they defended.

Human losses reached sixty-eight.

Ilyr sent the strike coordinates to every local group, human and Veyr. Open Current interposed two braids. One died under loyalist needles. The other redirected its charged stream into empty space, forcing an attacker to evade without adding thermal disturbance near the young.

Patient Ember called for reciprocal separation. No general answer came.

The battle continued because enough participants still believed the next shot would create safety.

Beyond the anti-sunward plane, a human formation that had not yet committed began to accelerate. Ilyr recognized its command pattern from Hesper records: Kes Renn, whose local maneuver had violated appearance while fulfilling purpose. The reserve numbered too few ships to defeat Thren's loyal groups directly. Its vector also seemed wrong for Anchor Seven. Kes aimed above the inner plane, away from the exposed structure and the battle clustered around it.

Thren interpreted the reserve as retreat and reassigned six braids toward the human strike wrecks.

Ilyr traced Kes's geometry. The human ships would pass high over the cradle, fire along a plane parallel to its magnetic arches, then use Mazarine's stellar wind to bend their projectiles downward only after they cleared the protected volume. The maneuver demanded delayed guidance and local timing from three separated cruisers. Concordance would have predicted it. One open anchor now spent most of its capacity trying to seal Ilyr out.

KES APPROACH CREATES CRADLE CROSSING, Thren warned.

The claim distorted the geometry. Thren knew it. Loyal firing groups reacted to the declared threat rather than calculate independently, because the last anchor still carried command rank with its predictions.

Ilyr pushed the human path into the Continuance band. NO CROSSING. VERIFY LOCALLY.

Several loyal braids did. Two left Thren's firing solution after confirming the reserve plane. A third transmitted objection but remained. The shared forecast frayed under evidence it could no longer hide.

Thren detached from Anchor Seven and turned toward the high human approach. Last Burning's Edict, the war leader's braid, carried damage from earlier fighting but retained all three vessel-strands around its witness core. Its particle channels charged toward Kes's lead cruiser.

Kes divided the reserve.

One cruiser burned prograde above the cradle. Another cut anti-sunward. The third held a cold vector until Thren committed, then fired three coil salvos into empty coordinates.
